Curvature, support certificates, and barrier complexity of conic lifts



                                               Abstract
       Conic lifts change factor counts, support-certificate ranks, affine barrier parameters, and
   computational interfaces in different ways. We establish quantitative relations among these
   resources for balls and related convex bodies. Mixed contact curvature gives a dimension-minus-
   two bound for proper definable cones and a complementary-rank bound for symmetric cones. For
   a finite repeatable dictionary of simple symmetric cones, put B = max a(r − 1) > 0, where a is
   the Peirce constant and r the Jordan rank. The exact least worst-support certificate rank of an s-
   dimensional ball, s ≥ 2, is ⌈(s−1)/B⌉, with minimization over each entire genuine certificate fiber
   and no global selection assumption. Global differentiable selections impose additional topological
   costs. Boundary and recession tests yield delimited exact restricted-barrier frontiers and concrete
   intrinsic parameter formulas. For a fixed unit objective in a fixed weighted norm-tree metric,
   call a node active if its subtree carries nonzero objective support. The squared leading distance
   coefficient is the sum of active node weights plus half the inactive node weights; this determines
   the central route’s asymptotic excess. For feasible PSD column packing, exact source allocations
   give a sharp first-power comparison between off-center and centered projected Hessian quotients.
   We also give resource ledgers, conditioned approximation and exact compilation results, and
   access-dependent Newton and query comparisons. The remaining bounded narrow-cap and
   nonsymmetric parameter intervals are stated explicitly. Movement bounds imply work bounds
   only under the specified charging and output contracts.

                                                6
1     Introduction
An exact conic reformulation represents a convex body as the projection of an affine slice of a
product of cones. It can change the number of factors, their dimensions, the rank of a supporting
certificate, and the barrier on the affine domain. It can also change which data a Newton step needs
and which outputs are inexpensive to produce. These resources are related, but none determines all
the others. The purpose of this paper is to give precise comparisons, sharp formulas in specified
models, and explicit examples that separate the models.
    Euclidean balls and their products provide the main setting. Their slack has a simple differential
structure, yet their lifts already exhibit certificate sharing, singular boundary choices, nontrivial
projection fibers, and different barrier metrics. We also treat norm balls, matrix balls, balance
slices, power and entropy cones, and exact constraint compilers. Throughout, an exact formula is
accompanied by the class over which it is optimized. A lower bound for a chosen representation is
not automatically a lower bound for every representation of its projection.

1.1   Principal results and their scope
The basic geometric calculation differentiates a nonnegative cone pairing at a primal–polar contact.
A proper cone of real dimension m supplies at most max{m − 2, 0} independent mixed-curvature
directions. In a simple Euclidean Jordan algebra, complementary primal and dual ranks p, q sharpen
this count to apq, where a is the Peirce constant (Theorem 3.2). Definable local selections suffice; no
globally smooth selection is needed. For a ball of dimension s ≥ 2 and a cone-dimension cap d ≥ 3,
the exact minimum factor count and total cone dimension over exact definable lifts are consequently
                                    s−1
                                            
                                 k=     ,              M = s − 1 + 2k.
                                    d−2
Classical norm trees attain both bounds (Corollary 3.4).
   Our central rank invariant minimizes over the entire fiber of genuine certificates for a fixed
support. A genuine certificate represents the support slack on the whole affine slice, rather than
only on selected boundary points. Fix a finite repeatable dictionary K of simple symmetric cones
and put B = maxK∈K a(K)(r(K) − 1) > 0. For every s ≥ 2, Theorem 4.1 proves
                                                               s−1
                                                                        
                              inf max min           rankJ Yi =                                     (1.1)
                                                 X
                                                                   .
                               L v∈Ss−1 Y ∈DL (v)
                                                  i
                                                                B

The lift is fixed before the support is chosen. For each fixed lift the lower bound holds for every
certificate on a dense open set of supports with surface-null complement. A Peirce-perspective
construction attains the bound in every simple family, including the Albert cone. It also shows
why choosing the formulation after the objective changes the answer. Sequential compression and
private curvature describe which rank costs add for product sources (Section 5).
    Global selection is a different requirement. If primal boundary points and dual certificates must
be chosen C 1 over the entire support manifold, saturation of a local rank bound forces a global map
into a product of support orbits or cone-base boundaries. Covering and cohomology arguments then
impose additional resource costs. We prove exact Lorentz frontiers and selected symmetric-cone
rank frontiers, together with product saturation and whole-fiber rigidity theorems (Sections 6–12).
The whole-fiber results derive regular structure from a stronger fiber hypothesis; they do not assume
that arbitrary lifts have smooth selectors.
    The barrier analysis distinguishes three optimization problems: the standard product barrier in
the ambient cone, that same barrier after affine restriction, and the best barrier of any form on a fixed

                                                   7
affine domain. Boundary nullity and recession give lower tests for the restricted standard parameter.
These tests yield exact minima under global selection, several selection-free exact cases, and intrinsic
optima for grouped, packed, and balance slices. Norm trees have a separate root-incidence law. The
results do not constitute a complete classification. For the remaining bounded narrow-cap cases
in (8.6), with s = qB + 1 and q ≥ B + 2, the proved interval is q ≤ inf L νstd,slice ≤ q + 1. The
nonsymmetric estimates in Section 18 likewise include parameter intervals, not general formulas for
their optima.
    Movement is always measured in a specified Hessian metric. Exposing certificates yield path-
independent target-distance bounds, and the rank frontier makes these bounds depend on the
permitted lift dictionary. Primal–dual movement for arbitrary logarithmically homogeneous barriers
has a different endpoint and metric contract from primal movement in a displayed barrier. An
explicit refinement determines the shortest-distance coefficient for every fixed weighted norm tree
and fixed unit leaf objective c. If ωv ≥ 1 is the barrier weight of internal node v, call v active when
its subtree contains nonzero objective support. For the target 1 − cT w ≤ ϵ, Theorem 26.1 gives,
from any fixed interior reference,

            d(ϵ) =       Kc log(1/ϵ) + O(log log(1/ϵ)),        Kc =              ωv +                       (1.2)
                     p                                                  X

                                                                                        2 v inactive

The corresponding central route has coefficient         v ωv . Thus inactive subtrees contribute to
                                                        pP

unavoidable distance even though their exposing dual blocks vanish, and they also produce a
precisely quantified excess in central-path length. The tree, weights, and objective are fixed before ϵ
tends to zero.
    For packed PSD representations, exact auxiliary elimination identifies a common marginal
Newton system at the fiber center. Away from that center, the source allocation equations improve
a generic squared comparison to a sharp first-power transfer. At the same projected point, if the
feasible residuals and their exact diagonal center obey µD0 ⪯ D ⪯ LD0 , Theorem 29.3 proves
                                                             −1/2        −1/2
                         L−1 H0 ⪯ HD ⪯ µ−1 H0 ,         κ(H0        HD H0        ) ≤ L/µ.                   (1.3)

A two-source feasible example attains the condition factor. Exact allocations are essential: approxi-
mate radial metadata needs its own allocation error factor. The matrix comparison alone neither
identifies the reduced Newton right-hand side nor constructs a cheap preconditioner.
    Finally, we specify when mathematical resources become computational costs. Exact resource
ledgers distinguish ambient coordinates, free coordinates, and restricted parameters. Sparse elimina-
tion gives classical comparisons under matched access and full-output contracts. Query examples
separate normalized projected states from readable values, full vectors, exact reusable compilers, and
fresh scale maintenance. Some of these states need one query while classical output requires linearly
many queries. A movement bound composes with work only when the same exposed rank enters
both estimates and a fresh per-round charge is an explicit premise (Theorem 28.1). A one-shot
query bound cannot simply be multiplied by a number of geometric checkpoints.

1.2   Relation to the literature
Conic lifts and slack factorizations are the starting framework of Gouveia, Parrilo, and Thomas [43,
Theorem 2.4]; the survey Fawzi et al. [37] places their use among geometric and algebraic methods
for concise convex descriptions. Fixed-size PSD factor counts [33], PSD rank and algebraic-boundary
bounds [34, 36], and semidefinite extension degree [8] measure related but different resources.
Extension degree minimizes the largest permitted PSD block while allowing finite products. Fawzi’s

                                                     9
SOC obstruction and Saunderson’s short-face-chain theorem use neighborliness to exclude finite
product lifts in their respective settings [32, 80]. Our local curvature bounds quantify resources
when a smooth body is representable; our certificate invariant also minimizes within each fixed
support fiber. Kummer’s ball bounds concern direct spectrahedral descriptions [58], which must be
distinguished from projections of products of cones.
    Positive curvature itself does not obstruct SOC representability. Scheiderer [81, Theorem 1.2]
proves that compact convex semialgebraic sets with Nash-smooth boundary and everywhere strictly
positive curvature have SOC lifts. That existence theorem allows an arbitrary finite number of
order-two PSD blocks. It does not specify the factor-count frontier, support-fiber minimax, or global-
selection frontiers studied here. This distinction is particularly relevant because both approaches
apply to positively curved boundaries.
    The Jordan classification, Peirce spaces, quadratic representations, and support manifolds
are classical [31, 45, 73]. Symmetric-cone factorization algorithms provide additional context [82].
Recursive small-Lorentz-cone norm representations are also classical [13, Section 2]. We use standard
covering, bundle, and cohomology tools [48, 83]; their application here depends on the precise global
contact hypotheses. The claimed geometric contributions are the stated quantitative inequalities,
fiber quantifiers, rigidity consequences, and attaining constructions, not these underlying tools.
    Self-concordant barrier theory supplies the analytic framework [70]. Ambient optimality on
symmetric cones is established in the work of Güler–Tunçel and Cardoso–Vieira [20, 47]; self-
scaled barriers have a classified form [49]. Conic extension, affine restriction, and projective
transformation of barriers belong to an existing theory [55]. Accordingly, the distinction between
ambient and restricted parameters is not itself a claim of novelty. Our contribution is to compute
the particular restricted or intrinsic quantities proved here and connect them to exact lift resources.
For nonsymmetric cones we use established lower-bound and barrier constructions [22, 35, 53]. The
universal barrier’s parameter bound [60] and practical natural-cone methods [24] also make clear
why existence of a small parameter should be separated from derivative evaluation cost.
    Nesterov–Todd and Nesterov–Nemirovski develop the barrier geometry and the comparison
of central paths with Riemannian distances [69, 72]. The unpublished companion The Cost of
Following the Central Path [21] already contains the weighted exposed-minor estimate, several
distribution-sensitive movement bounds, and standard-barrier calculations for grouped, packed,
and norm-tree formulations. We reproduce the proofs needed here, so no theorem depends on
access to that companion. The formulation-specific developments here include composition with
the certificate-rank frontier, the general affine-PSD exposure profile, and the exact active/inactive
weighted-tree coefficient (1.2). We do not claim the general distinction between central-path length
and shortest distance as a new observation.
    The computational arguments likewise use established ingredients: search, oracle interrogation,
approximate counting, and information bounds [14, 18, 66, 67, 84]; sparse cone augmentation [27];
and elimination from a supplied tree decomposition [39]. Our statements attach these tools to
specified conic representations and outputs. Quantum interior-point methods and their end-to-end
resource analyses [7, 26] are relevant algorithmic comparators. More recent iterative refinement
improves the high-precision behavior of quantum interior-point methods [62]; we make no blanket
claim that all such methods retain earlier inverse-accuracy dependencies. Our exact-field full-output
comparisons and the feasible quotient theorem (1.3) have their own explicit access, feasibility, and
output assumptions. In particular, the first-power improvement uses elementary positive trace
inequalities and the allocation equalities; it is not a new general matrix-order principle.
    The specific results we put forward as new are the selection-free entire-fiber minimax (1.1),
the active/inactive weighted-tree coefficient (1.2), and the sharp feasible allocation-aware quotient
transfer (1.3), in the precise models stated above. These claims concern the formulas and their

                                                  10
quantifiers, not priority for the general methods used to prove them. The comparisons above identify
the scope of the checked primary results; absence from a literature search is not evidence of priority.

1.3       Conventions and reading guide
Table 2 collects the recurring notation. Symbols such as p, q, L have local uses in some construction
and counting sections; those uses are defined where introduced. A theorem’s stated domain, metric,
and order of optimization always take precedence over an informal resource name.

                               Qm has total dimension m.

                               and dual contact ranks. rankJ denotes Jordan rank; real PSD Jordan rank
                               is ordinary matrix rank.

   Part I develops local curvature and certificate fibers. Part II treats global selection, restricted
barriers, and saturation. Part III gives formulation families and nonsymmetric comparisons.
Part IV addresses controlled approximation and exact compilation. Part V connects certificates
to metric distance, culminating in the weighted tree theorem. Part VI gives resource ledgers and
computational contracts, including the sharp PSD quotient comparison. The appendices retain
topological refinements, restricted-kernel distinctions, robust approximation, and conditioning
counterexamples. Each part supplies the proofs needed for its formulation claims; the contents and
model table allow readers to follow a particular resource without treating every result as a theorem
about the same class of lifts.




                                                  11
Part I
Local curvature and certificate fibers
2     Exact lifts and local conventions
2.1   Exact lifts and genuine certificates
A proper cone is a closed, convex, pointed cone with nonempty interior in its finite-dimensional real
ambient space. Its dual is K ∗ = {y : ⟨x, y⟩ ≥ 0 for all x ∈ K}. Let C ⊂ Rs be a compact convex body
with nonempty interior and 0 ∈ int C. Its polar body is C ◦ = {v ∈ Rs : ⟨x, v⟩ ≤ 1 for every x ∈ C}.
An exact lift is a triple
                                  L = (K, A, π),       C = π(K ∩ A),
where K = ki=1 Ki , A is an affine subspace, and π : A → Rs is affine. No compactness of K ∩ A is
            Q

assumed. A lift is proper if A ∩ int K =
                                       ̸ ∅. When we speak of relative Slater feasibility, we use the
following minimal-face reduction.
Lemma 2.1 (Reduction and certificate normalization). Unrestricted free variables in a finite affine
lift of a compact body can be eliminated without changing its cone factors. The cone can then be
replaced by the smallest face containing its feasible points, in the span of that face, so that the
resulting lift is proper. For a proper lift and every v ∈ ∂C ◦ , the set

                   DL (v) = {Y ∈ K ∗ : ⟨X, Y ⟩ = 1 − ⟨π(X), v⟩ for every X ∈ A}                     (2.1)

is nonempty. Thus certificates satisfy the support-slack identity on the entire affine slice, including
its infeasible points.
Proof. Write the original affine equalities in cone coordinates X and free coordinates z. A free
direction that preserves these equalities while fixing X must have zero output direction: otherwise
its entire line projects into the compact body. Consequently the output depends affinely only on
X, on the affine subspace of X for which the equalities admit some z. Linear elimination gives the
claimed representation.
    Choose X0 ∈ ri(K ∩ A), and let F be the minimal face of K containing X0 . For every X ∈ K ∩ A,
the relative-interior property gives X0 − ϵ(X − X0 ) ∈ K ∩ A for some ϵ > 0. The face property
therefore gives X ∈ F . Conversely F ∩ A ⊂ K ∩ A. The point X0 belongs to ri F , so replacing K by
F and A by A ∩ span F gives Slater feasibility in span F . Faces of a product are products of faces.
    For the last assertion write A = {X : M X = b} and extend the affine output as π(X) = T X + t.
Maximizing ⟨v, T X + t⟩ over K ∩ A has value one. Slater conic duality gives a multiplier λ with
Y = M ∗ λ − T ∗ v ∈ K ∗ and ⟨b, λ⟩ = 1 − ⟨t, v⟩. Hence for every X ∈ A, ⟨X, Y ⟩ = ⟨b, λ⟩ − ⟨T X, v⟩ =
1 − ⟨π(X), v⟩. This is the duality step in the proper-lift direction of [43, Theorem 2.4].

    For symmetric cones, every face is the cone of squares in a Peirce-one subalgebra. Jordan ranks
in that face agree with ambient ranks of its members. Throughout, ranks, determinants, and barriers
of a relative-Slater lift are evaluated after this reduction. This convention avoids requiring a positive
ambient certificate when the original presentation fails Slater’s condition. Faces need not themselves
belong to the chosen dictionary: they are reductions of its factors, and their capacities never exceed
those of the original factors.
    For C = Bs2 = {x : ∥x∥2 ≤ 1} its polar is itself, and a support is v ∈ Ss−1 . Define

                       rL (v) =     min           rankJ Yi ,   R(L) = max rL (v).                   (2.2)
                                              X
                                  Y ∈DL (v)                           v∈Ss−1
                                              i


                                                        12
Both extrema exist as extrema of nonempty subsets of a finite set of integers; compactness of the
certificate fiber is unnecessary. The zero certificate is impossible because the support slack is not
identically zero on C. This observation will also explain the separate interval case s = 1.

2.2   Regularity obtained from the lift
We call data definable if they are definable, allowing real parameters, in one fixed o-minimal
expansion of the ordered real field. Only the universal-cone statement below needs this generality.
All symmetric-cone data and selections used for the rank theorem are semialgebraic. Definable
choice and finite differentiable stratification are standard [85]; in the semialgebraic setting see [16].

Lemma 2.2 (Generic regular selections). For a proper definable lift, primal boundary lifts and
normalized support certificates can be chosen definably. On a definable C 2 boundary patch, a
dense relatively open subset makes their compositions with the definable normalized outward-normal
contact map C 1 , in definable local coordinates. For a symmetric-cone lift of a Euclidean ball, the
certificates can additionally be chosen to attain rL (v). The common regular subset can then be
chosen semialgebraic, with a lower-dimensional complement in Ss−1 .

Proof. The primal fiber and the fiber (2.1) are nonempty closed definable sets; the identity on
A amounts to finitely many affine equalities. Their minimum-Euclidean-norm points are unique,
since both fibers are convex, and their graphs are definable by quantified formulas. For minimum
Jordan rank, each condition i rankJ Yi = j is semialgebraic by spectral polynomial equations and
                              P

inequalities. Partition the support sphere according to the least realized j and apply semialgebraic
choice in each resulting nonempty fiber. This choice need not minimize norm and need not be
globally continuous.
    Finite C 1 cell decomposition, refined for all the maps at once, makes them C 1 on each full-
dimensional cell. The union of the interiors of those cells is relatively open and dense; its complement
has smaller dimension. In the semialgebraic case this complement has surface measure zero. A
definable C 2 boundary patch and its normalized normal map are eligible for the same decomposition.
Faces used in reduction are definable: for X0 ∈ ri F , F = {X ∈ K : ∃ϵ > 0, X0 − ϵX ∈ K}.

                                                   13
Lemma 3.1 (Universal local bound). Let K ⊂ E be a proper cone, m = dim E, and let X : U → K,
Y : U → K ∗ be C 1 with ⟨X(u), Y (u)⟩ = 0. The bilinear form −⟨DX(0)[h], DY (0)[ℓ]⟩ has rank at
most m − 2 if X(0), Y (0) ̸= 0, and rank zero if either is zero. In particular rays contribute no mixed
curvature.
Proof. Put a = X(0) and b = Y (0). When both are nonzero, nonnegativity of ⟨X(th), b⟩ for positive
and negative t shows DX(0)[h] ∈ b⊥ . Similarly DY (0)[ℓ] ∈ a⊥ . The pairing from b⊥ to (a⊥ )∗ has
kernel b⊥ ∩ span{a} = span{a}, since ⟨a, b⟩ = 0. Its rank is (m − 1) − 1 = m − 2. Pullback by the
derivatives cannot increase rank. If a = 0, a two-sided derivative of X lies in K ∩ (−K) = {0}; the
case b = 0 is identical.

Euclidean Jordan algebra V , write x ◦ y for its product, e for its identity, and ⟨x, y⟩ = tr(x ◦ y) for

c1 , . . . , cr gives the orthogonal Peirce decomposition V = i≤j Vij , where Vii = Rci and dim Vij = a

for i < j in a simple algebra. The support idempotent of x ⪰ 0 is the sum of the frame idempotents

Lemma 3.3 (Mixed Peirce channels). Let X, Y : U → K be C 1 and complementary: ⟨X(u), Y (u)⟩ =
0. If X(0) and Y (0) have ranks p and q, then p + q ≤ r, and −⟨DX(0)[h], DY (0)[ℓ]⟩ is a positive
semidefinite symmetric bilinear form of rank at most apq. No local constancy of ranks or strict
complementarity is required.
Proof. Positive orthogonal elements are Jordan complementary and diagonalize in a common Jordan
frame [31, Chapters III–IV]. Thus

Proof of Theorem 3.2. Reduce the lift by Lemma 2.1. The strictly positive curvature condition
persists on an open subpatch. On that subpatch choose a contact in the common regular locus
supplied by Lemma 2.2. Differentiating (3.1) once in each independent variable and using rank
subadditivity with Lemma 3.1 gives (3.3). A reduced face has dimension no larger than its original
factor, so the original dimensions give valid bounds. For symmetric cones apply Lemma 3.3 instead.
The inequality pq ≤ ⌊r2 /4⌋ follows from p + q ≤ r. Faces of a simple symmetric cone have the
same Peirce constant at smaller rank, except that rank-one faces are rays. For the Albert cone its
rank-two faces are spin factors with Peirce constant eight. Their capacities likewise cannot exceed
the original factor’s capacity.
     The number apq is the exact local channel dimension. Indeed the inner derivations 4[Lz , Lci ], z ∈
Vij , independently rotate the i, j support directions. Their exponentials are Jordan automorphisms.
Applied simultaneously to X(0), Y (0), their derivatives in Vij are λi z, −µj z. Thus all apq channels
in (3.5) can be realized. This local fact asserts no global integrability of a specified collection of
channels into an exact lift.
     Write Qm = {(t, z) ∈ R × Rm−1 : t ≥ ∥z∥2 } for the Lorentz cone of total cone-space dimension
m.
Corollary 3.4 (Exact dimension-capped ball resources). Fix s ≥ 2 and an integer d ≥ 3. Among
exact lifts of Bs2 by arbitrary definable proper cone factors of dimension at most d, the minimum
number of factors and the minimum total cone-space dimension are
                                         s−1
                                           
                            kmin =           ,     Mmin = s − 1 + 2kmin .
                                         d−2
Both minima are attained by the same Lorentz norm tree. Among symmetric cone lifts under this
dimension cap, the least total ambient Jordan rank is 2kmin , again attained by that tree.
Proof. Let k+ count factors of dimension at least two and k1 count rays. Theorem 3.2 implies
s − 1 ≤ k+ (d − 2) and M ≥ s − 1 + 2k+ + k1 , proving the first two lower bounds. For symmetric
factors only non-ray irreducible factors can contribute curvature, and each has rank at least two.
Decomposing reducible factors cannot increase their dimensions or change total rank, so total rank
is at least 2kmin .
    For attainment we use a variable-arity form of the recursive norm representation in [13, Section 2,
equation (5)]. Put k = kmin and write s − 1 = b1 + · · · + bk , with 1 ≤ bi ≤ d − 2. Form a rooted tree
whose internal nodes are a chain. Node i < k has bi leaf children and the next internal node as one
additional child; the last node has bk + 1 leaf children. It has s leaves. Assign the coordinates of
x ∈ Rs to those leaves and an auxiliary scalar ti to each internal node. Impose
                        ti ≥ ∥child values of node i∥2   in Qbi +2 ,    t1 = 1.
Recursive elimination gives exactly ∥x∥2 ≤ 1. Strict feasibility holds at x = 0 by choosing the
nonroot scalars positive and successively smaller. The construction uses k rank-two factors and
total dimension i (bi + 2) = s − 1 + 2k, as required.
                P

    The total ambient Jordan rank is the parameter of the standard product log-determinant before
affine restriction and remains a valid parameter after restriction. It need not be the least parameter
of the restricted barrier, and it does not give an intrinsic barrier lower bound for the projected
ball. Keeping these distinctions explicit is essential when using curvature estimates to compare
formulations.

Fix a finite dictionary K of simple symmetric cones, allowing arbitrary finite repetition of its
members and optional nonnegative rays. The dictionary, rather than the list of factors in an
individual lift, is held fixed. For a non-ray dictionary member let r and a be its rank and Peirce

The capacity B differs from the maximum local curvature capacity a⌊r2 /4⌋. It bounds curvature
per unit dual rank. The distinction is visible for real PSD matrices of order four: the two capacities
are three and four, respectively.

The middle inequality uses pi ≤ ri − 1 whenever qi > 0; when qi = 0 both terms vanish. Rays

the selected support, and at every support in the regular set. The same bound gives both lower

4.1   A uniform Peirce-perspective construction
For x ∈ V put Lx y = x ◦ y and P (x) = 2L2x − Lx2 , the quadratic representation. For an idempotent
c, P (c) is orthogonal projection onto V (c, 1). We use the standard facts that P (x)K ⊂ K and
that P (x) is a cone automorphism when x is invertible. These hold in every Euclidean Jordan
algebra; see [31, Chapters II–IV] and the Peirce and quadratic representation discussion in [45,
pp. 1554–1555]. Their useful rank consequence can be verified directly: approximate any x by


                                                      16
invertible elements xn . Cone automorphisms preserve extreme rays, so P (xn )c has rank one when c
is primitive. The closedness of the set of nonnegative elements of rank at most one gives

                                  P (x)c ⪰ 0,        rankJ (P (x)c) ≤ 1.                         (4.5)

This argument is independent of associative matrix multiplication.

Lemma 4.2 (Primitive Peirce perspective). Let V be simple of rank r ≥ 2, let c be primitive, and
set d = e − c. The half-Peirce space W = V (c, 1/2) has dimension a(r − 1); give it the norm induced
by the trace inner product, ∥w∥2 = ⟨w, w⟩. For w ∈ W ,
                                      √
                    X(δ, w, z) = δc + 2w + zd ⪰ 0 ⇐⇒ δ, z ≥ 0, δz ≥ ∥w∥2 ,                      (4.6)
                det X(δ, w, z) = z r−2 (δz − ∥w∥2 ).                                             (4.7)

For g ∈ W and A > 0 the element

Proof. Peirce multiplication gives w2 = tc + u, where u ∈ V (d, 1) and

                                                                 ∥w∥2
                                   t = ⟨w2 , c⟩ = ⟨w, w ◦ c⟩ =        .

The definition of P and c ◦ w = w/2 give P (w)c = u. Its trace is tr u = tr w2 − t = t. By (4.5),
for w ̸= 0 we have u = tk for a primitive idempotent k ∈ V (d, 1). Thus w2 = t(c + k). Spectral
calculus shows that w lies in the Peirce-one subalgebra with identity c + k: the supports of w and
w2 coincide. Since c ◦ w = w/2, we have k ◦ w = w/2. The span of c, k, w is therefore a √  rank-two
Jordan subalgebra, orthogonal to d − k, and in this subalgebra the two eigenvalues of δc + 2w + zk
have sum δ + z and product δz − 2t = δz − ∥w∥2 . The remaining r − 2 eigenvalues are all z. This
proves both displayed claims for w ̸= 0. For w = 0 they follow from the diagonal element δc + zd.
    Expanding the quadratic representation by its definition proves (4.8). The first expression and
(4.5) show positivity and rank at most one; its c-coefficient A > 0 makes the rank exactly one. The
calculation above with w = g gives tr P (g)c = ∥g∥2 /2, proving the trace formula.

Attainment in Theorem 4.1. Choose a dictionary member attaining B, and use its notation from

sum of the AG . Thus every block has rank one and the total rank is q. Conversely, γ > 0 forces

gives total rank one, which is minimal. If WG ̸= W , arbitrary certificates may have extra half-Peirce
components orthogonal to WG ; the favorable construction sets them to zero, while the positive tail
trace still forces each block to be nonzero. No uniqueness of certificates has been asserted. The
exceptional pole has surface measure zero for s ≥ 2, proving (4.3) and finishing the theorem.

4.2    Examples and the order of quantifiers
The dictionary capacity is r − 1 for real PSD order r, 2(r − 1) for complex Hermitian order r,
4(r − 1) for quaternionic Hermitian order r, and m − 2 for a Lorentz cone Qm . For the Albert cone
H+3 (O) it is 8(3 − 1) = 16. Consequently unrestricted finite repetition of Albert factors gives the

exact value ⌈(s − 1)/16⌉. The proof used neither an associative representation of the Albert algebra
nor an embedding into a real PSD cone.
    The requirement that a lift be fixed before selecting the objective is substantive. In fact,
                             inf sup rL (v) = qB (s),      sup inf rL (v) = 1.                      (4.11)
                              L   v                         v     L

For the second identity rotate the construction so that the prescribed v is its north pole; the first is
Theorem 4.1. The universal lower bound one follows from the nonzero support slack. Likewise the
theorem does not optimize the rank of a certificate selected in advance by an algorithm: it optimizes
over the full fiber at each support. For s = 1, whenever at least one non-ray factor is available,
choose orthogonal primitive idempotents c, k and the section X = tc + (1 − t)k, 0 ≤ t ≤ 1, with
output 2t − 1. Its two support slacks have the rank-one certificates 2k and 2c, giving frontier one;
the expression ⌈(s − 1)/B⌉ = 0 is inapplicable.


5     Products: certificates, compression, and private curvature
Write C = ha=1 Bs2a , with sa ≥ 2, and M = a Ssa −1 . Its pure support rows are 1 − ⟨xa , v⟩,
             Q                                      Q

v ∈ Ssa −1 . A pure-row certificate represents this affine function on the entire original lift slice. In

                                                    18
particular, it has zero coefficients on the other projected coordinates. This condition is stronger
than an identity on a selected boundary sheet.

5.1    The support-join identity
Idempotents are ordered by their associated faces. Their join c ∨ d is the support of c + d. These
operations and Jordan rank are taken separately in each simple factor.
Lemma 5.1 (Compression measures new support). For an idempotent c, put f = e − c. For y ⪰ 0,
let t = supp P (f )y. Then
                  c ∨ supp y = c + t,         rankJ P (f )y = rankJ (c ∨ supp y) − rankJ c.
For positive weights, supp(     a λa ya ) =       a supp ya .
                             P                W

Proof. We have t ≤ f . Set g = f − t. Self-adjointness of P (f ) gives ⟨g, y⟩ = ⟨g, P (f )y⟩ = 0. Positive
orthogonality implies supp y ⊥ g, hence c ∨ supp y ≤ e − g = c + t. Conversely let k = e − (c ∨ supp y).
Then k ≤ f and ⟨k, P (f )y⟩ = ⟨k, y⟩ = 0, so k ⊥ t. Consequently both c and t belong to V (e − k, 1),
proving the reverse inclusion. Ranks add on orthogonal idempotents. Finally a positive element is
orthogonal to a positive sum precisely when it is orthogonal to every summand; their complementary
faces therefore agree. This proves the sum identity.

                                                      21
Part II
Global regularity and affine barriers
6     Global regularity changes the formulation frontier
The generic selectors used above provide differentiability on a dense open set. Requiring one C 1
selector on the entire sphere is a different condition. We first prove its exact cost for Lorentz factors
and full product rows, including factors that vanish on open sets.

6.1    Lorentz phases and pointwise no-sharing
Use the Euclidean self-dual pairing on Qm = {(t, z) : t ≥ ∥z∥}. A fixed rescaling converts this
convention to the Jordan trace pairing without changing any rank or resource count. Suppose (5.2)
has globally C 1 factors in Qmi , mi ≥ 3, and put ri = mi − 2. At contact define
                               Hia (x)(u, v) = −⟨Da Xi (x)[u], DYia (xa )[v]⟩.
Then i Hia is the sphere metric of rank pa = sa − 1. If either factor is zero, its derivative is zero
      P

by pointedness. If one is interior, its complementary factor is zero. Otherwise, on a neighborhood
where both factors remain nonzero, complementarity gives
                Xi = αi (1, qia (xa )),    Yia = βia (1, −qia ),        qia ∈ Sri ,    αi , βia > 0.
The cylinder identity makes the primal phase depend only on xa there. Differentiation cancels every
amplitude term and gives
                             Hia = αi βia (Dqia )∗ Dqia ⪰ 0,         rank Hia ≤ ri .                   (6.1)
If Hia ̸= 0, the same primal phase is locally constant in every other source direction. Thus
                                Hia (x) ̸= 0   =⇒      Hib (x) = 0      (b ̸= a).                      (6.2)
This statement is pointwise. It does not assign one source to a label for all x.

6.2    A top-class cover argument
We record the topology needed in the proof. Cohomology in this subsection has real coefficients.
If open sets Ua cover a space and ua |Ua = 0, the relative cup product implies a ua = 0: lift each
                                                                                Q

class to H ∗ (M, Ua ) and multiply to get a class in H ∗ (M, a Ua ) = 0. The same argument applies
                                                            S

to any subfamily that covers. The Künneth formula gives a nonzero product of any subset of
the pulled-back top classes on a product of spheres. A proper open subset of a connected closed
oriented p-manifold has zero ordinary cohomology in degree p when every component is noncompact;
in particular this holds for every proper open subset of Sp . These are the standard relative cup,
Künneth, and noncompact-manifold duality facts; see [48, Sections 3.2–3.3].
Lemma 6.1 (Saturated labels have a proper phase domain). Fix a source a with pa ≥ 2, and a
compact set E on which its active labels are exactly J, with i∈J ri = pa and ri < pa for every
                                                              P

i ∈ J. There is an open neighborhood P of πa (E) in Spa such that the dual phases give a local C 1
diffeomorphism
                                    (qia )i∈J : P −→
                                                     Y
                                                        Sri .
                                                               i∈J

The set P is proper, so the source top class vanishes on πa−1 (P ).

                                                     22
Proof. At a point of E, every active primal and dual factor is nonzero. Keep all other primal
coordinates fixed and vary xa nearby. Continuity keeps the primal factor nonzero and forces the
dual factor to stay on the nonzero boundary. Hence its normalized phase is defined on an open
neighborhood of xa . Equality between the total dimension pa and the capacity sum, together
with (6.1), makes the joint phase derivative invertible. Invertibility is open. The union of these
phase domains, restricted to their common invertibility locus, provides P .
    If P were the whole sphere, compactness would make this local diffeomorphism a finite covering
of the connected target. If any ri = 1, the target has infinite fundamental group, whereas Spa is
simply connected, which is incompatible with a finite covering. Otherwise the target is simply
connected, so the covering is a diffeomorphism. There are at least two factors, since ri < pa ,
and its intermediate cohomology differs from that of a sphere. Both cases are impossible. The
top-cohomology fact stated above completes the proof.

Independent norm trees attain them by the construction in Section 2. Indeed, partition pa into

leaves. At a common generic regular contact, pointwise no-sharing proves the matching lower
bounds. Such a contact lies in the primal regular locus and the inverse images, under each source
projection, of the dual regular loci; their finite intersection is dense open by definable selection. This
comparison makes no claim about arbitrary unstructured factor maps. Global regularity therefore
adds one capacity unit per source with pa > c, and one factor exactly for those such sources with
c | pa . These are formulation resources, not intrinsic barrier parameters of the projected body. A
product of L Lorentz factors has standard ambient parameter 2L; the restrictions to the affine slices
can have smaller parameters, as examined below.

6.3   Why local and joint identities are weaker

For U (t) = ((1 + t2 )/2, (1 − t2 )/2, t) and V (t) = ((1 + t2 )/2, −(1 − t2 )/2, −t), both vectors are in Q3

    For k ≥ 2 equal balls, the same lower proof gives k(s − 1) + 1. Pairing the sources and
using the preceding construction on each pair, with one coordinate-square construction on any
remaining source, gives ks − ⌊k/2⌋ factors. We do not identify the exact count for k ≥ 3. These
are complete bounds on a restricted contact kernel; neither construction supplies the missing full
labelled rows of an affine lift. The phase obstruction alone cannot close the remaining gap: a
product of spheres of total dimension n embeds in Rn+1 . To check the last assertion, embed a sphere
as an orientable hypersurface, then observe that the boundary of a small tubular neighborhood
of N m ⊂ Rm+1 ⊂ Rm+p+1 is N × Sp ; the added normal bundle is trivial. Induction proves the
assertion.

Remark 6.4 (Switching must pass through open vertex sets). A Lorentz label productive for two
sources must have a nonempty open primal vertex set. Indeed choose dual phase patches for both
sources on which their derivatives are nonzero. Their images each contain more than one point, so
smaller patches can be chosen with disjoint images. On the product of these patches cylindrical
complementarity would force a nonzero primal phase to equal both images. Thus the primal factor
is zero on that open cylinder. Moreover, the nonzero locus of each productive dual factor cannot be
dense. Otherwise the identity saying that the primal derivative in every unrelated source direction
is radial extends by continuity to all points; this excludes productivity for the second source. In
particular, analyticity or empty-interior primal vertex sets make globally productive label sets
disjoint. The proof of Theorem 6.2 does not need either extra hypothesis.

Example 6.5 (A codimension-two defect destroys the smooth premium). In a full binary norm tree
for Bs2 , let tv (x) = ∥xSv ∥ for each internal node v, where Sv is its set of descendant leaves; leaves
carry their signed coordinates and the root value is one. At each internal node with children v0 , v1
take
                   Av (x) = (tv (x), tv0 (x), tv1 (x)), Bv (z) = (tv (z), −tv0 (z), −tv1 (z)).
Their pairings telescope to 1 − ⟨x, z⟩. The dual factors are genuine certificates of the tree slice,
since the same telescoping works when the primal internal variables are free. Boundary fibers are
unique: the nonnegative squared cone slacks sum to 1 − ∥x∥2 = 0, forcing every internal norm to its
minimum. Both factor maps are globally Lipschitz and semialgebraic, and analytic away from the
sets xSv = 0. Every nonroot internal node has at least two leaves, so these sets have codimension at
least two in the boundary sphere when nonempty. Nevertheless the tree uses only s − 1 copies of
Q3 . For s ≥ 3, Theorem 6.2 requires s copies for globally C 1 factors. Thus unique boundary fibers,
Lipschitz regularity, and dense-open analyticity do not imply the global smooth conclusion.

    The tree also has a unique genuine dual certificate for every support z ∈ Ss−1 . To see this, write
an arbitrary dual block at node v as (αv , βv,0 , βv,1 ) ∈ Q3 . Matching coefficients on the entire affine
slice forces
                                                           if child vj is leaf ℓ,
                                              (
                                                  −zℓ ,
                        αroot = 1,    βv,j =
                                                  −αvj , if child vj is internal.


                                                     25
Induction from the leaves, using αv ≥ (βv,02 + β 2 )1/2 , gives α ≥ ∥z ∥. At the root this is equality
                                                 v,1             v      Sv
because ∥z∥ = 1. Equality then propagates down the tree: each child head is nonnegative and at
least its subtree norm, so equality in the sum of their squares forces equality for each child separately.
This also handles a zero subtree, whose head and descendant coordinates must all vanish. Thus the
unique tuple is exactly Bv (z) above. Every nonzero block has Jordan rank one; a zero block has
rank zero.

Proof. The lower bound is Theorem 5.2 with B = 1. For the upper bound take independent binary
norm trees, with sa − 1 factors for source a. The linear isomorphism
                                                                                 !
                                                             t + z1   z2
                                     (t, z1 , z2 ) 7−→
                                                               z2   t − z1
identifies Q3 with the real PSD2 cone; multiplying the dual image by 1/2 preserves the pairing and
does not change rank.
    Any genuine pure-row certificate for source a vanishes on all factors belonging to other sources.
Indeed evaluate its identity at a feasible tuple with source a at its support contact and every other
source at a Slater point of its tree. The slack is zero, and each nonnegative block pairing must
therefore vanish. A positive dual element orthogonal to an interior primal element is zero. What
remains on source a is its unique tree certificate, by the coefficient argument above. Consequently
every positive pure-row aggregate has rank at most a (sa − 1). Equality holds on the dense open
                                                       P

support set where every internal subtree norm is positive. This proves the upper bound and hence
the formula.

    The inner supremum in (6.6) is over genuine pure-row certificates. It neither minimizes over nor
describes all certificates in the fiber of the aggregate objective.
    For completeness, local saturation itself gives an exact linear-algebra statement. If positive
semidefinite contact forms Hi sum to a positive metric H and i rank Hi = rank H, then Pi =
                                                                     P

H −1/2 Hi H −1/2 are mutually orthogonal projections. Indeed write Pi = Zi Zi∗ with Zi of full column
rank; concatenating the Zi gives a square matrix Z with ZZ ∗ = I, hence Z ∗ Z = I. This determines
the orthogonal decomposition of the tangent metric at the contact, but says nothing about unused
portions of the cone, higher derivatives, or the global affine slice. The global arguments above use
compactness and the full two-point identities in addition to this local rank equality.
Proposition 6.7 (Everywhere differentiability already forbids saturation). Assume the Lorentz
full-row factors are everywhere Fréchet differentiable, without continuity of their derivatives, and
pa ≥ 2. If ri ≤ c < maxa pa for an integer c ≥ 1, then
                                                             pa + 1.
                                                     X
                                               T ≥
                                                         a

For one source this gives the same exact resource minima as Theorem 6.2. For several sources it
gives the additional count L ≥ a ⌈pa /c⌉ + 1 whenever every pa is divisible by c; no full additive
                                 P

differentiability premium is asserted.

                                                         26
Proof. All pointwise phase and no-sharing calculations remain valid with pointwise derivatives.
Suppose T = a pa =: n. Sum the row slacks and aggregate their dual factors. At every contact,
               P

equality in the rank bound forces every factor to be nonzero and to use its full capacity. Its
primal phase is therefore defined everywhere. Their product q : M → i Sri is differentiable with
                                                                        Q

invertible derivative, because its kernel is annihilated by the positive contact metric. The finite-
dimensional local inversion theorem for everywhere differentiable maps with everywhere nonsingular
derivative [78] makes q a local homeomorphism. Compactness makes it a finite covering. Since M is
simply connected, circle factors are impossible; with all ri ≥ 2 the covering is a homeomorphism.
Consequently a (1 + tpa ) = i (1 + tri ). The smallest exponent and its coefficient determine
                Q               Q

its multiplicity; cancel that power of 1 + tj and repeat. Thus the multisets of exponents agree,
contradicting the cap. This proves the strict capacity bound. If every pa is divisible by c, the
baseline count L0 satisfies cL0 = a pa , so T ≤ cL forces L ≥ L0 + 1. For one source, combine the
                                  P

capacity and count bounds with the grouped polynomial construction. If c ∤ p, its factor count is
already ⌈(p + 1)/c⌉ by rounding; if c | p use the extra factor.

   A simple smooth example shows why analytic continuation cannot replace the cover argument.
                  2
Put h(t) = e−1/t for t > 0 and h(t) = 0 for t ≤ 0, and use U from Proposition 6.3. For distinct
sources a, b, the factors

                         A(x) = h((xa )1 )U ((xb )1 ),            B a (v) = h(−v1 )U (0)

are smooth and nonnegative Lorentz-valued, with ⟨A(x), B a (xa )⟩ = 0 on the entire cylinder. The
dual is not identically zero, yet the primal phase varies in source b wherever (xa )1 > 0. This refutes
continuation of a cylindrical ray identity across arbitrary open zero sets. It is a counterexample to
that proof step, not a full-row factorization with fewer than the required factors.


7     Support-orbit rigidity and selected ranks
7.1    Saturation and support orbits
For a simple Euclidean Jordan algebra V of rank r and Peirce constant a, write Ip (V ) for its
manifold of rank-p idempotents. Its dimension is ap(r − p) and its tangent space at an idempotent c
is V (c, 1/2). These are classical facts about idempotent manifolds [31, 73]. We use them to identify
the additional restriction imposed by globally smooth factor maps.
Theorem 7.1 (Global saturation). Let C ⊂ Rn+1 be a compact convex body, n ≥ 1, containing
the origin in its interior, and suppose P = ∂C and D = ∂C ◦ are strictly convex C 1 hypersurfaces.
Suppose the full slack has a finite globally labelled factorization

Peirce constant ai . Put ci = ai ⌊ri2 /4⌋, with ci = 0 for rays. If i ci = n, each positive-capacity

factor has constant balanced complementary contact ranks pi , ri − pi , and the joint support map

                                              Ipi (Vi ),        x 7−→ (supp Xi (x))i

is a finite covering. There is exactly one positive-capacity factor. It is the Lorentz cone Qn+2 , except
that n = 2 also permits S3+ at the level of the support-orbit topology.

                                                           27
Proof. Let z be the unique polar contact of x. The contact correspondence is a homeomorphism; no
differentiability of that correspondence is needed. The tangent spaces are Tx P = z ⊥ and Tz D = x⊥ .
Their Euclidean pairing is nondegenerate: its left kernel consists of vectors in z ⊥ ∩ span{x}, which
is zero because ⟨x, z⟩ = 1. Mixed differentiation in independent boundary charts gives

                    ⟨u, v⟩ =       Hi (u, v),    Hi (u, v) = −⟨dXi (x)[u], dYi (z)[v]⟩.

The cross-Peirce argument of Lemma 3.3, which for its rank bound uses only the two independent

                    rank Hi ≤ ai pi qi ≤ ci ,   pi = rankJ Xi (x),   qi = rankJ Yi (z).

Nondegeneracy and i ci = n force equality throughout. For every positive-capacity factor, this
                      P

gives pi + qi = ri , the ranks are balanced, and rank Hi = ci . Ranks are lower semicontinuous along
the contact graph. Since the two ranks sum to ri , each is locally constant, hence constant by
connectedness.
    In the following support-differential argument all indices satisfy ci > 0; zero-capacity factors
have Hi = 0 and play no role. Let ei (x) = supp Xi (x). This is C 1 : locally the zero spectral cluster
is separated from the positive spectrum, and a smooth scalar cutoff equal to zero on the former and
one on the latter gives ei by spectral functional calculus. Differentiating ei ◦ ei = ei and Xi ◦ ei = Xi
shows that
                       dei [u] ∈ Vi (ei , 1/2), LXi dei [u] = 12 projVi (ei ,1/2) dXi [u].
Here LXi is invertible on the half-Peirce space: in a frame adapted to the positive support it acts
on a cross channel by λj /2 > 0. Consequently dei [u] = 0 precisely when the cross-Peirce component
of dXi [u] vanishes. In particular

                                       ci = rank Hi ≤ rank dei ≤ ci .

If every dei kills u, every Hi (u, v) is zero, so u = 0. The joint support differential is therefore
injective, and its domain and codomain have the same dimension n. The joint map is a local
C 1 diffeomorphism. Its image is open and, by compactness, closed in the connected target. It is
surjective and proper, hence a finite covering.
    We give the topological classification explicitly. For n ≥ 2, the source P ∼
                                                                               = S n is simply connected,
so this is the universal cover of the target. There can be no circle factor, since it would make the
target fundamental group infinite. The remaining factors have compact simply connected universal
covers. The universal cover of their product is the product of those covers. If there were at least
two positive-dimensional factors, the mod-two top cohomology class of one factor, tensored with the
unit classes of the others, would give nonzero cohomology in a degree strictly between zero and n.
The Künneth formula contradicts the cohomology of S n . Thus there is one factor. For n = 1, the
same conclusion follows directly from the sum of the positive integer dimensions.
    The simple-algebra classification now leaves the following cases. A spin factor has primitive-
idempotent orbit S m−2 and therefore gives Qn+2 . For Hr (R) the orbit is the real Grassmannian
Grp (Rp+q ), p + q = r. At r = 3 its balanced orbit is RP2 , covered by S 2 . At r ≥ 4, balanced ranks
satisfy p, q ≥ 2. The oriented Grassmannian is simply connected by the exact sequence of

                           SO(p) × SO(q) −→ SO(p + q) −→ Gr+
                                                           p (R
                                                               p+q
                                                                   ):

the induced map on fundamental groups onto π1 (SO(p + q)) = Z2 is surjective. Its kernel is nonzero:
use the diagonal element when p, q ≥ 3, and an even nonzero winding in an SO(2) factor otherwise.

                                                     28
Exactness therefore gives nonzero π2 of the oriented Grassmannian. Its dimension pq ≥ 4 precludes
a sphere.
    The complex Grassmannian is simply connected by the fibration with isotropy U (p) × U (q) in
U (p + q). The map on π1 is addition Z2 → Z, so its kernel gives nonzero π2 of the Grassmannian.
For r ≥ 3 its dimension 2pq ≥ 4 precludes a sphere. For quaternionic Grassmannians, the groups
Sp(k) are simply connected and π3 (Sp(k)) = Z, with standard inclusions preserving a generator.
These facts follow inductively from Sp(k − 1) → Sp(k) → S 4k−1 and Sp(1) = S 3 . The map on π3
of the isotropy inclusion is again addition Z2 → Z, giving nonzero π4 of the Grassmannian. Its
dimension 4pq ≥ 8 precludes a sphere. The classical-group and Grassmannian fibrations used here
are described in [48, Examples 4.53–4.55]. Finally the balanced idempotent orbit of H3 (O) is the
Cayley plane: rank-one idempotents are its points and complementation identifies the rank-two orbit
with it [9, Section 3.4]. Its cells have dimensions 0, 8, 16, so H 8 (−; Z) = Z; see [48, Example 4.47].
It is not a sphere. Order-two real, complex, and quaternionic Hermitian algebras are already spin
factors. This exhausts the classification.

7.2    Eliminating the real order-three exception
The apparent exceptional possibility is ruled out by the full ball slack. The following elementary
observation avoids a classification of linear determinantal representations.
Example 7.2 (Local constant rank need not extend globally). The affine pencil

                       1 + x3
                                                  
                                x1     x2
               L(x) =  x1    1 − x3    0 ,                det L(x) = (1 − x3 )(1 − ∥x∥2 ),
                                           
                         x2      0   1 − x3

is positive semidefinite of rank two on S 2 \ {(0, 0, 1)}. Indeed, its lower 2 × 2 block is positive definite
and its Schur complement is zero there. Its kernel line is
                                                  x1     x2
                                                                  
                                      span 1, −       ,−      ,
                                                1 − x3 1 − x3
so its kernel map is a local diffeomorphism onto an affine chart of RP2 by stereographic coordinates.
At the north pole, L(0, 0, 1) = diag(2, 0, 0) has rank one. Thus affine behavior and constant rank on
an open sphere patch do not justify global constant rank or a common kernel. This example only
invalidates that local-to-global shortcut. Proposition 7.5 below excludes the global slack factorization
using the full C 1 factor maps and finite affine gluing.
Lemma 7.3 (Injective kernels of an affine sphere pencil). Let s ≥ 2 and X(x) = C0 + sj=1 xj Cj be
                                                                                               P

a real symmetric matrix pencil, positive semidefinite of nullity one for every x ∈ S s−1 . If its kernels
do not share a nonzero vector, its kernel-line map is injective.
Proof. Suppose distinct x, y have the same kernel line, containing k ̸= 0. The affine scalar function
f (v) = k T X(v)k = a + b · v is nonnegative on the unit sphere and vanishes at x and y. Nonnegativity
says a ≥ ∥b∥. If b ̸= 0, a zero is possible only when a = ∥b∥ and v = −b/∥b∥, giving only one zero.
Thus a = b = 0. For every v, positivity and k T X(v)k = 0 imply X(v)k = 0, contradicting the
absence of a common nonzero kernel vector.

Lemma 7.4 (Finite affine selection on a sphere). Let n ≥ 2 and let F : S n → W be C 1 , with W
finite dimensional. Suppose a dense open subset is the union of finitely many nonempty open sets
Ui , and F |Ui = Li |Ui for ambient affine maps Li : Rn+1 → W . Then F is the restriction of one
affine map.

                                                     29
Proof. Merge sets with identical Li and put Ei = Ui . Continuity of values and tangent derivatives
gives j 1 F = j 1 (Li |S n ) on Ei . If distinct affine maps have the same tangent first jet at z ∈ S n , their
difference is M (z · x − 1) for some nonzero M ∈ W . Such a difference has matching first jet at no
other sphere point: matching derivatives force the other normal line to be Rz, and matching values
exclude −z. Thus each intersection Ei ∩ Ej lies in at most one point. Remove the resulting finite
set Z. The connected space S n \ Z is partitioned into the disjoint relatively closed sets Ei \ Z; each
is also relatively open because the partition is finite. Only one is nonempty. But the closure of a
nonempty open Ui cannot be finite, so there was only one distinct affine map. Density finishes the
proof.

Proposition 7.5 (No real order-three saturation for a ball). There is no factorization
                                                      q
                       1 − x · y = tr(X(x)Y (y)) +          aj (x)bj (y),
                                                      X
                                                                            x, y ∈ S 2 ,
                                                      j=1

with X, Y : S 2 → S3+ of class C 1 , continuous nonnegative aj , bj , finite q, constant complementary
ranks 2, 1, and a rank-one support map that is a local diffeomorphism onto RP2 .
Proof. Exchange X, Y if necessary so that Y has rank one. Every diagonal summand is zero, hence
aj (x)bj (x) = 0. For each of the finitely many continuous nonnegative functions f = aj , bj , the set
{f > 0} ∪ int{f = 0} is dense open. Intersect these sets and partition the result by their finitely
many positivity/zero patterns. On each nonempty open pattern set U , for each j at least one of
aj |U , bj |U is identically zero. Therefore all ray terms vanish for every pair (x, y) ∈ U × U .
     The rank-one support map of Y sends U onto a projective open set. Consequently the matrices
Y (y), y ∈ U , span S3 : an annihilating symmetric matrix would have a quadratic form zero on an
open set of projective directions, and hence zero identically. Choose six yℓ ∈ U whose matrices are a
basis. The equations
                                    tr(X(x)Y (yℓ )) = 1 − x · yℓ ,  x ∈ U,
force X|U to be an ambient affine pencil. There are only finitely many pattern sets, so Lemma 7.4
makes X one affine pencil on the whole sphere.
    Complementarity identifies the kernel line of X(x) with the support line of Y (x). This map is
a finite covering S 2 → RP2 , hence the universal double cover. Its image contains all lines, so its
kernels have no common nonzero vector. But Lemma 7.3 makes the map injective, contradicting its
two-point fibers.

Corollary 7.6 (Ball saturation classification). A finite globally bi-C 1 symmetric-cone factorization
of the full slack of Bs2 , s ≥ 2, satisfying i ai ⌊ri2 /4⌋ = s − 1 has exactly one positive-capacity factor,
                                            P

namely Qs+1 . Remaining factors, if any, are rays.
Proof. Apply Theorem 7.1 and exclude its sole additional case by Proposition 7.5.

                           s−1≤                             ai (ri − qi )qi ≤ B

         P

Each nonzero dual factor has qi = 1, ai (ri − 1) = B, and pi = ri − 1. Its primitive support orbit
has dimension B. The support-differential argument above gives a finite covering from SqB to the
product of these q primitive-idempotent orbits.
    If q > 1, a circle target gives infinite fundamental group. Otherwise the compact universal covers
of the positive-dimensional target factors have nonzero intermediate product cohomology, ruling
out a sphere source. If q = 1, the simple-algebra classification identifies the primitive orbit as a
sphere for spin factors, real projective space for real Hermitian factors, complex or quaternionic
projective space in the other classical cases, and the Cayley plane in the exceptional case. Complex
and quaternionic projective spaces have intermediate cohomology unless their matrix order is two;
the Cayley plane has its nonzero middle cohomology. These order-two cases are spin factors. For
real order R ≥ 3 the remaining map is the double covering SR−1 → RPR−1 . All other dual factors
vanish identically. Pairing the primal sheet with a basis chosen from the rank-one dual sheet makes
it an ambient affine pencil, since rank-one matrices with directions in a projective open set span
the symmetric matrices. Its kernel-line map has no common nonzero kernel and hence is injective
by Lemma 7.3, contradicting the double cover. Thus the only saturated possibility is the stated
matching spin factor. In every other case some rank is at least q + 1.
    For attainment outside the exceptional branch, choose a dictionary member attaining B and
partition all s coordinates into g = ⌈s/B⌉ nonempty groups embedded in its primitive half-Peirce

    To apply Theorem 7.7, note that proper faces have strictly smaller per-rank capacity than their
parent simple non-ray cone: classical faces have smaller order, spin faces are rays, and Albert proper
non-ray faces have capacity eight rather than sixteen. Thus each remaining face has capacity at
most B. If equality is needed in the rank chain of that theorem, the face must be an unreduced
original cone attaining B. In particular, a newly created proper face cannot introduce a matching
spin exception at capacity B. The same proof therefore gives at least κK (sa ) new rank at some
next contact. Iteration and Lemma 5.1 prove the bound; complementarity gives nullity. Separate
copies of (7.2) and the exceptional direct sections give equality at every contact.

explicit lower bound on ga .

full-slack factorizations of Bs2 over simple symmetric cones of real dimension at most an integer

8     The standard barrier after affine restriction
The rank of a certificate measures one exposed support. A barrier must control every direction in its
domain, including directions inside a projection fiber. We now make that distinction quantitative.

8.1   The parameter and two boundary tests
Write L = X ◦ + T for the effective affine slice of a product of Euclidean Jordan algebras, after
minimal-face reduction, and set Ω = L ∩ int K. The word effective means that redundant variables
which do not change the cone tuple have been removed. For

                                           F (X) = −              log det Xi ,

survives affine restriction, so (8.1) is precisely the least self-concordant-barrier parameter of this
specified function. It is not the infimum over other barriers on Ω. Ambient Jordan rank gives an
upper bound but need not give equality.

alone would not justify the conclusion.

Proof. The generic full-certificate rank theorem and complementarity force a boundary tuple with

                                            g

gives the sum parameter at most g. At any sphere point set tG = ∥wG ∥2 . Each fixed-tail block has

with X̄i implies that the compressed nullity z in Lemma 8.2 is at least q. Since i rankJ Di ≥ 1,
