that lemma proves the assertion.

   Consequently the unrestricted single-ball minimum is already exact when B does not divide s − 1:
both endpoints are ⌈s/B⌉. The same statement applies to Hermitian order caps with B = a(R − 1)
and to Lorentz dimension caps with B = d − 2. The remaining issue is bounded lifts at a divisible
tangent dimension. If every symmetric cone of real dimension at most d is permitted, the maximum
primitive capacity is B = d − 2, attained by Qd . Thus the exact smoothly selected standard-barrier
minimum is ⌈s/(d − 2)⌉ for 3 ≤ d < s + 1, and one for d ≥ s + 1. Separate source balls add these
quantities. This dimension-cap statement allows all symmetric cone types; using a higher-rank type
cannot improve the optimum.

8.2   A complete selection-free result at one channel
Theorem 8.4 (One-channel rigidity). Let s = B + 1. The infimum of νstd,slice over finite affine
lifts through K equals one if the dictionary contains QB+2 , and equals two otherwise. In the latter
case the corresponding infimum for (BB+1
                                       2   )b equals 2b. There are simultaneous pure-row certificates
whose every positive aggregate has total Jordan rank at least 2b.

Proof. Suppose first that one lift has νstd,slice < 2. The integral boundary orders in Lemma 8.1
imply that every boundary tuple has nullity at most one. Thus every member of every normalized
genuine certificate fiber D(v) has rank at most one. Such a member is nonzero, since its slack is
not identically zero. The positive midpoint of two elements on different product-cone rays has rank
at least two. Convexity therefore puts D(v) on one ray. The whole-slice identity fixes its scalar,
making D(v) = {Y (v)}.
    A Slater point provides the uniform normalization

                            0 < 1 − ∥πX ◦ ∥ ≤ ⟨X ◦ , Y (v)⟩ ≤ 1 + ∥πX ◦ ∥.

Strict positivity of X ◦ bounds Y (v) above and away from zero. The closed graph then makes Y
continuous. On the connected sphere its one active factor is fixed. Projectivization gives a continuous
injection SB → P(Vj ) into that factor’s primitive-ray manifold: if the rays agree, evaluating the
genuine identities at a lift of the ball’s center fixes their scalar, and the identities then fix v.
    The target dimension is aj (rj − 1) ≤ B. Invariance of dimension excludes a smaller dimension,
and invariance of domain makes the map a homeomorphism in equal dimension. The primitive-ray
spaces are RPr−1 , CPr−1 , HPr−1 , spheres for spin factors, and OP2 for the Albert algebra. The
classification and cohomology used in Section 7 show that the only sphere cases are the spin factors,
including the order-two classical coincidences. Thus a matching spin factor is necessary for a value
below two. Its direct section attains one; otherwise the two-group construction (7.2) attains two.
    For the product assertion apply sequential compression as in Section 5. At the next source,
compress the entire original certificate fiber to the faces complementary to the previously chosen


                                                  35
support join. The resulting families are compact, convex, and have closed graphs: the original fibers
are uniformly bounded by the same Slater normalization, and compression is linear. Their genuine
row identities on the remaining contact cylinder ensure that zero is not in any compressed fiber. If
every compressed certificate had total rank at most one, the preceding singleton and primitive-ray
argument would again give a matching capacity-B spin factor in one of the remaining faces. A
proper face of a simple factor has strictly smaller primitive capacity than that factor; hence no such
face can arise when the original dictionary has no matching spin factor. Some compressed certificate
must therefore have rank at least two. The support-join compression identity of Section 5 adds at
least two new rank units at each source. The final join, and every positive aggregate of the chosen
certificates, has rank at least 2b. All tuples at that simultaneous contact are complementary to
this join, so the boundary-order test gives νstd,slice ≥ 2b. Separate two-group constructions attain
equality.

    In particular, an Albert-cone dictionary has B = 16: its critical 17-dimensional ball has restricted
standard-barrier minimum two. The theorem concerns the optimum over lifts. It does not say that
every lift using a matching spin factor has parameter one.

8.3       Arbitrarily many factors under a wide cap
We give a geometric argument that closes a further part of the divisible case without any selection
hypothesis or factor-count restriction.

Lemma 8.5 (A face-codimension budget). Suppose a bounded relative-Slater affine slice P = L ∩ K
projects onto Bs2 . For any X ∈ P over its boundary, let EX be the linear span of the minimal face
of K containing X. Then codim EX ≥ s.

Proof. Write L = X ◦ + T . Since P is bounded and nonempty, T ∩ K = {0}; also 0 ∈       / L, since
otherwise P would be a bounded cone with nonempty relative interior projecting onto a ball. On
b = span{X ◦ } + T define height ℓ(tX ◦ + H) = t. A cone point of zero height is zero. There
L
are no cone points of negative height: otherwise adding a suitable positive multiple of X ◦ gives
a nonzero cone point of height zero; pointedness excludes cancellation to zero. Hence L   b ∩ K is
precisely the cone over P . The projection homogenizes to a linear surjection πb:Lb→R   s+1 whose
cone image is Qs+1 . If k = dim ker π
                                    b , then dim L = s + 1 + k. Every H ∈ L ∩ EX permits two-sided
                                                  b                       b
small feasible perturbations of X, because X ∈ ri(K ∩ EX ). Their images lie in the line spanned
by the target extreme ray. Thus dim(L     b ∩ EX ) ≤ k + 1. Grassmann’s dimension inequality gives
k + 1 ≥ s + 1 + k − codim EX , as required.

Theorem 8.6 (Wide-cap rigidity). Fix either Hermitian cones over one field F ∈ {R, C, H} with
order at most R, or Lorentz cones with dimension at most d. Put respectively B = a(R − 1) or
B = d − 2. Allow any finite number of factors and rays. If q ≥ 2 and B ≥ q − 1, the minimum of
νstd,slice for affine lifts of BqB+1
                                2    is q + 1.

Proof. Unbounded lifts were settled by Proposition 8.3. For a bounded lift suppose νstd,slice < q + 1.
All boundary tuples then have integer total nullity at most q. In a Hermitian block of order r ≤ R
and nullity m, the minimal-face codimension is
             ar(r − 1)        a(r − m)(r − m − 1)     am(2r − m − 1)
                                                      
      r+               − r−m+                     =m+                ≤ (B + 1)m.
                 2                     2                    2
A Lorentz block has codimension di − 1 ≤ B + 1 at a nonzero boundary point, nullity one there,
and codimension di ≤ 2(B + 1) at its vertex, nullity two there. Rays obey the same bound. Hence

                                                  36
codim EX ≤ (B + 1) nul X. Lemma 8.5 rules out nullity at most q − 1, since (q − 1)(B + 1) < qB + 1
under the stated hypothesis. Every tuple in every boundary fiber therefore has nullity exactly q.
    Each compact boundary fiber is a singleton. Indeed, from a relative-interior point of a nontrivial
fiber take a maximal nonzero affine chord. Its endpoint must leave the relative interior of the
minimal cone face, and thus acquire an additional null eigenvalue. The resulting nullity exceeds q, a
contradiction. Compactness makes the unique boundary lift X(v) continuous.
    On a dense open set the curvature chain is saturated:

                                                          ai (ri − qi )qi ≤ B
                                 X                    X                            X
                         qB ≤          a i p i qi ≤                                       qi ≤ qB.
                                  i                   i                               i

Exactly q cap-attaining blocks are consequently of corank one; all other blocks are interior. Along a
limit of any one generic active pattern its zero eigenvalues persist. Constant total nullity excludes
any additional zero eigenvalue or any new active block. Closures of distinct patterns are thus
disjoint, and their finite union covers the connected sphere. One pattern is fixed everywhere.
    The corresponding kernel lines define a continuous map from SqB to (FP R−1 )q , or to (SB )q in
the Lorentz case. It is injective. Indeed, equal lines put the two tuples in the relative interior of the
same product face. Any certificate exposing one projected point annihilates that face, so the genuine
slack identity makes the other projected point identical. Source and target are compact connected
manifolds of the same dimension, so invariance of domain would make the map a homeomorphism.
The target has nonzero intermediate mod-two cohomology, whereas the sphere does not. This
contradiction proves the lower bound. The grouped construction with q + 1 groups attains it.

   The proof also isolates a useful sufficient condition independent of cap width: a compact lift at
s−1 = qB, q ≥ 2, cannot have total boundary nullity exactly q at every point of every boundary fiber.
Such constant nullity would give singleton fibers and the same impossible global projective-product
embedding.

8.4   What a remaining bounded lift would have to do
The exact results above leave a sharply delimited classification problem. For a fixed Hermitian order
cap or Lorentz dimension cap, the single-ball cases not resolved by the preceding results have

                    s = qB + 1,           q ≥ B + 2,             q≤          inf      νstd,slice ≤ q + 1.   (8.6)
                                                                       affine lifts

Any lift improving the upper endpoint must be bounded. The following structural result develops
the consequences of such a hypothetical improvement; none of our preceding theorems depends on
resolving this remaining interval.

Proposition 8.7 (Certificate collapse and fixed generic labels). Fix a field F ∈ {R, C, H}, an order
cap R ≥ 2, and B = a(R − 1), where a = dimR F. Consider a bounded affine lift through finitely
many Hermitian cones of orders at most R and optional rays, in the divisible case s − 1 = qB,
q ≥ 2. Suppose νstd,slice < q + 1. For v ∈ SqB let P(v) and D(v) be its full primal and normalized
certificate fibers. Define
                                                                 (                                     )
                  Ei (v) =             RanF Yi ,          Z=      v:          dimF Ei (v) ≤ q − 1 ,
                              X                                        X

                             Y ∈D(v)                                     i

counting a nonzero ray certificate as dimension one. Then Z is nonempty and semialgebraic,
and dim Z ≤ qB − B. On a dense open semialgebraic set U , both fibers are singletons, strict

                                                            37
complementarity holds, and exactly q full-order blocks are of corank one. If B ≥ 2, one and the
same set of q block labels is active on all of U ; thus different generic label patterns cannot account
for a putative improvement. The possible remaining obstruction is degeneration of this fixed pattern
above Z.

Proof. The boundary-order bound gives nullity at most q at every primal boundary tuple, so
every certificate has rank at most q. A finite positive average spans all the spaces Ei (v), hence
  i dim Ei (v) ≤ q. Semialgebraicity follows by expressing the spanning condition with finitely many
P

certificates (the ambient dimension bounds their number) and eliminating quantifiers.
    Choose a dense open generic set where the full-fiber minimum-rank theorem holds. All certificates
there have rank exactly q, and all primal tuples have nullity exactly q. The compact primal chord
argument in Theorem 8.6 gives a singleton primal fiber. The dual fiber lies in a product face of
rank at most q; if it contained a nontrivial maximal chord, an endpoint would leave that face’s
relative interior and have rank below q. Thus it too is a singleton. On a further dense open
smooth locus, equality in the curvature chain gives exactly q full-order corank-one blocks and strict
complementarity.
    On each smooth stratum of Z of dimension t, choose smooth primal and certificate selections
after further stratification. Differentiating the slack on two copies of this stratum gives its positive
definite tangent metric, of rank t. The certificate ranks sum to at most q − 1, so the mixed Peirce
estimate gives t ≤ B(q − 1). This proves the dimension bound, including for the semialgebraic
closure Z.
    For any support v, compactness gives a limit of generic primal tuples. Its nullity is at least q by
persistence of zero eigenvalues, and at most q by the parameter hypothesis. If v ∈   / Z, an averaged
certificate of rank q forces every primal in that fiber to have nullity q. The fiber is consequently
a singleton. This singleton is the limit of every approaching sequence of generic tuples. Passing
to a subsequence of fixed generic labels shows that it has exactly that pattern: an additional zero
eigenvalue would exceed nullity q. Its inactive blocks are positive definite. Compactness then
provides a neighborhood of v in which no generic tuple has a new active label; since each generic
tuple has exactly q labels, its pattern is fixed.
    If Z were empty, this argument would give a continuous singleton lift and one fixed pattern on the
whole sphere. The kernel-line injection and projective-product contradiction in Theorem 8.6 would
apply. Hence Z ̸= ∅. If B ≥ 2, the complement of the closed semialgebraic set Z, of codimension
at least two, is connected. For example, semialgebraic triangulation and a small general-position
perturbation of a path avoid a subcomplex of codimension at least two. The local pattern just
proved is constant on this complement. Every nonempty open generic pattern region meets the
complement because Z has empty interior. There can therefore be only one such pattern.

    For Lorentz cones of dimension at most d ≥ 3, set B = d − 2 and retain the same boundedness
and parameter hypotheses, with optional rays. The Lorentz version replaces Ei by the support
face generated by the whole certificate fiber, and its dimension count by Jordan rank. Its rank-one
orbit has dimension at most B. The same proof gives dim Z ≤ qB − B and, for B ≥ 2, the
same fixed-label conclusion. When B = 1, the exceptional set may have codimension one and the
connected-complement argument does not apply.
    There is a useful precise statement about the topology of the exceptional fibers. The contact
incidence space
                          Γ = {(v, X, Y ) : v ∈ SqB , X ∈ P(v), Y ∈ D(v)}
is compact: primal compactness is assumed and the Slater normalization uniformly bounds all
dual fibers. The fiber of Γ → SqB is exactly P(v) × D(v), since every primal is complementary to


                                                  38
every genuine certificate. This is a nonempty compact convex set. The Vietoris–Begle theorem for
compact spaces with acyclic fibers [12] implies

                                         Ȟ k (Γ; Z/2) ∼
                                                       = Ȟ k (SqB ; Z/2).                            (8.7)

Thus the incidence space itself supplies no additional cohomology. The problem is that the generic
kernel-line map need not extend across rank-deficient fibers. Neither a global map nor a degree for
it follows from (8.7). We do not assert such an extension.

8.5   Counterexamples to stronger fiberwise conclusions
Example 8.8 (Boundary selections can escape over a compact target). Put δ = 1 − x1 and impose
three positive semidefinite blocks:
                                    !                        !                           !
                      1 + x1 x2                     δ x2                     δ  u
                                        ⪰ 0,                     ⪰ 0,                        ⪰ 0.
                        x2   δ                      x2 u                     u z−1

The first block describes exactly B22 . For every point of this ball with δ > 0, the remaining blocks
are feasible precisely when u ≥ x22 /δ and z ≥ 1 + u2 /δ. At (x1 , x2 ) = (1, 0), they instead require
u = 0 and z ≥ 1. Thus the projection is the entire closed disk. The choice x = 0, u = 1, z = 3
makes all three blocks positive definite, so the lift has Slater feasibility. On the boundary x1 = 1 − δ,
x22 = 2δ − δ 2 , every feasible completion satisfies

                                                        (2 − δ)2
                            u ≥ 2 − δ,         z ≥1+             −→ ∞            (δ ↓ 0).
                                                           δ
Consequently no boundary selection is locally bounded at (1, 0). Compactness of the target and
Slater feasibility do not imply bounded primal choices near contact; compactness assumptions on
the lifted boundary therefore require separate justification.

Lemma 8.9 (A compact base removes one gradient unit). Let F be a k-logarithmically homogeneous
self-concordant barrier on a pointed cone, with positive definite Hessian on its span. On a nonempty
base ℓ(X) = 1, where ℓ is nonnegative on the cone, its restricted gradient parameter is at most k − 1.

Proof. Write g = ∇F , H = ∇2 F . Homogeneity gives HX = −g and X T HX = k. The squared
dual norm of the gradient on ker ℓ is its orthogonal projection in the inverse-Hessian norm:
                                                                             1
                               min(g − αℓ)T H −1 (g − αℓ) = k −                     .
                                α                                       ℓT H −1 ℓ
The unit Dikin ellipsoid lies in the cone; since ℓ(X) = 1 and ℓ is nonnegative there, ℓT H −1 ℓ ≤ 1.
This proves the claim. Compactness of the base is a convenient sufficient condition for a strictly
positive height; the argument only needs the conditions stated above.

Example 8.10 (Small minimum nullity, larger global parameter). The Lorentz specialization of

2q − 1. For the upper bound homogenize 1 to τ in (8.8); the resulting cone has a compact τ = 1

strictly positive source weights, and a (2qa − 1) standard parameter. No constant mass can transfer
                                     P

between source certificates: a source affine slack µa − λa vaT xa is nonnegative only if µa ≥ λa , and
equality of the total constants forces equality sourcewise.
    This example disproves an extra-unit lower bound with the quantifier “every primal completion
at some support.” A single high-nullity completion is enough for the barrier lower bound, and is
exactly what that stronger statement would miss.
    Two simpler examples explain why convex fibers alone cannot globalize several projective channels.
In a two-dimensional F-subspace U , the set {Y ⪰ 0 : Ran Y ⊂ U, tr Y = 1} contains all primitive
rays in U , normalized to trace one, and their rank-two mixtures. At its scalar center there is no
distinguished eigenline. Adding q − 2 fixed rank-one blocks stays within a total rank budget of q.
For a primal example, encode the simplex qj=0 pj = 1, pj ≥ 0, using
                                           P


                    X0 = diag(p0 , p1 ),    Xj = diag(1, pj+1 ),         1 ≤ j ≤ q − 1,

and project to σ = qj=2 pj ∈ [0, 1]. The fiber at σ = 0 has p0 = t, p1 = 1 − t: its first kernel
                     P

changes between two lines at the endpoints and disappears in between. Its nullity is q − 1 inside
the fiber and q at either endpoint. The standard barrier is − qj=0 log pj , with exact parameter q
                                                               P

by Lemmas 8.9 and 8.1. This is an interval lift, not a ball lift in the critical dimension. It shows
precisely why a proof for the unsettled range must use more than boundedness, convexity, and a
nullity bound.


9    Exact parameters of concrete affine formulations
This section compares three representations of balls on their actual affine domains. The norm
tree minimizes the number of small cone factors, but its restricted standard barrier is often less

                                                   40
economical than the grouped formulation. The grouped and column-packed slices also admit lower
bounds for every self-concordant barrier on the same domain, including barriers with arbitrary
mixed derivatives. For an open convex domain Ω in its affine hull, write ϑopt (Ω) for the infimum of
the parameters of all self-concordant barriers with positive definite Hessian on that affine hull. This
infimum allows changes of the barrier, while retaining the formulation.
    The standard-parameter calculations for norm trees, grouped slices, and column packing also
appear in the unpublished companion manuscript The Cost of Following the Central Path [21].
We reproduce them with proofs to keep the present treatment self-contained. Here they support
additional questions about arbitrary barriers on a fixed affine domain and resource frontiers across
different lifts; the shared calculations and constructions are not claimed as separate new contributions.

9.1   Norm trees: the root incidence determines the standard parameter
Let T have N ≥ 2 leaves and L internal nodes, each with cv ≥ 2 children. At an internal node v, let
tv be its axial variable and let yv collect the values of its children: a leaf contributes a projected
coordinate, and an internal child w contributes tw . The linked cone has tv > ∥yv ∥ for every v. Its
root slice, with tr = 1, is denoted by ΩT and projects onto int BN  2 . Put

                     qv = t2v − ∥yv ∥2 ,      ΦT = −           log qv ,    FT = ΦT |tr =1 .
                                                       X

                                                           v

The positive-sheet condition tv > 0 is part of the domain.

Theorem 9.1 (Root-incidence law). The least parameter of the displayed restricted standard barrier

Corollary 9.2 (The best capped norm tree). Fix a Lorentz dimension cap d ≥ 3, and set

                 A = d − 2,          E = N − 1,        L0 = ⌈E/A⌉ ,              σ = L0 A − E.

Define χ = 1 if L0 ≥ 3 and σ ≥ max{0, d − L0 }, and define χ = 0 otherwise. Among all norm trees
with cv + 1 ≤ d,
                                  min ν(FT ) = 2L0 − 1 − χ.                                (9.4)
                                         T

Proof. The tree identity v (cv − 1) = E, with 1 ≤ cv − 1 ≤ A, gives L ≥ L0 . Any list of L0
                          P

increments in this interval with sum E is realized by a chain of internal nodes, as in Corollary 3.4.
For an all-internal root of arity c, one must additionally have 2 ≤ c ≤ min(d − 1, L0 − 1). The
remaining L0 − 1 increments must sum to E − (c − 1). Such increments exist exactly when

                       L0 ≥ 3,         E ≤ (L0 − 1)A + min(d − 1, L0 − 1) − 1.

The lower bound on their sum causes no further constraint, since E ≥ L0 and the root increment
may be chosen no larger than E − L0 + 1. Partitioning the remaining internal nodes into c nonempty
chains realizes the required descendant forest. The displayed inequality is precisely the condition
defining χ. An additional factor cannot improve the minimum: even its better root type gives
2(L0 + 1) − 2 > 2L0 − 1.

    The root-fixed domain has a barrier lower bound that does not depend on the displayed
logarithms.

Proposition 9.3 (Intrinsic norm-tree bounds). Every self-concordant barrier on ΩT has parameter
at least L. Every logarithmically homogeneous self-concordant barrier on the unfixed linked cone has
degree at least L + 1. The corresponding root-fixed lower bound for an independent forest is the sum
of its numbers of internal nodes.



                                                       42
Proof. Choose positive leaf values with squared sum one and set t∗v equal to the norm of the leaves
below v. Each zv∗ = (t∗v , yv∗ ) is a nonzero boundary vector. Intersect the linked affine slice with the
product of two-planes
                                        zv = αv (t∗v , yv∗ ) + βv (t∗v , −yv∗ ).
Cone membership is exactly αv , βv ≥ 0; its determinant is 4(t∗v )2 αv βv . Root fixing and linking
become
              αr + βr = 1,      αw + βw = αv − βv (w an internal child of v).                  (9.5)
The L variables βv are affine coordinates: these equations determine every αv recursively. Small
positive values of all βv give strictly positive αv , so the section has dimension L and meets the
interior of the linked slice. Nonnegativity and the equations bound all coordinates by one. At β = 0,
all αv = 1, and exactly the L independent inequalities βv ≥ 0 are active. Every relative boundary
point of this polytope lies on a cone boundary. Restriction of any barrier on ΩT is therefore a
barrier on this polytope. The independent-active-facet lower bound [70, Proposition 2.3.6] proves
the claim. Product sections prove the forest extension.
    The corresponding independent-facet principle for logarithmically homogeneous barriers on
polyhedral cones, with one additional parameter unit at a nonzero boundary point, is due to
Hildebrand [53, Theorem 6.1]. The tree section above gives the following direct argument for this
application. For the conic claim let the logarithmic degree be θ. Lemma 8.9 gives a root-slice
parameter at most θ − 1. The first claim gives θ − 1 ≥ L. Logarithmic homogeneity is essential to
this last argument.

   Thus the intrinsic tree parameter lies between L and the value in (9.1). We do not identify
these endpoints for every tree. In particular, the exact standard-barrier formula does not assert
optimality among arbitrary barriers.
   One tempting correction already fails on the two-block comb. The function
                   Fe (t, x, a, y, z) = − log(t2 − x2 − a2 ) − log(a2 − y 2 − z 2 ) + log a
has logarithmic degree three. At the interior point (3, 0, 1, 0, 0), in direction (0, 0, 1, 1, 0), its
derivatives are D2 Fe = 53/16 and D3 Fe = −441/32. Their squared self-concordance inequality fails,
since (441/32)2 − 4(53/16)3 = 45604/1024 > 0. Removing one logarithmic axial term per link
therefore does not produce a valid lower-parameter barrier by this formula.

9.2   Grouped fixed-tail slices: coupling cannot improve the parameter
For each ball a, partition its coordinates into nonempty groups G ∈ Ga , and put H =               a |Ga |.

                                                                                     

    For comparison, the product of k positive-dimensional balls itself has intrinsic parameter k. The
upper bound follows by restricting the one-parameter paraboloid barriers to u = 1 and adding; the
lower bound follows from a k-dimensional box section. Consequently the grouped domain has an
intrinsic parameter excess H − k over its projection. Under a genuine Lorentz cap 3 ≤ d < N + 1, a
single ball uses h = ⌈N/(d − 2)⌉ groups. In the notation of Corollary 9.2, put ζ = 1{d−2|N −1} , so

Here L0 ≥ 2; when L0 = 2, χ = 0, and when L0 ≥ 3 the last inequality is immediate. The grouped
lift uses at most one additional factor, while its parameter can be asymptotically half the tree’s
standard parameter. If d ≥ N + 1, the direct Lorentz slice already has parameter one.
     The same box section also gives the lower bound H for uaG > ∥waG ∥pp , 1 < p < ∞, with the
same allocation equations. The matching upper bound just proved uses the quadratic case p = 2; it
is not asserted for other exponents.

9.3    Bounded-fiber projection and one-block column packing
We give precise projection hypotheses because a lower bound on the projected body is not transferred
by merely counting lift variables. The following is a form of the classical exact partial-minimization
theorem [22, Theorem 5.2.1].

Lemma 9.5 (Parameter monotonicity under bounded-fiber projection). Let Q ⊂ E × V be a closed
convex set with nonempty interior, and suppose every nonempty fiber Qx = {y : (x, y) ∈ Q} is
bounded. Suppose C = πE Q contains no line. If F is a self-concordant barrier on int Q with positive
definite Hessian and parameter ν, then

                                   ϕ(x) = min{F (x, y) : (x, y) ∈ int Q}

is a self-concordant barrier with positive definite Hessian and parameter at most ν on int C. The
same statement holds in affine hulls after eliminating affine equations. In particular, ϑopt (int Q) ≥
ϑopt (int C).


                                                      44
Proof. First, inverse images in Q of bounded subsets of E are bounded. Otherwise choose (xj , yj ) ∈ Q
with xj bounded and ∥yj ∥ → ∞. Normalize their differences from a fixed q0 ∈ Q and take a
subsequence converging to a vertical unit vector (0, v). Closedness and convexity imply q0 +t(0, v) ∈ Q
for every t ≥ 0, contradicting the bounded fiber through q0 . This argument also proves that C
is closed, by lifting a convergent sequence in C and extracting a subsequence. The standard
relative-interior identity for linear images of convex sets gives πE (int Q) = int C.
    For each interior x, the fiber is bounded and its boundary lies on ∂Q. Barrier divergence and
strict convexity therefore give a unique interior minimizer y(x). The equation Fy = 0 and the implicit-
function theorem show that y(x) is C 2 . For a direction h, set h    b = (h, Dy(x)h). Differentiating
stationarity gives Hessian orthogonality to every vertical direction, and hence

                               Dϕ[h] = DF [h],
                                           b

                           D2 ϕ[h, h] = D2 F [h, b = min D 2 F [(h, k), (h, k)],
                                              b h]

                         D ϕ[h, h, h] = D F [h,
                           3              3  b h,

                                                  b

In the third identity the acceleration term is vertical and its Hessian pairing with h
                                                                                     b vanishes. The
positive definite Schur complement proves nondegeneracy; the two defining derivative inequalities
for F prove the same inequalities for ϕ. Finally, as xj tends to a finite boundary point of C,
the minimizers remain bounded by the first paragraph. Any cluster point lies on ∂Q, since an
interior cluster point would project to an interior point of C. Barrier divergence therefore implies
ϕ(xj ) → +∞. This completes the proof.

  Let F ∈ {R, C, H}, let p, b ≥ 1, and choose nonzero real linear subspaces Ea ⊂ Fp . With

                 p+b

    DF [H, K] = − tr(D−1 H),         D2 F [(H, K), (H, K)] = tr(D−1 HD−1 H) + 2 tr(D−1 K ∗ K).

                                                   45
The squared dual norm of this gradient is b, attained by (H, K) = (−D, 0). Therefore this is a b-
self-concordant barrier; the diagonal and column-subspace restrictions cannot increase its parameter.
The lower bound proves equality.

Thus column packing reduces the ambient parameter p + b to b on the fixed slice, even when arbitrary
coupled barriers are allowed. In contrast, the grouped slice may retain many more than b units.
The one-factor count, the ambient rank, and the intrinsic slice parameter describe different costs.
Example 9.7 (Repeated boundary blocks do not bound arbitrary barriers). For m ≥ 2, the diagonal
affine slice
                      (1/m, x/m), . . . , (1/m, x/m) : x ∈ int Bs2 ⊂ Qm
                                                   
                                                                      s+1

is affinely isomorphic to one ball. All m blocks reach nonzero boundary points simultaneously, and
polynomial slack factors are Aj (x) = (1, x)/m, Bj (z) = (1, −z). The restricted standard product
barrier is −m log(1 − ∥x∥2 ) plus a constant, with exact parameter m; its squared dual gradient norm
approaches m as ∥x∥ → 1. Yet the slice admits the one-parameter barrier − log(1 − ∥x∥2 ). The
active conormals coincide after restriction. Consequently simultaneous boundary multiplicity alone
is insufficient for an arbitrary-barrier lower bound; the polyhedral sections or projection argument
above supply the additional geometry.
    These parameter statements concern self-concordant barrier certificates on specified formulations.
They do not assert that every algorithm takes a number of iterations proportional to the square root
of the parameter, and they do not bound the conditioning or the arithmetic cost of a Newton solve.


10     Topology of saturated contact factorizations
The support-orbit argument has a counterpart for arbitrary proper cones. Here the relevant targets
are the boundaries of compact cone bases. Saturation forces these initially nonsmooth boundaries
to acquire smooth charts. This gives a joint covering theorem without a symmetry assumption on
the cones.

10.1    Normalized cone bases and the joint covering
Let P, D be compact connected C 1 manifolds without boundary, both of dimension n ≥ 1. Suppose

Proof. At each contact every individual cone pairing is zero. Its fixed-variable first derivatives are
therefore zero:
                      DAi (Tx P ) ⊂ Bi (ζ(x))⊥ ,   DBi (Tζ(x) D) ⊂ Ai (x)⊥ .                    (10.2)
Lemma 3.1 applies with the two tangent charts independent. Nondegeneracy and rank subadditivity
force every mixed channel to have rank ci . For ci > 0 this excludes both vertices, because a two-sided
derivative of a map into a pointed cone is zero at the vertex. Complementarity puts pi (x) in Ji .
    For a = Ai (x) and b = Bi (ζ(x)), the channel factors through b⊥ /Ra, a space of dimension ci .
The quotient derivative has rank ci , since the channel does. Direct differentiation shows

                            Dpi (x)[v] = 0      ⇐⇒          DAi (x)[v] ∈ RAi (x).

Thus Dpi has constant rank ci . The base boundary Ji is initially only a topological sphere. The
constant-rank theorem supplies a local ci -dimensional transversal on which pi is a regular embedding.
Invariance of domain makes this embedded patch relatively open in Ji . Consequently pi (P ) is
open and compact in the connected Ji , and is all of Ji . The regular patches give its C 1 embedded
structure. Radial projection from an interior point of the base is a C 1 diffeomorphism to Sci : a
supporting normal has strictly positive pairing with the radial direction, so that direction is never
tangent.
    If v ∈ i∈I+ ker Dpi , the preceding kernel formula and (10.2) give Hx (v, w) = 0 for every w;
            T

zero-capacity channels also vanish. Hence v = 0. Source and target of p have equal dimensions, so p
is a local diffeomorphism. Compactness and connectedness make it a surjective finite covering.
    If P is simply connected, a submersion pi : P → S1 would lift to a real-valued function, whose
maximum contradicts submersivity. Thus the target is simply connected and the cover has one
sheet. A product of two positive-dimensional spheres has intermediate cohomology, which proves the
single-sphere assertion. More generally, the graded indecomposable quotient H + /(H + )2 of rational
cohomology has one generator in the dimension of each sphere. Comparing this quotient on the
source and target proves the multiset assertion.

   For a convex body with C 1 primal and polar boundaries, the independent tangent spaces at a
contact are y ⊥ and x⊥ , where ⟨x, y⟩ = 1. Their Euclidean pairing is nondegenerate: its first-slot
kernel would lie in y ⊥ ∩ Rx = {0}. Thus the theorem applies without differentiating the supporting-
polarity map. It also applies to weighted product-ball contact kernels. The resulting covering
need not split as a product of maps depending on individual source coordinates. Nor does this
theorem identify an arbitrary saturated cone as Lorentz; it identifies its normalized boundary and
the capacity profile.

                                                       47
Corollary 10.2 (Smooth ball frontier for arbitrary proper cones). Let N ≥ 3 and let d ≥ 3 be an
integer. Allow arbitrary proper cone factors of dimension at most d. Among exact ball lifts with
global C 1 primal and genuine dual selections, the simultaneous minimum number of factors, total
cone dimension, and parameter of a barrier on the full ambient cone product are

                          (h, N + 2h, 2h),       h = ⌈N/(d − 2)⌉,           d < N + 1,
                         (

                          (1, N + 1, 2),                                    d ≥ N + 1.

The ambient-parameter conclusion includes coupled barriers, and hence also coupled logarithmically
homogeneous barriers.

Proof. If d < N + 1, every positive capacity is smaller than N − 1. Theorem 10.1 excludes equality
in the local bound, so their sum is at least N . If there are k such factors, k ≥ h and their dimensions
sum to at least N + 2k. Every proper cone of dimension at least two has a two-dimensional linear
section through its interior that is isomorphic to R2+ . Restricting any ambient barrier to the product
of these sections gives an orthant barrier, whose parameter is at least 2k [70, Proposition 2.3.6].
Extra factors cannot reduce these bounds. For d ≥ N + 1, the local bound already gives total
dimension at least N + 1, and the same section argument gives parameter at least two. The grouped
Lorentz lifts in Theorem 6.2, and the direct Lorentz lift in the second case, attain all bounds.

   The following local form is useful for cover arguments involving changing active labels. It also
explains why nonsmooth cone boundaries do not remove the topological obstruction.

Lemma 10.3 (Proper normalized-phase domains). Let p ≥ 2, let 0 < ci < p with i ci = p, and let
                                                                                               P

Ji be the boundaries of compact convex bodies of dimensions ci + 1. Suppose U ⊂ Sp is open and
qi : U → Ji are C 1 as ambient maps. If the combined derivative of q = (qi )i is injective everywhere,
then U ≠ Sp . In particular the top class of Sp restricts to zero on U . The same holds on the inverse
image of U under any coordinate projection from a product of spheres.

Proof. A map into Ji has derivative rank at most ci : rank ci + 1 would make its image contain an
ambient open set. Therefore joint injectivity forces each component to have rank ci . The transversal
and invariance-of-domain argument above gives regular charts on their images. If U were the whole
sphere, every image would be all of Ji and q would be a finite covering of their product. A circle
factor contradicts simple connectivity and finite covering degree; without circle factors, the product
is simply connected and its intermediate cohomology contradicts that of the sphere. Finally every
proper open subset of a connected closed oriented manifold has zero ordinary top cohomology, by
noncompact-manifold duality, component by component [48, Section 3.3].

The single-ball support-orbit obstruction has a stronger product form. Equality forces one spin

the joint support map and the fact that each dual row depends on only one source coordinate.

    Every positive-degree class in the mod-two cohomology of a product of spheres has square
zero. In a complex Grassmannian of order at least three, the degree-two Schubert class instead
satisfies σ12 = σ2 + σ1,1 ̸= 0, omitting terms outside its rectangle. The same relation holds with
degrees doubled for quaternionic Grassmannians; see [79, Section 2] for the Schubert ring and its
quaternionic counterpart. These spaces are simply connected, so their square obstruction persists
in the universal cover. For the Albert orbit, H ∗ (OP 2 ; F2 ) = F2 [u]/(u3 ), |u| = 8, gives the same
obstruction [48, Example 4.47].

Lemma 11.3 (The two real exceptions violate the row identities). Under the hypotheses of
Lemma 11.2, neither S3+ nor S4+ occurs.
Proof. Fix a row d at contact and let W = Ran Yid (xd ) in a real matrix block. Complementarity
along the entire cylinder gives
                               W ⊆ ker Xi (x)       whenever xd is fixed.                           (11.3)
Suppose first that i is an order-four block. Its primal support has rank two, and the two S 2 rulings
of its oriented Grassmannian are assigned to distinct source factors a, b. If W ̸= 0 for a row d ̸= a,
vary only xa . If dim W = 2, the primal plane is constant. If dim W = 1, the plane lies in W ⊥ , a
fixed three-space. The inclusion
                                     ⊥ ∼ 2           4 ∼ 2
                               Gr+
                                 2 (W ) = S −→ Gr2 (R ) = S+ × S−
                                                 +              2



                                                    50
has degree ±1 in both projections. To see this, represent an oriented plane by a unit simple bivector
and take its self-dual and anti-self-dual parts. For planes in a fixed three-space, the two parts are
related by an orthogonal map. Every map factoring through this inclusion therefore has its two
degrees equal up to sign. This contradicts the degrees (±1, 0) on the assigned a-cylinder. Thus
Yid ≡ 0 for every d ̸= a. Applying the same argument to the assigned b-cylinder makes all rows zero,
contradicting the positive complementary dual rank. No order-four block remains.
    For an order-three block assigned to source a, the primal rank is one or two. A nonzero W
for d =
      ̸ a either fixes its kernel line (primal rank two), or confines its range line to RP(W ⊥ ) of
dimension at most one (primal rank one). The lifted map on the assigned S 2 then has degree zero,
contradicting Lemma 11.2. Hence this block serves only row a.
    We next show that no other non-ray block serves row a. Every other order-three block has a
distinct assigned S 2 , so the preceding argument applies. Each remaining block is spin. Its sphere
target can be assigned to a source sphere of the same dimension with nonzero restriction degree:
in each degree at least two choose a nonzero term in the determinant of the invertible matrix on
cohomological indecomposables of the universal covers. For circles use the invertible rational matrix
induced on fundamental groups. These assignments are bijections; the degree-two assignments agree
with the signed permutation already proved, so none of these blocks is assigned to a. Fixing row a
and varying such an assigned source, a nonzero dual factor would force the spin primal support to
be constant. Indeed, a rank-two cone has only one primitive idempotent orthogonal to any fixed
nonzero complementary element. This contradicts its nonzero degree or winding. All other non-ray
factors in row a vanish.
    Fix all primal coordinates except xa . Row a is now a factorization of the S 2 ball slack through
one S3+ block and finitely many scalar rays. At each contact the ray mixed derivatives vanish, so
this block carries the full two-dimensional mixed metric. Its projective support map is a local
diffeomorphism and its ranks are complementary one and two. Proposition 7.5 excludes precisely
this factorization, exchanging its two arguments if necessary. Thus no order-three block remains
either.

Proof of Theorem 11.1. Lemma 11.2 gives the capacity inequality and covering. Its remaining con-
clusions and Lemma 11.3 leave only spin factors. The universal-cover cohomology and fundamental-
group counts give exactly one target sphere for each source sphere, of the same dimension. The
determinant assignment in the preceding proof matches each target to one source with nonzero
restriction degree or winding. No splitting of the covering as a product map is required.
    Let block i be assigned to source a, and fix a different row d. If Yid (xd ) ̸= 0, complementarity
makes the primitive support of Xi (x) constant as xa varies, by the uniqueness of the complementary
primitive ray in a spin cone. Its assigned degree would then be zero. Hence Yid ≡ 0 for d ̸= a, as
asserted. The direct separate Lorentz representations give the converse. All capacities are integers,
so failure of the equality profile gives the stated unit gap.

    When the scalar ray summands vanish identically, there is a shorter additional observation: the
joint support map at equality is injective. If two points have the same primal supports, the dual
rows at either point annihilate the primal factors at the other, and the full row identities force their
every coordinate to agree. Scalar rays prevent this argument, which is why the proof above retains
a covering until it has used the cylinder identities.
    For Hermitian factors over a fixed field, the theorem permits equality only with order-two
blocks and source sphere dimensions respectively 1, 2, 4 over R, C, H. Over all symmetric cones,
arbitrary spin sizes are allowed, but a cap mi ≤ d makes equality impossible if some source ball
has pa + 2 > d. These are statements about globally C 1 full-row factorizations. They do not


                                                  51
impose the same equality classification on lifts whose selections exist only on generic patches. The
Grassmannian topology and its cohomology are classical; the contribution here is their use with
the full row identities to classify capacity equality and exclude cross-row sharing, including finite
scalar ray terms. Aubrun, La Piana, and Müller-Hermes [6, Theorem 1] classify cones for which
every positive linear self-map factors through a finite direct sum of Lorentz cones. Their universal
quantifier over positive linear maps differs from the hypothesis here, which concerns one globally C 1
full-row slack factorization at equality in its mixed-curvature capacity. Our theorem does not assert
their Lorentz-factorization property for general cones.


12     Rigidity from entire boundary fibers
Global regularity of a chosen contact sheet is a substantive hypothesis. There is, however, a different
route to a global support map: constant nullity on every point of every boundary fiber makes that
map intrinsic to the projection. This section gives the resulting product rigidity theorem. The
hypothesis concerns the whole fiber, including points that no chosen regular sheet reaches.

Theorem 12.1 (Constant-nullity rigidity). Let C = ha=1 Bsa , where sa ≥ 2, and put M = a Spa ,
                                                               Q                                 Q

                                    E = {X ∈ L ∩ K : π(X) ∈ M }

                   n = L(d − 2),       Z(X) :=         ri − rankJ Xi = L              (X ∈ E),   (12.1)

                                {pa : 1 ≤ a ≤ h} = {d − 2, . . . , d − 2}                        (12.2)

as multisets. Exactly L cone factors have positive nullity on E; each is Qd and has nullity one. All

Proof. Projection E → M is a continuous surjection with convex, hence connected, fibers. It follows
that E is connected. Indeed, a separation of the compact set E into two nonempty clopen subsets
would give two disjoint compact images covering M : a connected fiber must lie on one side of the
separation. This contradicts connectedness of M .
    Each nullity zi (X) = ri − rankJ Xi is upper semicontinuous and integer-valued. Near any X ∈ E,
all zi are at most their values at X. Their constant sum forces equality term by term. Thus each zi
is locally constant, and connectedness makes it constant on E. If X, X ′ belong to one fiber, their
midpoint belongs to the same fiber. In a symmetric cone the support of a positive sum is the join of
the supports. Equality of the three ranks therefore implies

                                   supp Xi = supp Xi′              for every i.

                                                      52
The support map is continuous on a fixed-rank stratum. Since a continuous surjection from a
compact space to a Hausdorff space is a quotient map, these support maps descend to continuous
maps on M .
    Next choose primal and genuine pure-row dual selections on a common dense open semialgebraic
contact set; Lemma 2.2 supplies such selections with locally constant ranks. Summing the pure rows
gives the joint kernel a (1 − ⟨xa , va ⟩), whose mixed contact form is the product metric of rank n.

Equality throughout (12.3) implies that rays have zero nullity and every positive-nullity non-ray

primal coordinates; complementarity forces their dual coordinate to vanish for every genuine pure
supporting row at every point of M .
    The descended supports in the active Lorentz factors define

                                                   Σ : M −→ (Sd−2 )L .

This map is injective. If Σ(x) = Σ(x′ ), take any genuine certificate for the row 1 − ⟨πa (X), xa ⟩ and
evaluate it at any lift over x′ . Equal active support rays make every active pairing zero, and all
other dual coordinates are zero. Hence 1 − ⟨x′a , xa ⟩ = 0 for every a, so x′ = x. Source and target
have equal dimension. Invariance of domain makes the image open, and compactness makes it
closed. The target is connected; thus Σ is a homeomorphism. In the mod-two cohomology ring of a
product of positive-dimensional spheres, the quotient of the positive-degree ideal by its square has
one generator in each sphere dimension, with multiplicity. A graded ring isomorphism therefore
gives (12.2), including any degree-one generators. Conversely, under that profile the lift (1, xa ) ∈ Qd
has compact singleton fibers and one unit of nullity per source, as asserted.

    For a single s-dimensional ball with d < s + 1 and s − 1 = L(d − 2), Theorem 12.1 is an
impossibility statement. For products it is a sharp classification: equality permits only the profile
of separate direct factors. This conclusion requires neither a continuous chosen primal lift nor a
continuous chosen dual certificate.
Definition 12.2 (Accessibility by regular contacts). A point X ∈ E is contact-accessible if there
are contact points x(j) → π(X) and local C 1 primal and genuine pure-row dual contact selections
near x(j) , on constant-rank strata, whose primal values X (j) satisfy X (j) → X.
Corollary 12.3 (Accessible equality cases). Retain the lift and compactness assumptions of
Theorem 12.1 and suppose n = L(d − 2). If every X ∈ E is contact-accessible and the restricted
standard barrier satisfies νstd,slice ≤ L, then the profile (12.2) is necessary.

                                                               53
Proof. Lemma 8.1 gives Z(X) ≤ νstd,slice ≤ L at every X ∈ E. At each approximating regular contact
the curvature chain (12.3), without its final equalities, gives Z(X (j) ) ≥ L. Upper semicontinuity then
yields Z(X) ≥ lim supj Z(X (j) ) ≥ L. Thus Z = L throughout E, and Theorem 12.1 applies.

    A standard-barrier bound alone supplies the upper bound on nullity, not accessibility or the
reverse inequality at projection-singular fiber points. A single global section likewise controls only
its own image. The distinction is essential: the unrestricted barrier brackets in Section 8 are not
closed by this corollary.




                                                  54
Part III
Formulation families and barrier comparisons
13     Whole rows, minimum dimension, and affine normalization
The differential bounds above permit one slack row to use many factors. A different resource law
holds when an entire family of support rows must be assigned to one factor. In that model, ordinary
function rank and the dimension of a contact face give simultaneous sharp bounds without any
regularity assumption. We first derive this law, then separate two conclusions that remain valid
when rows may split: rigidity at minimum total dimension, and a dimension bound for projections
of a single normalized product-cone base.

13.1    Contact faces and exact grouping
Let Ca ⊂ Rna be compact full-dimensional convex bodies with 0 ∈ int Ca , for a ∈ [k]. Write

                 Ea = ext Ca ,       Pa = ext Ca◦ ,        Fa (z) = {x ∈ Ca : ⟨x, z⟩ = 1}.

Define the maximum contact-face dimension and its codimension by

                                 ea = max dim Fa (z),         λa = na − ea .
                                      z∈Pa

Here face dimension is affine dimension, so 1 ≤ λa ≤ na . The maximum exists because the nonempty
set of possible dimensions is a finite set of integers. For a proper cone K define

                                   f (K) = max ∗ dim span(K ∩ y ⊥ ).
                                             0̸=y∈K

Thus f (K) counts linear dimensions of proper exposed cone faces; the zero face is allowed.

Every contact face for an arbitrary normalized boundary normal is the intersection of contact faces

    By a whole-row partition we mean a partition G of [k] with one cone assigned to each group,
factoring all of that group’s rows as in (13.1). In particular, a row is not split between different
assigned cones. Allowing a group’s primal map to depend on other source coordinates does not
weaken the lower bound: fix those coordinates and apply the theorem.

Proposition 13.3 (Complexity of heterogeneous grouping). For Euclidean balls with dimensions
given as positive integers, deciding whether the whole-row grouping problem has a partition with at
most a specified number of groups is strongly NP-complete. This holds already when F = D − 1.

Proof. Under F = D−1, the second inequality in (13.5) follows from the first because mina∈G na ≥ 1.
Consider a 3-PARTITION instance with integers b1 , . . . , b3q , B/4 < ba < B/2, and a ba = qB
                                                                                          P

[40, Problem SP15]. Set na = ba , D = B + 1, F = B, and ask for at most q groups. Their total
dimension is qB, so every feasible group must have dimension exactly B and there must be exactly
q groups. The size inequalities force exactly three items in each group. This is precisely the original
3-PARTITION question. The reduction preserves polynomial bounds on all integers; if explicit
Euclidean-ball descriptions are required, their total coordinate count is polynomial on those strongly
bounded instances. A partition is a polynomially checkable certificate for the two integer inequalities,
which also proves membership in NP. The computational obstruction here is the classical packing
problem, rather than an additional difficulty in testing a fixed group’s conic feasibility.

13.2    Rigidity at the absolute dimension minimum
The following elementary equality argument applies to arbitrary compact bodies and allows every row
to split. It is useful because a cone change at this minimum cannot improve any linear-isomorphism
invariant.

Theorem 13.4 (Minimum-dimensional rigidity). Let C ⊂ RN , N ≥ 1, be a compact full-dimensional
convex body. Every exact lift over a proper cone of total dimension M , allowing affine slices, affine
projections, and free variables, has M ≥ N + 1. At equality, after the reduction of Lemma 2.1, an
invertible linear map carries the cone to

                                  hom(C) = {(t, tx) : t ≥ 0, x ∈ C}

and its feasible slice to {1} × C. The reduced projection is an affine isomorphism on that slice.
    The same cone-isomorphism conclusion holds for any factorization of the full slack operator
through a proper cone of dimension N + 1, without regularity assumptions on its factor maps. In
particular, it holds when that cone is a product and rows split across its factors.

Proof. First consider an exact lift. Eliminate free variables and reduce to the minimal face, of
dimension M ′ ≤ M , with a Slater affine slice A. Its affine output has full rank on the direction
space of A, so dim A ≥ N . If M ′ ≤ N , these facts force A to be the entire cone space. Its image is
an affine translate of a cone, which cannot be a full-dimensional bounded body. Thus M ′ ≥ N + 1.
   When M = N + 1, no face reduction is possible, and A must be an N -dimensional affine
hyperplane: the full-space case gives the same contradiction. The output map on A is an affine


                                                  57
isomorphism, so S = K ∩ A is compact. The hyperplane does not contain zero, again because
otherwise its feasible set and output would be translated cones. Write A = {z : ℓ(z) = 1}. Fix
w ∈ A ∩ int K. If nonzero v ∈ K satisfies ℓ(v) = 0, then w + tv ∈ S for all t ≥ 0, contradicting
compactness. If ℓ(v) < 0, the nonzero vector v − ℓ(v)w ∈ K ∩ ker ℓ gives the same contradiction;
pointedness ensures this vector is nonzero. Therefore ℓ is strictly positive on K \ {0}, and S is
a compact base. The affine output on A extends to a linear map π     e on the cone space because
A is a hyperplane not containing zero. The map T z = (ℓ(z), π (z)) is invertible: its kernel lies in
                                                               e
the direction space of A, where the output is injective. Writing each nonzero z ∈ K uniquely as
z = ℓ(z)s, s ∈ S, proves both claims.
    For the factorization assertion, translate C so 0 ∈ int C. The canonical vectors U (x) = (1, x)
for x ∈ ext C and V (z) = (1, −z) for z ∈ ext C ◦ each span RN +1 . The slack has rank N + 1, by
the affine-function argument in Theorem 13.1. If A(x), B(z) give another factorization in that
dimension, their images span as well. A linear relation among the U (x) is equivalent, by pairing
with the spanning dual family, to the same relation among the A(x). Hence an invertible T satisfies
A = T U and B = T −T V . Taking conic hulls gives

                              T hom(C) ⊆ K,         T −T hom(C)∗ ⊆ K ∗ .

The extreme points of the body and its polar generate their respective compact bases, so these
are the full homogenization cones. Dualizing the second inclusion reverses the first, and proves
equality.

    In Theorem 13.1, the labelled rows are exactly the extreme polar rows of a∈G Ca . To see this,
                                                                                  Q

its polar is {(za ) : a ha (za ) ≤ 1}. A nonzero extreme point must have one supported coordinate
                     P

block, since two positive support-function contributions give a nontrivial convex decomposition
into single-block points. Within that block it must be an extreme point of Ca◦ . Conversely each
such single-block point is extreme. Thus equality m = NG + 1 in that theorem forces K to be
linearly isomorphic to HG . For a fixed partition into g groups, every whole-row formulation of
total dimension a na + g therefore uses precisely those shared homogenization cones up to linear
                   P

changes of coordinates. Barrier parameters are preserved by such changes. In particular, the exact
shared spectral-cone values of Theorem 17.3, including its Euclidean vector-ball case, apply to every
such minimum-dimensional formulation.

Corollary 13.5 (Connected extreme sets under a strict factor cap). Suppose ext C is connected.
A full slack factorization through K = L
                                      Q
                                       i=1 Ki , with every Ki nonzero and proper, can have total
dimension N + 1 only when L = 1. Consequently, if dim Ki ≤ d < N + 1, then
                                                                  N +2
                                                                       
                              M ≥ N + 2,        L ≥ max 2,                    .                   (13.6)
                                                                    d
These conclusions also hold for exact lifts, after the stated reduction convention, and permit arbitrary
row splitting.

Proof. Normalize extreme rays by any strictly positive linear functional. For a product of L nonzero
proper cones, the extreme rays are supported in exactly one factor; the L factor-supported pieces
are nonempty and both open and closed in the normalized extreme-ray space. For hom(C) that
space is homeomorphic to ext C. Theorem 13.4 therefore excludes L > 1 at equality. A single factor
under the strict cap cannot supply dimension N + 1. Integrality gives M ≥ N + 2, and M ≤ Ld
gives the count bound. For an unreduced exact lift with M = N + 1, the theorem already ensures
that no reduction occurs.

                                                  58
    This is a one-unit exclusion of the absolute minimum, not an additive charge per factor. The
balance slices in Theorem 15.1 have connected extreme sets and M = N + 2 for arbitrarily many
essential factors. They therefore preclude the stronger general claim M ≥ N + L.

13.3    What one normalization equation does imply

Proof. Let Bi be the compact normalized base of factor i, embedded in the product with all other
coordinates zero, and put Ci = π(Bi ). Then C = conv i Ci , and dim aff Ci ≤ mi − 1. Every
                                                           S

extreme point of C belongs to some Ci , because any finite convex representation of an extreme

   An additional affine equation can create extreme points with several factor coordinates active.
The factor-base cover in this proof then need not cover the extreme points of the smaller slice. This
explains why the proposition and the balance-slice counterexamples are compatible.

13.4    Recession normalization and barrier transfer
The projection lemma in Section 9 requires bounded fibers. The next criterion says precisely when
a single affine level can provide that hypothesis. All interiors in the statement are relative to affine
hulls.
Proposition 13.7 (A criterion for barrier-preserving normalization). Let D be a nonempty closed
convex set containing no affine line, and let an affine map π satisfy π(D) = C, where C is a compact
convex body. Write R = rec D for its recession cone. If R ̸= {0}, choose a linear functional a strictly
positive on R \ {0}, and define
                               ma (x) = min{a(z) : z ∈ D, π(z) = x}.
Every minimum is finite and attained. For any real M ,
                          π(D ∩ {a = M }) = C            ⇐⇒         M ≥ sup ma (x).                          (13.7)
                                                                          x∈C

Whenever this holds, the level section is compact. If the supremum is finite, every self-concordant
barrier on ri D induces a barrier on int C with no larger parameter. If R = {0}, the same parameter
conclusion holds without taking a level section.

                                                       59
Proof. Compactness of C implies R ⊆ ker π0 , where π0 is the linear part of π. The cone R is pointed
because D contains no line, so a strictly positive a exists. Every nonempty sublevel D ∩ {a ≤ b} is
bounded. Otherwise normalize differences of an unbounded sequence from a fixed point of D and
pass to a unit limit r. Closedness and convexity imply r ∈ R, while boundedness above of a on the
sequence gives a(r) ≤ 0, a contradiction. Each fiber is closed; minimizing a on a compact nonempty
sublevel proves attainment and finiteness of ma (x).
    Fix 0 ̸= r0 ∈ R. Adding a nonnegative multiple of r0 to a minimizer in a fiber preserves that
fiber and reaches every larger a-value. This proves (13.7). The level section is closed and bounded
by the same sublevel argument. If the supremum is finite, choose M greater than it and greater
than a(z 0 ) for some z 0 ∈ ri D. Adding a suitable multiple of r0 to z 0 gives a point of the level in
ri D. Indeed, a relative ball around z 0 remains in D after translating by a recession direction. Thus
the affine restriction of the original barrier is a barrier on the relative interior of the compact level
section, with the same parameter bound. Lemma 9.5 then gives a barrier on C. If R = {0}, the
closed convex set D is bounded by the same normalized-sequence argument, and that lemma applies
directly.

Corollary 13.8 (Arbitrary line-free lifts of a simple-vertex polytope). Let C be an N -dimensional
compact polytope with a simple vertex. Every self-concordant barrier on the relative interior of
every closed line-free affine lift of C has parameter at least N , even if its fibers are unbounded. The
minimum over all such lifts and barriers is N .

Proof. Choose a lift zv ∈ D of each vertex v of C. For a convex combination x =          v θv v, its lifted
                                                                                       P

combination belongs to D and therefore

                                  ma (x) ≤       θv a(zv ) ≤ max a(zv ).
                                             X
                                                              v
                                             v

Proposition 13.7 transfers the barrier to C without increasing its parameter. At a simple vertex, the
classical independent-facet lower bound gives ν ≥ N [70, Proposition 2.3.6]. The universal barrier
on C attains the bound N [60, Theorem 2]; it may also be used on the canonical homogenization
slice, which is affinely isomorphic to C.

    The simple-vertex hypothesis is essential to this argument. Choosing N independent normals
at a nonsimple vertex does not reproduce its tangent cone. At e1 of the three-dimensional cross-
polytope, the four active normals are (1, ±1, ±1). The three with sign pairs (+, +), (+, −), (−, +)
are independent, but (−1, −1, −1) satisfies their three strict tangent inequalities and violates the
fourth. The cited simple-vertex lower bound cannot be applied after deleting that facet.

Example 13.9 (Compact projection need not admit affine normalization). Let D be the positive-
semidefinite lift of the disk in Example 8.8, with coordinates (x1 , x2 , u, z). Its recession cone is
exactly the positive z-ray. Indeed, compactness of the projected disk makes every recession direction
have zero x-coordinates. The second block then requires its u-coordinate to be nonnegative, while
the last homogeneous block ( u̇0 u̇ż ) forces u̇ = 0 and ż ≥ 0. Conversely every such direction is a
recession direction. If an affine subspace removed this ray from a nonempty section of D, that closed
convex section would have zero recession cone and hence be bounded. It could not project onto
the entire disk, since every completion of the boundary points approaching (1, 0) in Example 8.8
has z → ∞. Thus no projection-preserving affine restriction removes the recession ray, even for
this semialgebraic Slater lift over three 2 × 2 positive-semidefinite cones. This obstructs automatic
normalization; it does not disprove parameter monotonicity by some other method for general lifts
with unbounded fibers.


                                                    60
    The full-slack framework used in this section is classical [43]. The minimum-dimension statement
is an elementary rigidity consequence of that framework, and the normalized-base dimension bound
uses only a finite connected cover. The barrier endpoints in Corollary 13.8 are also classical; its
additional content is their transfer through a given closed line-free lift, by the explicit recession-level
criterion and bounded partial minimization. The whole-row grouping formulas concern a restricted
formulation model and do not assert optimality over arbitrary split-row lifts.


14      Complementary faces control sharing between source rows
The whole-row results constrain an indivisible row. We now allow every row to split over several
blocks, but retain globally C 1 factors of the full slack. The dimension of an exposed face then gives
a different, differential limit on sharing. Its global consequence extends the Lorentz no-sharing
theorem to every cone whose nonzero proper exposed faces are rays; smoothness of either cone
boundary is unnecessary.
    Let M = (Sp )k , p = s − 1 ≥ 2, and suppose
                                           L
                       1 − ⟨xa , z⟩ =            ⟨Ai (x), Bia (z)⟩,         x ∈ M, z ∈ Sp , a ∈ [k],                (14.1)

             ri = mi − 2,          c = d − 2,       fi =        max       dim(Ki ∩ b⊥ ),      f¯i = min(fi , k).

                                                           Ki ∩b⊥ ̸={0}

The maximum concerns dimensions, not the number of extreme rays. One may instead use any
upper bound on the dimensions of the nonzero exposed faces encountered by the selected dual
factors. Ray factors can be added: their mixed forms vanish and they contribute nothing to the
bounds below.

Theorem 14.1 (Local face packing). At x ∈ M put

           Hia (u, v) = −⟨Da Ai (x)[u], DBia (xa )[v]⟩,                   tai = rank Hia ,    Ii = {a : tai > 0}.

In particular |Ii | ≤ f¯i .

    Suppose c < p. If i f¯i ri < k(p + 1), every point belongs to some Za = {Ca = p}. Each Za is
                        P

closed because activity is open and Ca ≥ p everywhere. Its finitely many exact-active-label pieces
Ea,J are clopen in Za , hence compact: existing active labels persist, whereas a new label would raise

                                                                   62
Ca . On such a piece i∈J ri = p. Normalize the nonzero dual factors Bia using an interior primal
                      P

vector to obtain maps into the boundaries of compact dual-cone bases. These maps are defined near
the projection of Ea,J onto its source sphere. Indeed fix the other source coordinates at any point
of the piece; nearby contact primal factors remain nonzero and force the corresponding dual factors
to remain on the nonzero boundary.
    The joint derivative of these normalized dual maps is injective at every projected point. If a
tangent vector is killed by all normalized derivatives, each unnormalized derivative is radial and
therefore annihilates every primal contact derivative. The identity i Hia = gSp then kills that
                                                                        P

tangent vector itself. Injectivity persists on an open neighborhood. Lemma 10.3 shows that this
neighborhood is a proper open subset of Sp , since every ri < p and their sum is p.
    For a fixed a, choose pairwise disjoint open neighborhoods of the finitely many compact pieces
Ea,J , each inside the inverse image of its proper source neighborhood. The source top class ua
vanishes on their union Ua . The Ua cover M , so the relative cup-product argument of Section 6
contradicts u1 · · · uk ̸= 0. Thus the capacity budget is at least k(p + 1). For the count budget, if
c ∤ p the pointwise count already equals h. Otherwise write p = tc. A putative incidence budget
below k(t + 1) covers M by loci where some source has exactly t active labels. Every label there
must have capacity c, and the same compact-piece argument applies. This proves the count bound.
    Uniform face caps and R ≥ K imply R ≥ R∗ and L ≥ ⌈kh/f ⌉. Since R ≤ cL, they also imply
L ≥ ⌈R∗ /c⌉. The dimension bound follows from D = R + 2L. Finally each proper cone of dimension
at least two has a two-dimensional linear section through an interior point that is a pointed wedge.
Its relative boundary lies in the cone boundary. The product sections form a 2L-orthant. Restriction
of any ambient barrier and the orthant lower bound give ν ≥ 2L ≥ 2L∗ .

need not contain these attaining cones. No exact interpolation for f > 1 is asserted.

14.1    A sharp model for shared scalar channels
The distinction between whole rows and split rows is concrete. Define
                     Pq = {(t, u, w) ∈ R × Rq × Rq : t ≥ 0, wa ≥ 0, twa ≥ u2a }.
This closed proper cone has dimension 2q + 1. The polynomial factors
                          A(v) = (1, v, v ◦2 ),       B a (z) = (z 2 /2, −zea , ea /2)              (14.6)
give ⟨A(v), B a (z)⟩ = (va − z)2 /2 on [−1, 1]q × [−1, 1]. Dual feasibility follows directly by completing
a square when t > 0 and by continuity when t = 0.

      / J remain, giving dimension q − |J|. These cases give the maximum 2q − 1, including q = 1.
    The row functions span the linearly independent family 1, va , va2 of size 2q + 1, proving the
dimension lower bound. On a contact cylinder va = z, the other rows linearly recover 1, vb , vb2 for

    The cone Pq is the PSD-completable partial-matrix cone on a star: the center diagonal is t, its

                    Q

is (14.7), of parameter q + 1. Equivalently it is the Legendre barrier of the sparse arrow-matrix log

1 − ⟨xa , z⟩ on the unit spheres. Summing dimensions and parameters gives the stated ledger; the

    For comparison the rank-one real PSD factors A(v) = (1, v)(1, v)T and B a (z) = (ea − ze0 )(ea −
ze0 )T /2 give the same scalar kernels, but expose a PSD face of dimension q(q + 1)/2. A whole-row
version uses v = (1, x1 , . . . , xq ) and sums those dual outer products over the s coordinates of a row;
its dual rank is s, its nullity is 1 + (q − 1)s, and its contact face has dimension 2+(q−1)s   . These are
                                                                                             
                                                                                         2
constructions, not optimality claims among homogeneous cones.
    The homogenization of a group of q whole ball rows, by contrast, has dimension qs + 1 and
maximum exposed-face dimension 1 + (q − 1)s by Theorem 13.1. Its exact ambient parameter
is q + 1, as the vector-row specialization of Theorem 17.3. For k rows in g groups the ambient
parameter is k + g, while fixing each scale to one gives exactly − a log(1 − ∥xa ∥2 ) with intrinsic
                                                                         P

parameter k, independently of grouping. Thus factor sharing, face efficiency, and the barrier on the
actual slice measure different features of the formulation.
    The correspondence between full slack factorizations and cone lifts is classical [43]. The resource
bounds isolated here are the cross-contact direct-sum inequalities (14.2) and their conversion into
the global budgets (14.3); their hypotheses explicitly include a global C 1 choice for every labelled
row. The barriers used in the examples are classical spectral and PSD-completion barriers, not new
barrier constructions. In particular, neither local face budgets nor a favorable ambient count alone
proves an unrestricted algorithmic speedup.

15     Balance slices with sharp dimension and barrier bounds
Connected extreme points force an extra cone coordinate under a strict dimension cap, but they
do not force one extra coordinate per factor. We construct slices attaining the former bound for
arbitrarily many factors. Their intrinsic barrier parameters are also exact. This gives an explicit
separation between dimension resources and barrier resources.

Theorem 15.1 (Balance slices). The set Z is a compact convex body of dimension M − 2, with a
path-connected extreme-point set. Each displayed factor is indecomposable, its membership constraint
is essential, and it occurs in a nonconstant supporting slack. For arbitrary self-concordant barriers,
including barriers coupling all blocks,

                                   ϑopt (ri Z)
                                            b = ρ,            ϑopt (ri Z) = ρ − 1.                            (15.2)

The product Jordan log-determinant, restricted to these domains, attains both values. Every closed
convex affine lift of Z with nonempty bounded fibers has intrinsic barrier parameter at least ρ − 1.
The conclusions include heterogeneous mixtures of classical Hermitian, spin, and Albert factors.

    The elementary minimal-face criterion used below is classical in finite dimensions; see also
the general injectivity formulation of Henrion, Kružík, and Weis [52, Theorem 2.6]. The ambient
symmetric-cone barrier theory is standard [31, 47]. The content of the theorem is the combination
of the explicit balance construction, connected extreme points, and exact parameters on its two
sections, rather than a new general extremality or barrier theorem.

Proof. The point Xi0 = ei /ρ is strictly feasible. The total trace and balance functionals are
independent, so dim Z = M − 2. Positivity and fixed total trace imply compactness.
   We first classify and connect the extreme points. If a point X belongs to an affine section
{X ∈ K : AX = b}, it is extreme precisely when

                                             ker A ∩ span FK (X) = {0}.                                       (15.3)

Indeed, X is relatively interior to its minimal face, so a nonzero vector in this intersection gives a
feasible segment through X. Conversely, the endpoints of any such segment lie in the minimal face.
A rank-q ≥ 2 face in a simple Jordan cone has dimension q + ai q(q − 1)/2 ≥ 3, where ai is the Peirce
multiplicity. There are only two affine equations in (15.1). Thus each extreme point has one or two
nonzero blocks, each on a primitive ray. Normalize its primitive idempotents to trace one, and write
hi (P ) = ℓi (P ). The constraint columns are (1, hi (P )). The exact possibilities are consequently

   • one block P with hi (P ) = 0;

                                                         65
   • two blocks wP and (1 − w)Q with hi (P )hj (Q) < 0, where w = −hj (Q)/(hi (P ) − hj (Q)).

In the second case i ̸= j. These conditions also suffice by (15.3).
    We record the sign geometry of hi . By a Jordan automorphism and positive rescaling, its defining
element becomes ci1 − ci2 ; this follows from the spectral theorem and transitivity on Jordan frames.
For a classical factor Hr (F), F ∈ {R, C, H}, primitive idempotents are uu∗ /∥u∥2 , and their height is
proportional to
                                             |u1 |2 − |u2 |2
                                                             .
                                                  ∥u∥2
The positive region, in the chart u1 = 1, is {u2 : |u2 | < 1} × Fr−2 and is path-connected; the negative
region is obtained by interchanging coordinates. If r ≥ 3, the zero region is path-connected: for
u1 ̸= 0 it is SdimR F−1 × Fr−2 , and every such point can be joined, by letting a third coordinate tend
to infinity, to a fixed ray in the subspace u1 = u2 = 0. That subspace has connected projective
space (or is one point). This also joins the two chart components when F = R. For r = 2 the zero
region is SdimR F−1 ; only the real case has two points. Every zero ray admits a path entering either
strict sign region: vary |u2 | in the first chart, or introduce a first or second coordinate at a ray with
u1 = u2 = 0. For a spin factor, primitive idempotents form a sphere and height is a nonzero linear
coordinate. Its sign regions are hemispheres, its zero region is an equator, and the same assertions
hold, with a two-point equator in the three-dimensional cone.
    For completeness the Albert case can be checked in coordinates, without an analogy with
associative projective spaces. The chart of the octonionic projective plane with first diagonal
coordinate positive is parametrized by (x, y) ∈ O2 . Its primitive idempotent is

                                            1 x̄
