These are the usual reduced homogeneous coordinates [51, Section 3]; the primitive-idempotent
model is described in [9]. The two octonions x, y generate an associative subalgebra, which directly
verifies P 2 = P and tr P = 1. The positive region is B8 × R8 , and permutation of the first two frame
idempotents identifies the negative region with it. The zero region in this chart is S7 × R8 . Outside
the chart, zero height forces both first diagonal coordinates to vanish, leaving only ci3 : positivity
makes their Peirce off-diagonal components vanish as well. A path (x, ty0 ) with |x| = |y0 | = 1 tends
to ci3 as t → ∞. After reparametrization this is a continuous path on a closed interval, so the entire
zero region is path-connected. Varying |x| gives both signs near every finite zero point; keeping
|x| < 1 or |x| > 1 and letting |y| → ∞ gives such paths at ci3 .
    These facts give paths of extreme points of Z as follows. From any two-block extreme point,
move its positive-height ray within the positive region to a path ending at height zero, while holding
the negative-height ray fixed. The displayed balance weights converge to one and zero, so this joins
the point to a one-block extreme point. For prescribed zero-height rays P and Q in different blocks,
choose continuous paths Pt and Qt such that P0 = P , Q1 = Q, h(Pt ) > 0 for t > 0, and h(Qt ) < 0
for t < 1. The positive path may be held constant after entering its sign region, and similarly for
the negative path. The uniquely balanced pair extends at t = 0, 1 to P, Q, respectively. Finally, any
two zero rays in one block can be connected through a zero ray in another block. This last step
uses L ≥ 2 and also handles each two-point zero region. Thus all extreme points lie in one path
component.

Corollary 15.2 (An exact strict-cap frontier). Suppose the factors in (15.1) all have dimension d,
and put N = dL − 2. Among full-slack factorizations of Z through products of arbitrary nonzero
proper cones of dimension at most d, the minimum factor count and minimum total cone dimension
are
                              Lmin = L,      Mmin = N + 2 = dL.
These statements permit row splitting and impose no regularity on the factor maps.

Proof. For a competing product, ordinary slack rank gives M ′ ≥ N + 1. Equality would identify
that product with the homogenization cone of Z by Theorem 13.4. The projectivized extreme
rays of a nontrivial product form a disconnected union, whereas those of the homogenization are
homeomorphic to the connected set of extreme points of Z. Equality therefore requires a single
factor. This violates the cap, since d < N + 1 = dL − 1. Consequently M ′ ≥ N + 2 = dL and
L′ ≥ M ′ /d ≥ L. The displayed relative-Slater slice gives an attaining full-slack factorization by
Lemma 2.1.

Corollary 15.3 (An alternate classical moment). For L copies of Hr (F)+ , r ≥ 2, the preceding
theorem and corollary remain valid with Ai = 11∗ − Ir instead of an off-diagonal Peirce element.
Writing δ = dimR F, the zero-height primitive rays in one block form Sδ(r−1)−1 .

Proof. This matrix has zero diagonal and signature (1, r − 1), with eigenvalues r − 1, −1, . . . , −1.
The Slater, barrier, and essentiality proofs therefore apply unchanged. In eigenvector coordinates
(a, b), the moment is ((r − 1)|a|2 − ∥b∥2 )/(|a|2 + ∥b∥2 ). A zero ray has the unique representative


                                                     67
   √
(1, r − 1 u) with ∥u∥ = 1, proving the sphere assertion. Every strict-sign ray can be moved to a
zero ray while retaining its sign until the endpoint, by scaling the two components; if one component
vanishes, first introduce it. Two prescribed zero rays in distinct blocks admit
                                                                             √   an explicit extreme
path. For their fixed unit vectors u, replace the representative by (1, λ(c) r − 1 u), where
                                             r−1−c
                                λ(c)2 =                  ,      −1 < c < r − 1.
                                          (r − 1)(1 + c)

Fix 0 < c0 < 1 and assign moments tc0 and −(1 − t)c0 . Their balance weights are 1 − t and t. This
connects the two prescribed one-block points. A second block again connects the two components
when F = R, r = 2. Connectedness and the cap conclusion follow exactly as above.

    At matched cone dimension d = r + δr(r − 1)/2, the classical family and the spin-factor family
built from QL d both have body dimension dL − 2, minimum capped factor count L, and minimum
total dimension dL. Their optimal direct-body barrier parameters are rL − 1 and 2L − 1. These are
two different bodies. The comparison proves that count and dimension do not determine intrinsic
barrier cost; it does not construct a Lorentz lift of the classical body. For Albert factors the same
formulas give N = 27L − 2, Mmin = 27L, and intrinsic parameter 3L − 1.
    The sharp family-specific combination above follows from classical minimal-face, Jordan-barrier,
and simplex arguments, together with the explicit connectivity proof and minimum-dimension
rigidity. No priority claim is attached to the alternate moment construction. The relation to
intrinsic path length requires an additional metric calculation and is treated separately below.

Example 15.4 (A failed coupled logarithmic cancellation). In two three-dimensional Lorentz
factors write qi = t2i − x2i − yi2 , and impose x1 + x2 = 0 and t1 + t2 = 1. The candidate
Ψ = − log q1 −log q2 +log(q1 +q2 ) cancels a boundary logarithmic order, but is not self-concordant. At
(t1 , t2 , x1 , x2 , y1 , y2 ) = (1/3, 2/3, 0, 0, 0, 1/3), the feasible direction varying only y2 gives D2 Ψ = 13/4
and D3 Ψ = 25. Thus (D3 Ψ)2 > 4(D2 Ψ)3 . For example these derivatives follow directly from
q1 = 1/9, q2 = 1/3 − 2h/3 − h2 along that line. This illustrates why the parameter proof above uses
the restricted Hessian metric and an independent lower section, not boundary orders alone.


16      Norm balls and weighted power cones
The local bound depends on a curved boundary patch, rather than on rotational symmetry. It
therefore gives the same unrestricted dimension frontier for every ℓp ball with 1 < p < ∞. The
global frontier also persists, but its proof uses the primal and polar boundaries as independent C 1
manifolds. This distinction matters at coordinate zeros.

16.1     Exact counts and the dimensions of count-optimal lifts
Write
             BpN = {x ∈ RN :         j |xj | ≤ 1},
                                            p          Kp,m = {(t, u) ∈ R × Rm−1 : t ≥ ∥u∥p }.
                                 P

Fix an o-minimal expansion in which the relevant fixed powers are definable; Rexp suffices. For
rational exponents one can work entirely with semialgebraic cones and lifts.

Theorem 16.1 (Dimension-capped ℓp balls). Let 1 < p < ∞, N ≥ 2, and let d ≥ 3 be an integer.
Among exact lifts of BpN through arbitrary definable proper cones of dimensions at most d, with




                                                        68
arbitrary affine equations, projections, and free variables, the minimum number of factors and

                                      N −1
                                               
                                 k∗ =      ,             M∗ = N − 1 + 2k∗ .                         (16.1)
                                      d−2
Every such lift has at least k∗ factors of dimension at least three, even if arbitrarily many rays are
allowed.

Proof. On the boundary patch where every coordinate is nonzero, f (x) =             j |xj | is smooth and
                                                                                           p
                                                                                 P


                                   D2 f (x) = p(p − 1) diag(|xj |p−2 ) ≻ 0.

Its restriction to the tangent space, divided by ∥Df (x)∥, is the positive second fundamental form.
After the reductions of Lemma 2.1, Theorem 3.2 gives N − 1 ≤ i (si − 2)+ , where si ≤ d are the
                                                                      P

reduced factor dimensions. If k+ factors have si ≥ 3, then k+ ≥ k∗ and their dimensions sum to at
least N − 1 + 2k+ . Original dimensions and the total factor count are no smaller.
    For attainment partition N − 1 = b1 + · · · + bk∗ with 1 ≤ bi ≤ d − 2. Make a chain of internal tree
nodes: node i < k∗ has bi new leaf children and node i + 1; the last node has bk∗ + 1 leaf children.
Node i requires its scalar to dominate the p-norm of its children, using Kp,bi +2 , and the root scalar
is one. There are N leaves. Induction from the leaves shows that a node scalar dominates the
p-norm of its descendant leaves. Conversely, assigning exactly those subtree norms makes every
internal constraint feasible whenever ∥x∥p ≤ 1. The projection is therefore exactly BpN , and the
total dimension is i (bi + 2) = N − 1 + 2k∗ . At zero leaves choose strictly positive internal scalars
                    P

decreasing from the root to obtain Slater feasibility.
    The cones Kp,m are closed, pointed, full-dimensional and convex. For p = a/b rational, nonneg-
ative auxiliary variables satisfying zjb = |uj |a , wb = ta , t ≥ 0 and j zj ≤ w give a semialgebraic
                                                                       P

description after projection. This also handles an even denominator without changing the meaning
of the power.

    The tree construction is established prior work: the three-dimensional version is Krokhmal
and Soberanis [57, Proposition 1]; see also Blanco and Martínez-Antón [15] for representations by
specified cone families. The contribution of Theorem 16.1 is its matching lower bound against
arbitrary definable proper factors and affine lifts, together with the total-dimension optimum. The
polyhedral endpoints p = 1, ∞ have no such curved patch and are excluded. Restriction to interior
two-dimensional sections of the positive-capacity factors also gives the ambient barrier lower bound
2k∗ , as in Corollary 10.2. Its attainment is asserted here only for p = 2.

Example 16.3 (A saturated lift with a non-strictly-convex factor). For Bp3 , set

                           K1 = Kp,3 ,        K2′ = {(s, t, z) ∈ Kp,3 : t ≥ 0}.

The two constraints
                                 (t, x1 , x2 ) ∈ K1 ,        (1, t, x3 ) ∈ K2′
project exactly onto Bp3 : the first already implies t ≥ 0, so the extra halfspace in the second is
redundant on the affine lift. At x = 0 and 0 < t < 1 both constraints are strict. Thus both
full three-dimensional factors are necessary in the curvature count, and their total capacity is
2 = 3 − 1. Nevertheless K2′ has the two-dimensional exposed face {(s, 0, z) : s ≥ |z|}, whereas every
proper nonzero face of Kp,3 is a ray. The factors are not linearly isomorphic. At every contact
with (x1 , x2 ) ̸= 0, the added facet is inactive and the local cone germ is unchanged. In particular,
saturation alone cannot classify the global factors or justify a target-dependent barrier premium.

16.2    Global selections and the Young construction
Let q = p/(p − 1). The two boundaries ∂BpN and ∂BqN are C 1 and strictly convex. A global
factorization in this subsection means C 1 primal selections on the first boundary and genuine C 1
normalized dual selections on the second, satisfying the full two-point identity for 1 − ⟨x, y⟩. The
two selections are differentiated in their own boundary coordinates.

Theorem 16.4 (Global C 1 frontier for ℓp balls). Let N ≥ 3 and let d ≥ 3 be an integer. Among
exact lifts with these global selections through arbitrary proper factors of dimension at most d, the
simultaneous minimum capacity S = i (dim Ki −2)+ , total factor count k, and total cone dimension
                                       P

                                   (N, h, N + 2h), h = ⌈N/(d − 2)⌉, d < N + 1,

The lower bound does not require definability of the cones or selections.




                                                        70
Proof. At a contact ⟨x, y⟩ = 1 the tangent spaces are y ⊥ and x⊥ . Their Euclidean pairing is
nondegenerate, since its first kernel is y ⊥ ∩ Rx = {0}. Consequently S ≥ N − 1 by Lemma 3.1.
If equality holds, Theorem 10.1 allows exactly one positive-capacity factor, of dimension N + 1:
both boundaries are C 1 diffeomorphic to SN −1 . A strict cap excludes this case, so integrality gives
S ≥ N . Counting positive-capacity factors then gives k ≥ h and M ≥ N + 2h. For d ≥ N + 1, the
direct lift Kp,N +1 with primal and dual maps (1, x), (1, −y) attains the second line.

    The scalar case r = 1 of (16.5) is the power cone P1/p = {(u, v, w) : u, v ≥ 0, u1/p v 1/q ≥ |w|}.
The corresponding standard p-norm model is recorded in MOSEK ApS [65, Section 4.2.2]. The
construction and Young’s inequality are not novelty claims. Theorem 16.4 proves their optimal
resource count under the stated global regularity condition. For d = 3 it gives exactly N factors,
including when the dictionary is restricted to three-dimensional power cones.
    The scalar selections in (16.7) are both C 2 only when p = 2. If 1 < p < 2, the primal map fails to
be C 2 at coordinate zeros and the dual map is C 2 ; for p > 2 the roles reverse. This does not affect the
preceding proof, which does not differentiate the polarity map yj = sign(xj )|xj |p−1 . Every ambient
barrier has parameter at least 2h in the strict-cap case, by the product orthant-section argument
of Corollary 10.2. This is attained for p = 2; no such equality is asserted for the nonsymmetric
perspective cones. The reduced affine domain in (16.6) also has the intrinsic barrier lower bound
h: fix all allocations uG positive with sum one, set all but one coordinate per group to zero, and


                                                       71
                                             1/p   1/p
vary the remaining coordinate in (−uG , uG ). This gives an h-dimensional open box through
the domain interior, whose boundary lies on the domain boundary. Restriction and the box lower
bound prove the assertion. The Euclidean case is attained by the grouped barrier of Section 9; the
construction here makes no matching intrinsic-parameter assertion for p ̸= 2.
Proposition 16.5 (The two-dimensional power-cone exception). For N = 2, one arbitrary three-
dimensional proper cone suffices for Bp2 , with global C 1 primal and dual selections. Within the
dictionary of three-dimensional power cones, the minimum total factor count is one for p = 2 and
two for p ̸= 2, even without requiring global selections.
Proof. The direct Kp,3 lift proves the first assertion, and the two scalar Young factors give the
upper bound in the power dictionary. A one-factor power-cone lift has total dimension three; by
Theorem 13.4 its cone would be linearly isomorphic to Kp,3 . We show that this is impossible unless
p = 2.
    For Pα , 0 < α < 1, the only projective boundary rays with possibly non-smooth second derivatives
are its two axial rays. In the chart u = 1 at the first, the boundary is v = |w|1/(1−α) ; in the chart
v = 1 at the second it is u = |w|1/α . Elsewhere the boundary is smooth with positive curvature. If
α ̸= 1/2, exactly one of these exponents is below two, so exactly one boundary ray is not C 2 . For
Kp,3 the projective section is the p-unit circle. Near (1, 0) it has graph

                                                              |x2 |p
                              x1 = (1 − |x2 |p )1/p = 1 −            + o(|x2 |p ),
                                                                p

and the same holds at its other three coordinate points. It therefore has exactly four non-C 2
rays when p < 2, and none when p > 2. An invertible linear cone map induces smooth projective
coordinate changes and preserves this count. This excludes α =    ̸ 1/2. For α = 1/2, the section
is a nonsingular conic with nonzero second fundamental form everywhere. When p > 2 the four
coordinate points instead have zero second fundamental form; its vanishing is preserved by projective
transformations, as follows by differentiating a homogeneous supporting equation along its tangent.
When p < 2 the C 2 obstruction already suffices. At p = 2 both cones are Lorentz cones, which
proves the remaining case.

Minimal-face reduction can only reduce the displayed dimensions. For the nonnegative scalar cone
the positive upper graph t = g(x) has exactly the same Hessian on the simplex tangent; its section
has dimension m and curved boundary dimension m − 1. The case m = 1 in the vector statement
reduces to the ordinary Euclidean ball section.

    This result is a dimension bound, not an exact count for every rational weight. Wang [87,
Theorem 3.6] proves the scalar bound m − 1 for simple mediated-set representations, together
with separate denominator-dependent bounds; his Theorem 3.15 gives ⌈m/2⌉ for arbitrary three-
dimensional SOC lifts. The proof above permits arbitrary affine cone-coordinate maps, auxiliary
equations and free variables, and also gives the Euclidean-output term k − 1. Blanco and Martínez-
Antón [15, Definition 1 and Theorem 14] relate extended representations and mediated graphs; their
constructive maps select triples of mediated variables. We make no priority claim for the scalar
inequality from this comparison alone, and do not extend denominator-sensitive bounds beyond
their established representation classes. Theorem 16.6 supplies a self-contained geometric proof in
the affine-lift model used throughout this paper.


17     Matrix balls and sparse completion cones
Matrix balls illustrate two different forms of sharing. A common scale reduces the number of
cone coordinates and the ambient barrier parameter. A positive-semidefinite completion cone
instead preserves a sparse matrix structure. After fixing the scale or the diagonal blocks, both
constructions have exactly the same barrier on the product of matrix balls. The spectral barrier
and the completion barrier used below are classical [3, 24]; the results here identify their full-slack
maps, sharp dimension and face constraints, and their common reduced geometry.




                                                    73
17.1    Mixed contact rank with split rows
For F ∈ {R, C, H} let δ = dimR F and use the real Frobenius pairing ⟨X, Z⟩ = Re tr(X ∗ Z). The
extreme points of the spectral-norm ball in Fr×c , 1 ≤ r ≤ c, are the coisometries

                                                    Er,c = {X : XX ∗ = Ir };

those of its nuclear-norm polar are Zr,c = {uv ∗ : ∥u∥ = ∥v∥ = 1}. These descriptions follow from
singular-value decomposition: a contraction with a singular value below one is a nontrivial average
of contractions, whereas equality on every row forces any such averaging at a coisometry to be
trivial. Similarly, the nuclear unit ball is the convex hull of the rank-one unit matrices, which are
exposed by suitable contractions.
    Suppose a product of these extreme manifolds has a globally C 1 labelled full-row factorization

Proof. For a rank-one polar support, contact fixes a 1×1 corner of Xa and kills both cross rectangles;

To see this, equality in spectral/nuclear duality fixes the supported singular-vector corner to −tIha ,
kills the cross rectangles, and leaves an arbitrary contraction in the complementary rectangle. The
scale contributes one dimension. Each supported block loses δa ha (ra + ca − ha ) coordinates; this is
minimized at ha = 1. Consequently the maximal proper exposed-face dimension is 1 + NG − mina λa ,
proving sufficiency and the count formula.

    Computing the heterogeneous minimum partition is strongly NP-hard: real vector rows ra = 1
and a nonbinding face cap F ≥ D − 1 give ordinary bin packing with item sizes ca and capacity
D − 1. The strongly bounded instances of 3-PARTITION keep even the expanded matrix dimensions
polynomial. This computational statement concerns whole-row grouping, not arbitrary split-row
factorizations.

The real diagonal section is the cube (−1, 1)R , whose sharp barrier lower bound is R [70, Proposition
2.3.6]. This proves the second equality, including coupled competitors.

    The cap theorem by itself optimizes factor count and dimensions in a whole-row model. Equa-
tion (17.6) gives the exact barrier costs of its displayed attaining cones; it does not infer a barrier
lower bound for arbitrary higher-dimensional cones carrying the same rows. For identical rows and
g = ⌈k/Q⌉, these ledgers are (M, νambient , νslice ) = (kδrc + g, kr + g, kr).

17.3    Completion cones and separator credits
Let G be an undirected graph on n vertices, with all diagonal entries specified. Over R or C, let
HG be the space of Hermitian matrices supported on those entries, with the real trace pairing.
The sparse positive-semidefinite cone is KG = {Y ∈ HG : Y ⪰ 0}. Its dual CG identifies with the
partial Hermitian matrices that admit a positive-semidefinite completion. This identification has
no closure gap: in a convergent sequence of partial matrices all diagonals are bounded, and every
positive-semidefinite completion obeys |Zij |2 ≤ Zii Zjj . A convergent subsequence of completions
therefore completes the limit. Also, CG is pointed because a matrix and its negative can both be
completed only if all their diagonals, and hence all entries, vanish.

Proposition 17.4 (Classical completion barrier and its exact parameter). For arbitrary graphs
G1 , . . . , Gl of orders n1 , . . . , nl ,
                                                           

                                    ϑopt int        CG j  =
                                                Y               X
                                                                       nj .
                                                 j               j

For one graph an attaining barrier, up to a constant, is

                                      FG (S) = − log det Z(S),                                  (17.9)

where Z(S) is the unique maximum-determinant positive-definite completion. If the graph is chordal,
its maximal cliques Q and a clique forest’s separators T , counted with multiplicity, give

Empty separators contribute zero.


                                                     77
Proof. The restriction of − log det Y to int KG is an n-LH self-concordant barrier. Its signed
Legendre dual
                             FG (S) = sup {−⟨S, Y ⟩ + log det Y }
                                           Y ∈int KG

is another n-LH self-concordant barrier [3]. Stationarity says that Y −1 completes S. Equivalently,
stationarity of the strictly concave completion problem says that Z(S)−1 is sparse. Boundedness of
the set of completions and existence of a positive-definite completion guarantee existence; strict
concavity gives uniqueness. Since Y is sparse, ⟨S, Y ⟩ = tr(Y −1 Y ) = n, giving FG = −n−log det Z(S).
                                                                                                 P
                                                                                                        nj
The diagonal section of CG is precisely Rn+ , and it meets the interior. Across a product it is R+ .
The sharp orthant bound proves the stated lower bound for every coupled barrier.
    For a chordal graph, eliminate leaf cliques successively. If a leaf clique meets the remainder in
separator T , Schur complementation maximizes the determinant by making the residual cross block
zero and multiplies the determinant of the remainder by det SQ / det ST . Iteration gives (17.10), the
classical clique–separator formula [10, 46]. The running-intersection identity Q |Q| − T |T | = n
                                                                                P          P

gives its degree of logarithmic homogeneity. Self-concordance comes from duality, not from a general
permission to subtract logarithmic barriers.

    For scalar leaves, pa = 1, the construction has D = hδ (s) + k(δs + 1) and parameter s + k. It

arithmetic and stores O(s2 + ks) scalars. These counts concern barrier evaluation; a Newton solve
with additional constraints has a separate cost.

17.4    Exact equality of the reduced geometries
After fixing the shared scales or the star’s diagonal blocks, all constructions have the same retained
coordinates and the same function Φ in (17.8). For one oriented block, with M = I − XX ∗ , its
derivatives are

          ∇Φ(X) = 2M −1 X,         D2 Φ(X)[H] = 2M −1 H + 2M −1 (HX ∗ + XH ∗ )M −1 X.

Hence their Hessian metrics and their reduced Newton systems are identical. Specifically, with the
same real affine map A, right side b, and objective c, both systems use
                                          !                                 !
                            D2 Φ(X) A∗              −DΦ(X) − τ c − A∗ y
                                       ,                                .
                               A    0                   b − AX

This also identifies their central paths and reduced condition numbers. When orientation requires
transposition or adjunction, the identification is a fixed real orthogonal coordinate change (including
conjugation signs in the complex case). An oracle already supplied for one of these reduced objects
therefore transfers by that public coordinate change. Constructing it from different ambient data is
a separate operation: the ambient scales, completion coordinates, dual variables, and KKT systems
are different. Neither the ambient parameter saving nor the sparse coordinate count alone implies a
running-time or quantum-query saving.


18     Nonsymmetric cones: barrier premiums and evaluation costs
Dimension and contact capacity do not determine a cone’s best barrier parameter. This section makes
that distinction quantitative for the norm and perspective cones of Section 16. Throughout this
section νLH (C) denotes the infimum of the parameters of logarithmically homogeneous self-concordant
barriers on C. A barrier on a product may couple all its factors. The logarithmic-homogeneity
hypothesis is essential in the cross-ratio arguments below.

18.1    An additive premium for fixed cone dictionaries
For 1 < p < ∞, put c = min{1/p, 1 − 1/p}. If p ̸= 2, let γ ∈ (0, 1) solve

                                                                           1 + γc
                     (1 − 2c)γ 1−c + (1 − c)γ 1−2c = c,      h(p) = 1 +             .            (18.1)
                                                                          1 + γ 1−c

Set h(2) = 2. The left side of the root equation is strictly increasing from zero to 2 − 3c > c, so the
root exists and is unique. Rearranging gives
                                                       1 − 2c c
                                        h(p) − 2 =           γ .                                 (18.2)
                                                        1−c



                                                  79
Consequently 2 < h(p) < 3 for p ̸= 2, and h(p) = h(q) when 1/p + 1/q = 1. The one-factor
bound h(p) for both three-dimensional power cones and norm cones is due to Hildebrand [53,
Corollaries 7.1–7.2].
     We first record precisely the product argument. For a proper cone C, choose a compact affine
base, a boundary basis e1 , . . . , em , and an interior point x lying in the relative interior of their
simplex. On the projective line through ea , x, let ya be the opposite boundary contact and za the
intersection with the span of the other basis vectors. Write qa = (ea , x; ya , za ), using the convention
(a, b; c, d) = (a − c)(b − d)/((a − d)(b − c)) and continuity at infinity. Hildebrand’s Theorem 5.4
states, in particular, that
                                       qa ≥ 2 =⇒ νLH (C) ≥                                          (18.3)
                                   X                             X

Lemma 18.1 (Product of positive-branch certificates). Suppose each proper cone Ci has the
preceding configuration with cross-ratio sum Si ≥ 2. Then νLH ( i Ci ) ≥ i Si .
                                                               Q        P

Proof. Normalize each local base by ℓi = 1 and the product base by i ℓi = 1. Embed each eia into
                                                                           P

its own direct summand, and choose X = (αi xi )i , where αi > 0 and i αi = 1. These embedded
                                                                        P

vectors form a boundary basis and X lies in their simplex interior. Along the line from an embedded
eia through X and beyond, every other block remains a positive multiple of xj . The first opposite
boundary contact is therefore determined by block i. The missing-basis hyperplane is determined
by the same block. Projection onto block i induces a projective isomorphism on this line, sending
the four defining points to their local counterparts. Thus its cross-ratio is qia , unchanged by αi .
Applying (18.3) to the product gives the result.

Proof. For 1 < p < 2, Hildebrand’s norm-cone configurations have positive cross-ratio sums tending
to h(p) > 2. Apply Lemma 18.1 and take the limit. For p > 2, the signed Legendre transform
F∗ (s) = supx∈int C {−⟨s, x⟩ − F (x)} preserves the LHSC parameter and has domain int C ∗ . Since
   ∗ = K , the preceding case applied to the dual product proves the assertion. This dual step
Kp,3      q,3
is necessary: the direct p > 2 configurations lie on the other branch of Hildebrand’s bound. In a
larger norm cone, set all but two norm coordinates to zero. This linear section meets the ambient
interior and is Kp,3 , so barrier restriction proves (18.4).
    In Pp,ri retain one output coordinate. The resulting interior-meeting section is the scalar power
cone u1/p v 1/q ≥ |w|. Hildebrand’s power-cone configurations are on the positive branch for the
exponent represented by p ≥ 2; swapping u, v handles p < 2. Lemma 18.1 proves (18.5). At p = 2
every displayed cone is a Lorentz cone, and the product orthant lower bound and standard sum
barrier both give 2k.

    The preceding theorem supplies additive lower bounds on a fixed product, without assuming a
separable barrier. Combining it with Theorem 16.1, any dimension-capped lift of BpN using norm-cone
factors of dimensions between three and d has ambient LH parameter at least h(p)⌈(N − 1)/(d − 2)⌉.
Theorem 13.4 gives a dictionary-independent statement at the absolute minimum dimension: any

                                                     80
lift with N + 1 cone coordinates has lifting cone isomorphic to Kp,N +1 and thus LH parameter at
least h(p).
     This premium cannot be assigned to arbitrary factors merely because the target is a non-
Euclidean ball. For rational p, scalar power inequalities have finite Lorentz lifts [87]; applying these
to |xj |p ≤ uj , j uj = 1, gives an exact Lorentz-product lift of BpN . Its individual factors have
                P

optimal parameter two. This observation does not settle the best parameter over all dimension-
capped dictionaries, since such a lift can use more factors than the minimum count.

       Representation of BpN       Ambient LH optimum bounds             Scalar-power construction
       Coordinate power cones                   [N h(p), 3N ]                       3N

For the last entry restrict a grouped barrier to u = v = t in every norm-cone block; the tree contains
N − 1 + kt norm coordinates in total. The existential upper endpoints use dimension-parameter
barriers, whereas the last column uses (18.9). Their oracle and parameter guarantees cannot
be combined as if they belonged to one barrier. At p = 2 the exact ambient optima improve
to 2N, 2kg , 2kt . The generalized-power theorem of Roy and Xiao [77] concerns Euclidean vector
outputs, and supplies no constant-parameter barrier for the ℓp vector output in (16.5).

18.3    Necessary scales for characteristic barriers
For a proper cone C ⊂ Rm , define its characteristic integral ZC (x) = C ∗ exp(−⟨x, y⟩) dy. Its
                                                                                   R

logarithm is m-logarithmically homogeneous. The universal-barrier result supplies parameter m
without scaling [60]; smaller scalar multiples require a separate self-concordance proof.

Theorem 18.3 (Boundary asymptotics of the characteristic family). Let p > 2 and r ≥ 2. If
κ log ZC is self-concordant, its LH parameter satisfies

to barriers obtained by taking the Legendre dual of a scaled characteristic barrier of the dual cone.

final statement follows from (18.6), Kp,r+1
                                        ∗    = Kq,r+1 , and preservation of parameter by Legendre
duality. Characteristic integrals themselves need not be preserved by that duality, which is why the
family is named explicitly.

    The theorem constrains one barrier family, not all LH barriers. In particular its bounds do not
replace the lower endpoints in the table. For p > 2, the perspective bound is strictly smaller than the
norm bound at the same r, since their cross-multiplied difference has the sign of (r − 1)(1/p − 1/2).
This compares necessary scales, not actual optima.

18.4    The three-dimensional gap and failed explicit formulas
For Kp,3 , the general bounds are
                                          h(p) ≤ νLH (Kp,3 ) ≤ 3.                                    (18.16)
The value is two at p = 2 and three at the polyhedral endpoints p = 1 and p = ∞ [53]. The optimal
value in the other cases is not established here. Recent barrier-design work still treats these optimal
parameters as unknown [75]. For p > 2, Theorem 18.3 gives the stronger family-specific necessity
ν ≥ 3p/(p + 1). This is the scale conjectured sufficient in Chares’s numerical experiments, not a
proof of sufficiency.
Proposition 18.4 (Strict separation from the cross-ratio bound). For p > 2, 3p/(p + 1) > h(p).
Consequently no scaled characteristic barrier on Kp,3 attains Hildebrand’s bound. For p < 2 the
analogous statement concerns the dual-characteristic family and the bound 3p/(2p − 1).
Proof. Set s = p − 2 > 0 and A = γ 1/p . Equation (18.1) becomes As (sA + s + 1) = 1, whereas
h(p) = 2 + sA/(s + 1) and 3p/(p + 1) = 2 + s/(s + 3). The left side is increasing in A. At
A0 = (s + 1)/(s + 3) its logarithm is g(s) = log(2s + 3) + (s + 1) log((s + 1)/(s + 3)). Here g(0) = 0
and, putting z = 2/(s + 3),
                                2z                               8 − 24z + 24z 2 − 9z 3
 g ′ (s) = log(1 − z) + z +          =: H(z),        H ′ (z) =                          >0   (0 < z < 2/3).
                              4 − 3z                               (1 − z)(4 − 3z)2

                                                         83
The numerator decreases to zero at 2/3: its derivative is −3(8 − 16z + 9z 2 ) < 0. Since H(0) = 0,
g(s) > 0 and A < A0 . The desired inequality follows. Duality gives the last assertion.

    Several explicit ansatzes cannot close this gap. First, a hypothetical 2-LHSC barrier F on a
proper cone must be a Lorentz barrier up to a linear map and an additive constant. To see this, take
h tangent to a level set of F at x, put a = F ′′ [h, h], b = F ′′′ [h, h, h], and use homogeneity to obtain

            F ′′ [h + tx, h + tx] = a + 2t2 ,      F ′′′ [h + tx, h + tx, h + tx] = b − 6at − 4t3 .

Self-concordance at t = ± a/2 forces b = 0. For Q = e−F this implies D3 Q = 0 on level-tangent
                             p

triples; two-homogeneity gives D3 Q[x, ·, ·] = 0. Hence Q is quadratic. Its Hessian is positive on x
and negative definite on the level-tangent space, so its signature is (1, m − 1). The barrier boundary
condition identifies the cone with a component of Q ≥ 0. This argument proves nonattainment at
two for non-Lorentz cones; it alone would not prove a strict gap for the infimum.
    For a more concrete obstruction, consider, on Kp,3 ,

                        F = −α0 log A − β0 log(A2/p − y 2 ),           A = tp − |x|p .

If this is an LHSC barrier, an affine line reaching a generic boundary point where A > 0 forces
β0 ≥ 1 by univariate self-concordance. Along (t, x, y) = (1, 1 − δ, 0) the same argument forces
α0 + 2β0 /p ≥ 1. Its LH degree therefore satisfies ν = pα0 + 2β0 ≥ p > h(p) for p > 2. For 2 < p ≤ 3
the nonzero coefficient of |x|p at y = 0 also precludes C 3 regularity on the interior axis. The still
simpler formula
                             −a log(tp − |x|p − |y|p ) − b log t, a > 0,
is not C 2 on that axis for 1 < p < 2 and has zero transverse Hessian there for p > 2.
    Even a regular bounded correction cannot repair the latter formula. On t = 1, let L(x, y) =
−a log(1 − |x|p − |y|p ). For p ≤ 3, p =
                                       ̸ 2, adding a C 3 function cannot cancel its interior-axis loss of
C or C regularity. For p > 3, put xδ = (1 − δ)1/p and yδ = δ 1/(p−2) . These points are interior for
  2      3

small δ, and direct differentiation gives

      Lyy (xδ , yδ ) = ap(p − 1) + o(1),        Lyyy (xδ , yδ ) = ap(p − 1)(p − 2)δ −1/(p−2) (1 + o(1)).

If a correction has bounded yy and yyy derivatives along this sequence, the corrected second
derivative stays bounded while the third diverges. Either convexity fails or self-concordance does.
An admissible correction must therefore have singular transverse behavior near the flat boundary;
no conclusion is drawn about unrestricted corrections.
    For completeness the premium has explicit endpoint expansions. Write d0 = |1 − 2/p| and
w = W (e−1 ), where W is Lambert’s function. Then

 2 ε (L + L + 1) + O(ε L ).
 1 2   2                3 3

     The literature supplies the scalar cross-ratio bound, the perspective cones, the scalar power
barrier, and the partial-minimization machinery. The contributions developed here are the product
certificate with its fixed-dictionary consequences, and the necessary characteristic-family scales
and explicit failure mechanisms. To the best of our knowledge, the dimension-dependent bounds
(18.12)–(18.13) have not been stated for these families. Neither these bounds nor the parameter
ledger solves the general optimal-barrier problem.


19     Exponential factors and entropy aggregation
A change in the ambient cone can improve a barrier parameter even when coupling a barrier on
a fixed product cannot. The following results make this distinction exact for exponential and
relative-entropy cones. Their barrier construction and lower-bound method are specializations
of Fawzi and Saunderson [35]; we give the certificates and closure details needed for formulation
comparisons. In particular, we do not claim a new general optimal-barrier theorem.

19.1    A reusable recession certificate
For a closed full-dimensional convex set C, let n(C) be the supremum of            j aj /bj over interior

Lemma 19.1 (Orthant-input hypographs). Let n, B ≥ 1 be integers and h : Rn++ → RB be
componentwise concave and homogeneous of degree one. For

                                   Hh = {(x, z) : x > 0, z ≤ h(x)},

every self-concordant barrier has parameter at least n + B.

Proof. This is the orthant-output specialization of Fawzi and Saunderson [35, Theorem 3.10]. Here
is its certificate. For 0 < ϵ < 1, set vi = ϵ1 + (1 − ϵ)ei and x0 = i vi = b1, where b = 1 + (n − 1)ϵ.
                                                                    P

Concavity and homogeneity imply d = h(x0 ) − i h(vi ) ≥ 0 componentwise. Choose T ′ > maxj dj
                                                    P

and T > T ′ . At y0 = (x0 , h(x0 ) − T 1) use recession directions (vi , h(vi )) with coefficients 1 and
thresholds b, and (0, −T ej ) with coefficients 1−T ′ /T and thresholds 1. Their joint subtraction leaves
(0, d − T ′ 1) ∈ Hh . Each input threshold makes an input coordinate zero; each output threshold
makes an output inequality tight. Hence each threshold point is noninterior. The certificate has
value n/b + B(1 − T ′ /T ). First let T → ∞, then ϵ ↓ 0.


                                                      85
Theorem 19.2 (Exact exponential-product parameter). Let

                                  Kexp = {(u, v, w) : v > 0, w ≥ veu/v }.

The optimal parameter of an arbitrary self-concordant barrier on a product of R primal or dual
exponential cones and P rays is 3R + P .
Proof. Write the primal cone as the hypograph of h(v, w) = v log(w/v). Lemma 19.1 supplies
certificates tending to three. The compatible barrier

                                  − log v log(w/v) − u − log v − log w
                                                                


has parameter three [35]. A ray has a certificate of value one and barrier − log t. Equation (19.2)
and the sum of these barriers give the product assertion. The map (a, b, c) 7→ (a − b, −a, c) is a
linear isomorphism from Kexp ∗ onto Kexp : its interior formula is a < 0, c > −a exp(b/a − 1), and its
closure face a = 0, b, c ≥ 0 maps to the primal face v = 0, u ≤ 0, w ≥ 0.

    The same argument gives j (nj + 1) for a product of scalar hypographs of homogeneous concave

At the boundary, extend each active scalar entropy by ϕ̄(0, y) = 0 for y ≥ 0, and ϕ̄(x, 0) = +∞ for
x > 0. The elementary bound ϕ(x, y) ≥ −y/e shows that these terms are uniformly bounded below
along convergent input sequences. If a point belongs to (19.3), choose an interior approximating
sequence. Every active i has some Abi > 0; boundedness of zb and the lower bounds on all other
terms give an upper bound on ϕi . Thus xi > 0, yi = 0 is impossible for active i. Lower semicontinuity
then gives z ≥ A:J ϕ̄J . Conversely, every such inequality is approximated by positive inputs: replace
each zero coordinate by ϵ; the active entropies converge to their finite extended values, and adjust
the output by their vanishing change. Choosing q = ϕ̄J and r = z − A:J q proves exact finite fiber
existence in (19.5). The final parameter follows from Theorem 19.2.

    The zero-column correction matters for exact sets. With N = B = 1 and A = 0, CA = R3+
includes (x, y, z) = (1, 0, 0); a finite exponential constraint q ≥ ϕ(1, 0) cannot represent this point.
Keeping an unused exponential cone gives only a nonclosed image with the right closure. Replacing
it by two rays restores exactness.
    For a partition of N terms into B nonempty blocks, one may instead sum the local epigraph
coordinates within each block, without separate output rays. The exact ambient parameter is then
3N , while the intrinsic aggregate optimum is 2N + B. Each nonnegative block output residual can
be absorbed into one local epigraph coordinate, proving surjectivity even on the boundary. The
parameter difference is N − B. For a general positive map the lift (19.5) saves n+ on passage to the
intrinsic cone, and the ratio of the usual parameter-dependent short-step factors is
                                       s
                                           2N + n+ + B q
                                                      < 3/2.
                                             2N + B

This compares parameter-based upper bounds, not actual iteration counts.

Part IV
Approximation and exact compilation
20     Approximation with controlled cone factors
The loss of two curvature directions per proper cone is stable under approximation only with
additional control of the factors. We give two versions: a finite sampling theorem requiring no
derivatives, and a local smooth theorem in which the mixed derivative of the slack remains exact.
The hypotheses concern selected factors in specified Euclidean coordinates. They are stronger than
approximation of the projected convex body.

                                                    91
20.3    Why metric accuracy alone is insufficient
For every ϵ > 0, choose a finite δ-net U ⊂ SN −1 with (1 − δ 2 /2)−1 ≤ 1 + ϵ. The polytope

                                  P = {x : ⟨u, x⟩ ≤ 1 for all u ∈ U}

contains BN2 . For x ̸= 0 choose u within δ of x/∥x∥. Then ⟨u, x/∥x∥⟩ ≥ 1 − δ /2, so ∥x∥ ≤ 1 + ϵ for
                                                                                 2

x ∈ P . One nonnegative slack per inequality gives an exact ray-only lift of P . Therefore no positive
Hausdorff or multiplicative error threshold alone preserves the non-ray capacity sum. The number of
scalar inequalities is a separate resource. In the lifted polyhedral Lorentz-cone approximation model,
Ben-Tal and Nemirovski prove matching bounds of order N log(1/ϵ) on that resource for small ϵ [13,
Theorem 1.1 and Proposition 3.1]. For a realization as an orthant of that dimension, its optimal
ambient barrier parameter equals the number of slacks. This is a property of that realization, not of
an arbitrary custom cone for its projection.
    A different obstruction occurs at fixed error even when every block is counted. For d ≥ 3,
                                    2(d−1)                    √ 2(d−1)
                                  B2       ⊂ Bd−1
                                              2   × Bd−1
                                                       2   ⊂ 2B2       .
                                           √
The outer ratio and Hausdorff distance 2 − 1 are attained by pairs of unit vectors. Two Qd factors
represent the product, whereas the exact ball requires at least ⌈(2d − 3)/(d − 2)⌉ = 3 factors of
dimension at most d.
    Even bounded factors and arbitrarily small sampled slack error do not replace relative conditioning
in Theorem 20.1. For its two-point sample (r = 1), fix α > 0 and put β = τ /α. In R2+ take

                A(v0 ) = B(v0 ) = (α, α),   A(v1 ) = (0, α + β),   B(v1 ) = (α + β, 0).

The kernel values are 2α2 , τ + α2 , τ + α2 , 0, so the error tends to zero. Its mixed difference is
nevertheless −2τ = −h2 −pτ 2 , exactly  the sphere value, although (m − 2)+ = 0. Apply the diagonal
automorphism D = diag( β/α, α/β) to A and D−1 to B. All factors become bounded as α ↓ 0
                                  p

and the pairings stay unchanged. The relative increment hypothesis still fails. This finite example
does not by itself construct a global fixed-size approximation of a ball.
    Approximate slack factorizations and their geometric approximation properties are classical.
Gouveia, Parrilo, and Thomas bound inner and outer polytope approximations using the maximum
column and row Euclidean norms of a finite slack-matrix error; they also obtain approximate
factorizations from lifted approximations of nested polyhedra [44, Sections 2.7 and 4.2]. The results
here add explicit local conditioning hypotheses at curved contacts that preserve the dim Ki − 2 rank
bound. They do not assert that a metric approximation admits those selections or that failure of
the hypotheses imposes an algorithmic cost.


21     Exact compilers and the limits of representation-specific rank
A formulation can become smaller because several input constraints have one exact replacement,
because a source-dependent function has a finite summary, or because auxiliary variables compose
simpler functions. These are different operations. We give precise criteria for the first two and
show why exponential-feature rank does not obstruct the third. Throughout this section, a compiler
means a map from the specified input data to an exact representation with the specified output
format. A restriction on that format is part of the theorem.




                                                  92
21.1    Intersections of translates
For a proper cone K ⊂ Rd , write x ≤K y if y − x ∈ K. The following is the finite-dimensional
vector-lattice characterization of simplicial cones, expressed as an exact compilation criterion. The
order-theoretic result is classical; see, for example, Németh [68, Introduction] and the references
there.

Proposition 21.1 (One-translate compilation). Let d ≥ 1. The identity

                                     (a − K) ∩ (b − K) = c − K

has a solution c for every pair a, b ∈ Rd if and only if K = T Rd+ for an invertible linear map T . In
that case, for any finite family,
                          N
                              (ai − K) = T m − K,       mj = min(T −1 ai )j .                      (21.1)
                          \
                                                                i
                          i=1

If the output apex must be one of the two input apices, the universal pairwise property holds if and
only if d = 1.

Proof. The intersection identity says precisely that c is the greatest lower bound a ∧ b. Negation
supplies least upper bounds, so the induced order is a vector lattice. Here is a finite-dimensional
proof of the resulting simpliciality. Distinct extreme rays of K are disjoint in the lattice sense: a
positive element below representatives of both rays must lie on both and therefore vanish. The
lattice decomposition identity

        0≤z ≤x+y         =⇒     z = (z ∧ x) + (z − x)+ ,   0 ≤ z ∧ x ≤ x,       0 ≤ (z − x)+ ≤ y

shows by induction that finite sums of disjoint groups of extreme rays are disjoint. Any linear
dependence among distinct extreme-ray representatives would equate two nonzero positive sums
over disjoint groups, which is impossible. Thus there are at most d extreme rays. A compact base of
a closed pointed cone is the convex hull of its extreme points; since K spans Rd , there are exactly d
such rays and they form a basis. This gives K = T Rd+ . Coordinatewise minima prove the converse
and (21.1).
    An input apex can always be retained exactly when every pair is comparable. This is equivalent
to K ∪ (−K) = Rd . For d ≥ 2, the unit sphere is connected, whereas its intersections with K and
−K would be disjoint nonempty closed sets covering it. Pointedness gives the contradiction. A
one-dimensional proper cone has a total order.

    The number of incomparable input apices need not control the size of the output: even an
antichain of N apices in a simplicial cone is summarized by d coordinate minima. For explicit
classical output and coherent access to an individual transformed entry (T −1 ai )j , quantum minimum
finding [28] gives
                            √                              √
                   Q = O d N log(d + 1) ,         Q = Ω(d N ),       R = Θ(dN ).                 (21.2)
                                            


Here Q and R denote bounded-error quantum and randomized entry-query complexities, respectively,
and success means that all d output coordinates are correct. Exact comparison and retrieval of an
encoded entry are included in the oracle contract. For the upper bound, run each minimum search
with failure probability at most 1/(3d). For the quantum lower bound, restrict each column to all
ones or to a single hidden zero. The output supplies d independent search-decision answers, to

                                                  93
which the adversary direct-sum theorem applies [2, Theorem 4]. For the randomized lower bound,
couple an input with independent equiprobable all-one/single-zero columns to the input with any
one column changed. If an algorithm succeeds on the full answer vector with probability at least
2/3, each column must be distinguished with constant advantage; a uniform hidden zero then forces
Ω(N ) expected probes in each column. Summing over columns gives Ω(dN√         ). The logarithm in
the quantum upper bound accounts for simultaneous success; no exact Θ(d N ) upper bound is
asserted here. If the supplied oracle instead returns a physical coordinate of ai and T −1 is dense,
forming one transformed entry can require d source queries.
    For the canonical logarithmic barriers, the scenario product has parameter N d and the compiled
translate has the optimal parameter d. On identical-apex instances, a radial curve whose common
transformed slack is α1 has lengths
                             √                           √
                               N d |log(α1 /α0 )| and      d |log(α1 /α0 )| ,

respectively. This follows directly from the Hessians of −N j log sj and − j log sj . It compares
                                                                     P                 P

these barriers, not all algorithms for the underlying optimization problem.
    A factorwise radial restriction permits the same operation for arbitrary proper cones. If

Indeed, the translates in each factor are nested in their scalar radius. The corresponding query

Proposition 21.2 (Lorentz-translate obstruction). For every N , there are a1 , . . . , aN ∈ R3 such
that every finite identity
                                      N                    m
                                          (ai − Q3 ) =         (cℓ − Q3 )
                                      \                    \

                                      i=1                  ℓ=1
satisfies m ≥ N .
Proof. Choose distinct vi ∈ S1 and 0 < ρ < R, and set ai = (0, ρvi ). On the plane t = −R, the
intersection is i B(ρvi , R). The point zi = −(R − ρ)vi satisfies
               T


        ∥zi − ρvi ∥ = R,       ∥zi − ρvj ∥2 = (R − ρ)2 + ρ2 + 2ρ(R − ρ)⟨vi , vj ⟩ < R2     (j ̸= i).

Hence each original circle contributes an open boundary arc. An alternative translate intersects
this plane in a disk. Its radius must be positive because the common intersection contains an open
disk. Every point of an original open arc lies on the boundary of at least one alternative disk. Two
distinct circles intersect in at most two points, so finitely many alternative circles can cover that
arc only if one coincides with the original circle. Distinct original circles therefore require distinct
alternative disks.

    This obstruction already occurs in a cone of Jordan rank two. Copying the three-dimensional
variable at each constraint and imposing consensus along a path gives a formulation whose graph of
variable blocks is a path. Thus a bound on cone rank or on this block graph does not bound the
surviving number of translates. The proposition concerns same-space, same-oriented translates only.
It gives no lower bound for lifts with auxiliary variables, different cone maps, or custom barriers.

Theorem 21.3 (Continuous summary dimension). If Rh = R < ∞, the source dependence in
(21.4) is summarized by R scalar linear moments. Conversely, choose any q linearly independent
dilates h(λ1 r), . . . , h(λq r). If a continuous map E : Rq++ → Rm admits a deterministic decoder
recovering i ai h(λi r) for every r > 0 and every a ∈ Rq++ , then m ≥ q. Consequently Rh is the
            P

exact minimum summary dimension for these unrestricted positive-coefficient families; if Rh = ∞,
no fixed finite dimension suffices.

Proof. Choose a basis h1 , . . . , hR of the dilation space and write h(λr) =                   ℓ mℓ (λ)hℓ (r). Then
                                                                                              P

                     Mℓ =        wi ci mℓ (di /ci ),         Φ(U, V ) = U            Mℓ hℓ (V /U ).

The coefficient functions mℓ are continuous: choose R evaluation points giving an invertible evaluation
matrix and solve for the coefficients using the continuous values h(λrj ).
     For the lower bound, equality E(a) = E(b) would give equality of the reconstructed functions,
hence a = b by linear independence. Thus E is injective. If m < q, append q − m zeros to obtain a
continuous injection from the open set Rq++ into Rq . Invariance of domain [48, Theorem 2B.3] says
its image is open, whereas the image lies in a proper coordinate subspace. This is impossible.

    Decoder continuity is unnecessary. Encoder continuity and the finite-dimensional real output are
essential hypotheses; arbitrary set-theoretic encodings into one infinite-precision real are excluded.
For a fixed restricted family of coefficients, the lower bound need not equal Rh .

Proposition 21.4 (Classical dilation classification). The rank Rh is finite if and only if
                                                       J
                                           h(r) =            rρj Pj (log r),                                     (21.5)
                                                       X

                                                       j=1

where the ρj are distinct complex numbers and Pj are nonzero polynomials, with conjugate terms
paired when h is real-valued. The empty sum represents h = 0, with Rh = 0. Otherwise Rh =
  j (deg Pj + 1).
P


Proof. Put f (y) = h(ey ). Its translation span W is finite dimensional exactly when the dilation
span is. Translation acts on W by a continuous group T (t) of invertible matrices: continuity follows
by choosingR an invertible finite evaluation matrix as above.R For completeness, choose small δ > 0 so
that M = 0δ T (t) dt is invertible. The identity T (s)M = ss+δ T (t) dt proves differentiability of T .
The group law then gives T ′ (s) = T (s)A with A = T ′ (0), so T (s) = esA . Evaluation at zero shows

                                                           95
that f is an exponential polynomial; the Jordan form of A gives (21.5). Conversely, translates of
each eρj y Pj (y) lie in the span of y ℓ eρj y for 0 ≤ ℓ ≤ deg Pj . These functions are linearly independent.
Each occurs in the cyclic translation span: on this finite-dimensional span the differential operator
acts with one Jordan chain of length deg Pj + 1 for each distinct ρj , and a polynomial in that
operator isolates each chain. This also gives the stated rank.

    The translation-space classification is due to Anselone–Korevaar [4]; see also Engert’s extension
and historical account [30]. We included the elementary continuous one-parameter proof to specify
all assumptions. For example, − log r has rank two, rα has rank one, and er has infinite rank. The
latter follows from independence of distinct exponentials eλr on any open interval. The contribution
of Theorem 21.3 is the exact continuous-summary interpretation, rather than a new classification of
translation spaces. It concerns source evaluation, not arbitrary conic lift size or barrier parameter.

21.3    A two-moment relative-entropy example
For positive definite Hermitian matrices use the homogeneous, uncorrected Umegaki convention
D(X∥Y ) = tr X(log X − log Y ). Let the weights and positive scalar scales be fixed, and define

                    A=                       B=       wi ci log(ci /di ),   Deff = Ae−B/A .           (21.6)
                              X                   X

                               i                  i

Then
                              wi D(ci X∥di Y ) = AD(X∥Y ) + B tr X = D(AX∥Deff Y ).                   (21.7)
                     X

                      i

Indeed, scalar multiplication adds (log c)I to a matrix logarithm; no commutativity of X and Y
is required. The identity extends to the positive-semidefinite closure with the usual condition
supp X ⊆ supp Y for finite relative entropy. If that condition fails, both sides are +∞. If X = 0,
both sides vanish.
    After acquisition of A, B, all value and derivative evaluations use one relative-entropy term and
no further access to the scenarios. The scalar restriction, in which − log r has dilation rank two,
shows that two continuous real statistics are necessary for general positive coefficient/scale families.
If A is fixed in advance, only B needs to be stored. For ci = 1 and i wi = 1, Deff = i dw     i .

    The convention matters. For D+ (X∥Y ) = D(X∥Y ) + tr Y − tr X and C = i wi di , the exact
                                                                                    P

                               wi D+ (ci X∥di Y ) = D+ (AX∥Deff Y ) + (C − Deff ) tr Y.
                       X

                          i

The compiler preserves a weighted sum in one objective or epigraph; it does not preserve separate
scenario inequalities or separate outputs. Nor does its source-free online evaluation apply when the
scales change with the optimization variables.
    The relative-entropy epigraph barrier of parameter 2n+1 for n×n matrices is established by Fawzi
and Saunderson [35]. Positive-map composition and removal of redundant input log determinants
are treated more generally by He et al. [50]. Equation (21.7) is an elementary scaling identity; its
role here is to make an exact source summary explicit. It is not a new barrier construction.
    Acquiring the moments is a separate task. For a uniform average of bounded coherently accessible
entries, quantum amplitude estimation uses O(min{N, η −1 }) queries for constant-success additive
error η, compared with the randomized upper bound O(min{N, η −2 }) [18]. This statement presumes
that preparing the uniform index state and computing an entry are included in the access model;
arbitrary implicit weights are not free. On Boolean entries, exact acquisition, or error strictly below

                                                          96
1/(2N ) in the mean, recovers the Hamming weight and requires Ω(N ) quantum queries [67]. If A
is known exactly, an estimate Bb changes the epigraph boundary by precisely (B b − B) tr X. Since
X ⪰ 0, one-sided bounds on B therefore give globally valid inner and outer epigraphs.
    A small barrier parameter alone does not supply source-free evaluation. For z ∈ {0, 1}N , let
                                        N
                      ϕz (x1 , x2 ) =         D(x1 ∥e−zi x2 ) = N x1 log(x1 /x2 ) + |z|x1 .
                                        X

The epigraph on x1 , x2 > 0 is a linear image of a single scalar relative-entropy cone: use
D(N x1 ∥N x2 ) = N x1 log(x1 /x2 ) and shift the epigraph coordinate by |z|x1 . Thus

                        Fz (t, x1 , x2 ) = − log t − ϕz (x1 , x2 ) − log x1 − log x2

is a three-parameter logarithmically homogeneous self-concordant barrier, up to an additive constant
[35]. Under the promise |z| ∈ {0, 1}, its value at the common interior point (2, 1, 1) differs by
exactly log 2. An oracle returning
                          √         that barrier value with error less than log 2/3 solves unique-search
decision and requires Ω( N ) quantum or Ω(N ) randomized source queries. The lower bound is on
initial source access: it does not apply after |z| has been learned and stored.

Theorem 21.6 (Bounds for exact exponential lifts of a product-cosh graph). Let r ≥ 1, K > 0,
and fr (z) = ri=1 cosh zi . Its signed exponential rank on every nonempty open subset of Rr is 2r .
              Q

Proof. The expansion fr (z) = 2−r ϵ∈{−1,1}r e⟨ϵ,z⟩ has 2r distinct nodes. To prove independence
                                         P

against any competing finite node set, choose a direction not perpendicular to any pairwise node

    For the upper bound impose, for each i,
                                                                                         !
          (zi − si , 1, ui ), (−zi − si , 1, vi ) ∈ Kexp ,    ui + vi ≤ 2,       si , 1, t

Eliminating ui , vi gives si ≥ log cosh zi , and eliminating s gives exactly t ≥ fr (z). Restricting to the

   For the lower bound choose T > 1 and intersect the given epigraph with t ≤ T . Its image is the

                           C = {(z, t) : z ∈ [−K, K]r , fr (z) ≤ t ≤ T } ⊂ Rr+1 .
It has nonempty interior. In a neighborhood of (0, 1) its lower boundary is the graph of fr , and
                                            h                                    i
                       ∇2 fr (z) = fr (z) diag(sech2 zi ) + (tanh z)(tanh z)T ≻ 0.                      (21.10)
Its second fundamental form is therefore positive definite in all r tangent directions. The intersection
adds one nonnegative-ray factor and does not add an exponential factor. After translation to put
an interior point at the origin, Lemma 2.1 eliminates free variables and passes to the minimal
face. These sets are definable in the real exponential structure; the generic selection argument
and Theorem 3.2 apply. Each retained face of an exponential cone has dimension at most three
and thus contributes at most one unit to i max{dim Ki − 2, 0}; every ray contributes zero. The
                                           P

r-dimensional positive-curvature patch gives r ≤ R. Finally, Theorem 19.2 gives the exact ambient
parameter 3R + P for the original product. The artificial truncation ray is used only in the curvature
argument and is not charged to that original product.
    The same proof gives R ≥ r for any definable convex epigraph with an r-dimensional C 2 graph
patch having positive definite Hessian, provided that a compact polyhedral truncation retains
that patch. It is important to keep the epigraph coordinate in this argument: a level-set section
of fr alone has only r − 1 curved directions. Theorem 21.6 makes no optimality claim for the
constant 2 in its constructive upper bound. Its lower bound follows from curvature, independently
of exponential-feature rank.

                                                         98
Proposition 21.7 (Polyhedral obstruction to an approximate transfer). Let D ⊂ Rr be a compact
polytope and let f > 0 be convex and C 2 on a neighborhood of D. For every 0 < ε < 1, a finite
piecewise-affine convex function p satisfies

                                       (1 − ε)f ≤ p ≤ f     on D.

In particular,
                                epi p/(1 − ε) ⊆ epi f ⊆ epi p
                                               
                                                                 over D,
and the two approximating epigraphs use only scalar linear inequalities.

Proof. Let mf = minD f > 0 and Lf = supD ∥∇2 f ∥op . If Lf = 0, one tangent is exact on D.
Otherwise choose a finite δ-net N ⊂ D, with δ 2 ≤ 2εmf /Lf , and set

                                  p(z) = max{f (x) + ⟨∇f (x), z − x⟩}.
                                         x∈N

Convexity gives p ≤ f . Taylor’s theorem on the segment between z and a net point within δ
gives f (z) − p(z) ≤ Lf δ 2 /2 ≤ εf (z). The epigraph of a finite maximum of affine functions is their
halfspace intersection, giving the claim.

    Thus uniform relative approximation on a fixed cube can use zero exponential cones when
arbitrarily many scalar inequalities are free. Charging those inequalities, or imposing the conditioned
contact hypotheses of the preceding approximation results, changes the question. Neither exact
signed-feature rank nor the exact curvature count alone is an approximation lower bound. Together,
the three compiler models identify which representation or access restriction supplies each conclusion;
none yields a lower bound on all optimization algorithms.


22     Projection, sufficient statistics, and limits of local compilation
An exact reformulation may discard the original recourse coordinates and optimize over their
projection. This changes the computational interface: a small first-stage barrier need not provide
all recourse variables, and a small existential barrier need not have a cheap reusable oracle. We
make these distinctions explicit. Farkas elimination, quadratic epigraph barriers, and quantum
spectral approximation are established tools. The results below combine them under precise access
assumptions and identify limitations of two specific compilation rules. They make no claim that a
small projected barrier preserves the full product-slack geometry.

22.1    Polyhedral and ordered recourse
Let W ∈ Rq×p and T ∈ Rq×k be public, and consider

                       x ∈ X0 ,      W yi = hi − T x,       yi ≥ 0   (1 ≤ i ≤ N ).               (22.1)

Write H = range W and C = {z ∈ H : W T z ≥ 0}. This polyhedral cone is pointed in H: if
z, −z ∈ C, then W T z = 0 and z ∈ H, whence z = 0. Let z1 , . . . , zL generate its extreme rays; L = 0
is allowed.

            p 

                                                     100
    Here is a deliberately conditional algorithmic consequence. Suppose these inequalities and any
constraints for X0 define a full-dimensional LP in k effective variables, with finite optimum; assume
coherent row access, the data precision assumptions, and an initial point sufficiently close to the
central path required by Apers and Gribling [5, Theorems 1.3 and 6.1]. Their algorithm returns
an explicit feasible ϵ-optimal first-stage vector. With fixed q√and a polynomial number of public
constraints for X0 , simulating each row query locally gives O( e N poly(p, k)) block queries. This is
a reduction to that algorithm; initialization, facial reduction, and full recourse output are additional
tasks.
    Finally, if recourse has the ordered form h0 + αi e − T x ∈ K, where K is a proper cone and e ∈ K,
it reduces exactly to the single constraint with α∗ = mini αi . This follows because (αi − α∗ )e ∈ K,
and the minimum is attained. For a symmetric cone of rank√r, its standard barrier adds at most r
to the first-stage parameter. The threshold again takes √ O( N ) queries. The scalar family x ≥ zi ,
                                                              e
zi ∈ {0, 1}, shows the familiar OR lower bounds Ω( N ) quantum and Ω(N ) randomized [14] for
determining the threshold to error below 1/2. These statements concern ordered offsets; arbitrary
offsets have no such single-threshold reduction.

Proof. Expansion gives the sufficient statistic and the derivatives. Since ker Q = i ker Ai , q is
                                                                                                    T

constant along the deleted directions. For a line direction h, put a = Ds[h] and b = −D2 s[h, h] ≥ 0.
Direct differentiation gives

           Df [h] = −a/s,     D2 f [h, h] = a2 /s2 + b/s,          D3 f [h, h, h] = −2a3 /s3 − 3ab/s2 .

For u, v ≥ 0, 2u3 +3uv ≤ 2(u2 +v)3/2 , as is seen by squaring and subtracting, leaving 3u2 v 2 +4v 3 ≥ 0.
Apply this with u = |a|/s, v = b/s to obtain self-concordance. Also (Df [h])2 ≤ D2 f [h, h]. The
Hessian is positive definite on the quotient, and the function diverges at the epigraph boundary.

exactly one. Addition of G removes any remaining flat directions and gives the asserted parameter
bound.


                                                         101
    This is the familiar quadratic epigraph barrier, not a new barrier formula. Its relevance here is
the exact replacement of many product factors by a single statistic, while retaining local recourse
access. Querying block i recovers ri = Ai x − bi and ti = ∥ri ∥2 . This supplies a feasible completion
for every projected feasible (x, τ ); strict recourse can be obtained by distributing positive coupling
slack. Materializing all N recourse scalars requires Ω(N ) output operations, whereas one requested
block uses one block query plus local arithmetic.
    The statistic may also be approximated safely. Let C be the stack of all Ci , with m rows and
k + 1 columns. The quantum spectral approximation theorem of Apers and Gribling [5, Theorem 3.1]
gives, with high probability, M

the source’s O(

                p

oracle simulates one row query at constant query cost. The case Ai = 0, bi ∈ {0, 1}, with zero
or one nonzero bi , reduces objective approximation with error below 1/2 to OR, and gives the
corresponding quantum and randomized scenario-query lower bounds.
    The matrix counterpart is equally exact. Let Ri (x) = Bi0 + kj=1 xj Bij ∈ Rdi ×s and let R(x)
                                                                   P

stack the Ri (x). Delete directions h with DR[h] = 0.

                                f (x, Y ) = − log det Y − R(x)T R(x)

is a barrier of exact parameter s. No barrier for this domain has parameter below s. Without
quotienting, addition of a ν0 -barrier for X0 gives a barrier of parameter at most ν0 + s. All data
enter through the positive-semidefinite block Gram matrix

                                 Mjℓ =                      0 ≤ j, ℓ ≤ k,
                                         X

of order (k + 1)s.

Proof. Put S = Y − RT R. Along a direction, set

                     A = S −1/2 DS S −1/2 ,      B = 2S −1/2 (DR)T DR S −1/2 ⪰ 0.

Differentiation gives

                 Df = − tr A,     D2 f = tr A2 + tr B,      D3 f = −2 tr A3 − 3 tr(AB).

The bounds | tr A3 | ≤ ∥A∥3F and | tr(AB)| ≤ ∥A∥F tr B, together with the scalar inequality in
Proposition 22.2, prove self-concordance. Moreover (tr A)2 ≤ s tr A2 ≤ sD2 f . The Hessian vanishes

                                                    102
only if DR = 0 and DY = 0, so is positive definite in the effective variables. The boundary
divergence is immediate. Varying only Y in direction S gives decrement squared s. For the lower
bound against any barrier, fix x and restrict Y = R(x)T R(x) + diag(u1 , . . . , us ). This is the positive
