bound is s. Finally, expanding RT R gives j,ℓ ξj ξℓ Mjℓ , with ξ0 = 1.
                                            P


    Equivalently, this is a fixed-identity Schur-complement slice of a PSD cone; the restriction credits
the fixed block. Applying spectral approximation to the stack [Bi0 , . . . , Bik ] and using (22.6) gives
a uniform Loewner upper bound on R(x)T R(x), by congruence with ξ ⊗ Is . Thus the same safety
argument compiles a matrix epigraph. It does not by itself give a relative objective guarantee for an
arbitrary SDP objective.

22.3     A scalar normalization cannot repair every convolution barrier
The preceding reduction uses an additive quadratic statistic. A natural alternative for general blocks
is to convolve block measures and take the Legendre transform of the resulting log-Laplace function.
The following obstruction concerns exactly that construction and scalar normalizations that depend
only on the number of blocks.

Proposition 22.4 (Convolution normalization obstruction). Let µ be uniform probability measure
on [0, 1], with log-Laplace function f and conjugate F = f ∗ . For N independent variables, consider
the conjugate of the log-Laplace function of their average, multiplied by a positive scalar aN . Suppose
this rule must be a standard self-concordant barrier for both of the following collections: N copies of
µ, and one copy of µ with N − 1 point masses at zero. Then aN ≥ 1. Its barrier parameter on the
replicated collection is therefore at least N .

Proof. Independence gives fav (θ) = N f (θ/N ) for identical copies, hence fav ∗ (x) = N F (x). For the

second collection it gives fav (θ) = f (θ/N ), hence fav (x) = F (N x). For θ < 0,
                                                      ∗


                         f (θ) = log(1 − eθ ) − log(−θ),      f ′ (θ) = −1/θ + O(eθ ),
                        f ′′ (θ) = θ−2 + O(eθ ),             f ′′′ (θ) = −2θ−3 + O(eθ ).

As x = f ′ (θ) ↓ 0, Legendre differentiation gives F ′ = θ, F ′′ = 1/f ′′ (θ) and F ′′′ = −f ′′′ (θ)/(f ′′ (θ))3 .
Therefore
                                   (F ′ )2              |F ′′′ |
                                           −→ 1,                  −→ 1.
                                    F ′′              2(F ′′ )3/2
Affine rescaling x 7→ N x does not change either ratio. Multiplying a function by aN divides the
                   √
second ratio by aN , so self-concordance for the second collection forces aN ≥ 1. On the first
collection, the first ratio tends to aN N , which is a lower bound on its barrier parameter.

    The degenerate blocks are part of the hypothesis. The proposition neither rules out a rule using
the individual distributions nor supplies a lower bound for all barriers of the projected interval. In
particular, it does not obstruct a nonfactorized entropic or universal barrier. We claim only the
stated normalization impossibility, rather than novelty for the classical conjugacy identity used in
its proof.

22.4     Two obstructions to reusable exact summaries
Quadratic sufficient statistics have bounded dimension when the first-stage dimension is fixed. This
fails for arbitrary exponential sums even in one first-stage variable.

                                                      103
Proposition 22.5 (Continuous exact summaries). Fix distinct real a1 , . . . , aN and let qw (x) =
P       ai x , with w ∈ RN . Suppose a continuous statistic S : RN → Rd determines the entire strict
  i wi e                 ++                                      ++
epigraph of qw . Then d ≥ N . The decoder need not be continuous.

Proof. Equal statistics give equal epigraphs and hence equal functions. Their first N derivatives at
zero give the Vandermonde system i (wi − vi )aji = 0, 0 ≤ j < N , so w = v. Thus S is injective. If
                                    P

d < N , composing with the coordinate inclusion Rd ,→ RN contradicts invariance of domain, since
its image lies in a subspace with empty interior.

    The next statement concerns preprocessing queries rather than the dimension of a continuous
statistic. An offline summary is called reusable if, after preprocessing, it can answer all the stated
requests with joint success probability at least 2/3, using no further input queries. It may be
classical or quantum, provided reuse has this operational meaning. This excludes interpreting a
single consumable quantum state as an oracle for arbitrarily many later answers.

Proposition 22.6 (Reusable boundary oracles). Let z ∈ {0, 1}N , γ = 1/4, and define the planar
epigraph
             Ez = {(x, t) : t ≥ φz (x)}, φz (x) = x2 + max (2ix − i2 + γzi ).            (22.7)
                                                              1≤i≤N

Each inequality has a constant-size rotated-SOC representation. A reusable summary giving all
values φz (i) to absolute error less than γ/3 requires Ω(N ) quantum preprocessing queries to the
bit oracle for z. The same lower bound holds for reusable membership answers at the public points
(i, 2i2 + γ/2) with the promise of vertical margin γ/2.

Proof. At x = i, the ith affine term exceeds term j ̸= i by (i − j)2 + γ(zi − zj ) ≥ 3/4. Thus
φz (i) = 2i2 + γzi . Either oracle recovers every bit. For completeness, a T -query quantum algorithm
on an N -bit input has final state in the span of at most DT = Tj=0 Nj fixed vectors: expand
                                                                     P         

its amplitudes in the characters of the input signs, whose degree increases by at most one per
query. Purification handles intermediate measurements and randomness. An ensemble of 2N states
in a space of dimension at most DT can be identified, on a uniform input, with probability at
most DT /2N , because each density matrix is bounded above by the identity on that space and
the decoding POVM sums to that identity. For T ≤ N/4, the binomial entropy bound makes this
probability tend to zero. Constant successful recovery therefore requires T = Ω(N ), as in oracle
interrogation [84].

    Rescaling the exposed locations to x = i/N and the affine terms to 2N ix − i2 preserves the gap.
Replacing the common quadratic by xr on x > 0, for fixed rational r > 1, or by ex , gives the same
recovery proof with constant-size power or exponential constraints. The locations then stay in a
bounded interval, so the common exponential term causes no large-value precision issue. This is a
restriction
         √ on reusable preprocessing. Computing a single requested boundary value can still use
fresh O(
       e N ) maximum-finding queries. It also does not rule out approximate summaries under a
weaker norm or an input distribution that changes the recovery promise.

22.5    The parameter cost of additive local logarithms
The same exposed arcs impose a distinct barrier obstruction, even though Ez has only two coordi-
nates.




                                                 104
Proposition 22.7 (Additive local barrier cost). For the slacks si (x, t) = t − x2 − 2ix + i2 − γzi in
(22.7), suppose
                                              N
                               F (x, t) = −         wi log si (x, t),   wi > 0,
                                              X

                                              i=1

is a standard self-concordant barrier with parameter ν on int Ez . Then wi ≥ 1 for every i, and
ν ≥ i wi ≥ N . Nevertheless, this line-free planar domain admits a barrier of parameter two.
     P


Proof. At (i, φz (i) + ϵ), the ith slack is ϵ and all other slacks stay at least 3/4. In direction h = (0, 1),

                   D2 F [h, h] = wi ϵ−2 + O(1),           |D3 F [h, h, h]| = 2wi ϵ−3 + O(1).
                                      √
The self-concordance ratio tends to 1/ wi , forcing wi ≥ 1. For fixed x and t → ∞,

                                  (DF [h])2     ( i wi /si )2
                                                     P

                                                                 X

The gradient inequality implies the parameter bound. The epigraph is full-dimensional and contains
no affine line, because its boundary grows quadratically in either horizontal direction and is bounded
below on each vertical line. The universal-barrier theorem in dimension two therefore supplies a
two-parameter barrier [60].

    The upper and lower statements concern different permitted formulas. A barrier that combines
scenarios through a global construction can have parameter independent of N ; a weighted sum of
these local logarithms cannot. Together with the reusable-oracle lower bound, this explains why an
existential projected barrier, an explicit sufficient statistic, and a cheap oracle are three separate
requirements.




                                                        105
Part V
Movement in barrier metrics
23     Certificate ranks and movement in a fixed barrier metric
A lower bound on a barrier parameter does not by itself give a lower bound on primal movement.
The missing information is which part of the cone an objective forces toward its boundary. We now
connect the certificate ranks proved above to this part of the metric. The weighted exposed-minor
estimate below is also established in the companion manuscript [21]; we include its proof to make
the connection independent of that manuscript. The Jordan identities are classical [31], as is the
conversion from bounded local steps to Riemannian distance [72]. The contribution here is their
composition with the lift-dictionary and selected-certificate frontiers, with the same certificate
retained in both arguments.
    For a barrier F with positive-definite Hessian on its effective affine domain Ω, write
                                                                            Z 1
                    ∥h∥2F,x = D2 F (x)[h, h],        dF (x, y) = inf              ∥γ̇(t)∥F,γ(t) dt.
                                                                        γ    0

The infimum is over piecewise C 1 curves in Ω joining the two points. For a set T ⊂ Ω, put
dF (x, T ) = inf y∈T dF (x, y), with value +∞ if T is empty. A free affine direction on which the slack
does not change is removed before defining this metric.

In this statement the certificate is transformed with the coordinates; its pairing, and hence the gap,

upper bound for every slice.
    Hauser and Güler’s classification [49, Theorem 5.5] says that every self-scaled barrier on a
symmetric cone is, up to an additive constant, of the displayed form on its irreducible factors,
with αi ≥ 1. Consequently the theorem covers all self-scaled barriers on a fixed symmetric cone.
Changing the scales changes both Qα and ∆α ; retaining the unweighted ∆ after rescaling would be
incorrect. The classification gives no analogous assertion for arbitrary coupled barriers on an affine
slice.

    The product assertion only needs existence of one such aggregate certificate. It neither says
that every aggregate certificate has this rank nor minimizes over its whole dual fiber. A high-rank
certificate suffices because its determinant estimate holds for every accurate primal endpoint. For
self-scaled barriers the same statements hold with the actual weighted Qα ≥ Q. The constants
∆α can depend on the lift, objective, reference, and certificate; the corollary is a fixed-instance
asymptotic statement, not a uniform finite-accuracy estimate over all lifts. In particular one cannot
replace Q in this argument by a larger ambient or restricted barrier parameter.

23.3    Affine PSD pencils and spectral scales
A complementary formulation uses a compression of an affine pencil. It is useful when an exposed

rank-profile interpretation also appear in [21].

Choose r = ⌈cΛ⌉ with fixed c > 2/(3a) in the geometric case. Every summand in (23.14) is Oa (Λ2 ),
so the upper order is Λ3/2 . In the polynomial case take r = ⌈cε−1/(β−1) ⌉, c > Cβ . Then log(rdi /ε)
is a bounded constant minus β log(i/r), whose positive-part square has a finite integral on (0, 1].
The sum is Oβ (r), giving the matching order ε−1/[2(β−1)] . Constants here may depend on the fixed
c and decay parameter. These examples concern arbitrary feasible paths in specially chosen box
spectrahedra, with rank growing at the specified rate; they do not supply matching central paths or
matching upper bounds for all affine pencils.


24     Arbitrary barriers and the distinction between primal and dual
       movement
The rank-sensitive movement bounds use a specified symmetric-cone metric. A different conclusion
holds uniformly over logarithmically homogeneous barriers if movement is measured in the full
primal–dual metric. We give the classical geometric argument explicitly and then combine it with
the formulation bounds. The dimension envelope and the objective-distribution calculations below
also appear in the companion manuscript [21, Sections 5, 9, and 10]; they are included to make the
different metric and endpoint contracts precise.

24.1    The feasible gap set in the product metric
Let F be a ν-logarithmically homogeneous standard self-concordant barrier on a proper cone K.
Define the negative-pairing conjugate and product barrier by

                   F∗ (s) = sup {−⟨s, x⟩ − F (x)},                Fb (x, s) = F (x) + F∗ (s).
                            x∈int K

Consider the strictly feasible conic pair

               min{⟨c, x⟩ : Ax = b, x ∈ K},             max{⟨b, y⟩ : AT y + s = c, s ∈ K ∗ }.

Write z(η) = (x(η), s(η)) for its exact centers, with s(η) = −η −1 F ′ (x(η)) and gap ν/η. Distances
for Fb allow all curves in the ambient product interior; requiring intermediate affine feasibility can
only increase them. A dot denotes differentiation in λ = log η.
Proposition 24.1 (Classical allocation of central velocity). Put H = F ′′ (x), g = H −1/2 F ′ (x), and
let Π project orthogonally onto H 1/2 ker A. Then

Proof. Homogeneity and conjugacy give Hx = −F ′ (x), ∥g∥2 = ν, and F∗′′ (s) = η 2 H −1 . Differen-

D0 from the numerator’s distance bound, by the triangle inequality. The theorem says nothing
about unrestricted long moves, primal-only endpoints, or query counts.

24.2    A dimension envelope for arbitrary cone dictionaries
Theorem 24.3 (Dimension-only barrier charge). Let a compact full-dimensional body C ⊂ RD ,

deletion of zero factors, suppose all factors have dimension at most an integer d ≥ 2. Define

                                      Ψd (h) = 2⌊h/d⌋ + min{h mod d, 2}.

Every possibly coupled logarithmically homogeneous standard self-concordant barrier on this opera-

                                  ν ≥ Ψd (D + 1) ≥ 2(D + 1)/d.                              (24.5)
                           ν/2 in (24.4) is at least       Ψd (D + 1)/2.
                       p                               p
Thus the coefficient

                                                       112
Proof. The operational ambient dimension M is at least D + 1, by Theorem 13.4. This also follows
from the ordinary rank D + 1 of the body’s affine slack operator after the operational reductions
of Section 3. Let r0 factors be rays and q0 have dimension at least two. In each nonray factor a
two-dimensional linear section through an interior point is a proper planar cone, hence an orthant
after a linear change of coordinates. Their product with all the rays is an interior orthant section of
dimension r0 + 2q0 . Restriction of the barrier preserves its homogeneity degree and self-concordance,
so the sharp orthant lower bound gives ν ≥ r0 + 2q0 [53, 70]. This argument permits arbitrary
coupling. The cap gives r0 + dq0 ≥ M ≥ D + 1. For h = ad + b, 0 ≤ b < d, the minimum of r + 2q
over nonnegative integers with r + dq ≥ h is 2a + min(b, 2): for q ≤ a its minimum is h − (d − 2)q,
while q ≥ a + 1 costs at least 2a + 2. Finally min(b, 2) ≥ 2b/d.

     The envelope is an exact integer consequence of these two charges; it does not assert an attainable
lift frontier for every body. No definability or contact regularity is needed. For a product of balls it
uses D = a sa , whereas on a positively curved D-body with definable factors, Theorem 3.2 gives

     For completeness, these arc estimates give an explicit bounded-move schedule. Set L(η) =
 0    qeff (u) du/u and choose successive centers at increments of at most log(1 + R) in L, where
0 < R < 1. For a nonzero objective this scalar function is strictly increasing, so the parameters are
uniquely specified. The standard self-concordant distance comparison log(1 + ∥y − x∥x ) ≤ df (x, y)
implies that each endpoint chord has starting Dikin norm at most R; its distance is at most the
intervening arc length [72, Section 3]. The number of chords is at most ⌈LP / log(1 + R)⌉. Evaluation,
numerical inversion of L, reading weights, and materializing the centers have separate costs. This
is a mathematical movement schedule, not a running-time guarantee for computing exact real
parameters.
     As a concrete scale separation, take wa = 2−(a−1) and m = ⌈log2 (4/ϵ)⌉ ≤ k. The first
estimate gives LP (0, 2m/ϵ) = O(log3/2 (1/ϵ)) as ϵ ↓ 0. This is also the order of this central arc: on
2j−1 ≤ η ≤ 2j , 1 ≤ j ≤ m,
                                          j ≤ Q2 (η) ≤ j + 4/3.
                              √
Each interval contributes Θ( j), giving Θ(m3/2 ) up to 2m ; the remaining interval to 2m/ϵ contributes
    √
O( m log m) because qeff ≤ 2m there. The conclusion concerns central arclength and allows k to
grow with requested accuracy. Fixed finite k eventually has all its positive weights visible. These
distribution laws and their broader spectral versions are prior companion results [21, Section 5];
their role here is to prevent an ambient parameter or factor count from being substituted for the
actual primal motion of a chosen formulation.

Remark 24.5 (A pointwise obstruction to a parameter-only argument). Even Dikin containment,
determinant growth, and the gradient inequality alone do not supply the missing primal coefficient.
In the cube at xδ = (1 − δ)1, 0 < δ ≤ 1, put P = 11T /k and
                                       2       2                      1
                               Hδ =      2
                                           P + 2 (I − P ),     gδ =      .
                                      kδ      δ                       kδ
                                                                                                    √
Then (Hδ−1 )ii = δ 2 (1 − 1/(2k)), so its unit ellipsoid fits inside every cube facet, while ∥1∥Hδ = 2/δ,
det Hδ = 2k /(kδ 2k ), and gδT Hδ−1 gδ = 1/2. These pointwise data are not asserted to integrate to a
global barrier. The genuine product-ball examples above already disprove a uniform parameter-only
primal statement; the matrices explain why these weaker pointwise tests cannot rescue it. No
assertion about all barriers for uniformly nondegenerate objectives follows either way.


25     Movement in concrete affine formulations
The ambient cone count does not determine distance in the restricted barrier metric. We make this
distinction explicit for grouped balls, norm trees, balance slices, and shared spectral cones. The
barrier-height argument and the grouped, packed, and unweighted tree central paths below are also
proved in the companion manuscript [21]. We include proofs to make the comparisons self-contained.


                                                  115
The new all-objective distance refinement for trees is given in the next section. Throughout, distance
means the infimum of lengths of piecewise C 1 curves in the indicated open affine slice, with its
displayed Hessian metric; the distance to a target set is the infimum over its points. We write
[u]+ = max{u, 0}.

   Both estimates concern the whole accurate set, rather than the length of a selected central path.

In particular, for an integer m ≥ 1 and 0 < R < 1, at most m chords of local norm at most
R per round require at least [Dϵ + log(1 − ρ)]+ /[−m log(1 − R)] rounds. For M chords, where
M ≥ 1 is an integer, some rj is at least 1 − exp(−[Dϵ + log(1 − ρ)]+ /M ). These statements allow
arbitrary computation between the counted moves; they impose a geometric restriction on the
primal trajectory.

25.4    A separate control on central-path parameter changes
The endpoint bounds do not require accepted iterates to remain near a central path. When they do,
one also obtains a bound on each individual parameter increment. This retains a distinct refinement
of the grouped short-step analysis, and its proof applies to the tree profile as well.

26      Exact distance coefficients for weighted norm trees
The finite-accuracy estimate above is uniform over objectives. For a fixed objective, one can
determine the leading distance coefficient more precisely. An internal subtree on which the objective
vanishes contributes half its barrier weight to the squared coefficient. This contribution is invisible
to the rank of a nonzero exposing certificate, because the corresponding dual block is zero. It
nevertheless cannot be avoided: the subtree must shrink toward the vertex of its Lorentz cone.
    We use the norm-tree domain ΩT of Section 9. Write V for its internal nodes, b = |V |, and w
for its vector of leaf coordinates. Fix weights ωv ≥ 1 and the displayed barrier

refer to this Hessian, with the root fixed to one and all linking equations imposed. Fix a unit vector
c in the leaf space, put g(w) = 1 − cT w, and define

At an active node, tv tends to αv > 0 and ṫv = O(h). The same√holds for active child axes and

Starting the curve at any sufficiently large fixed λ0 , its length to λ is Kc λ + O(log λ). Connect
x0 to the starting point by an interior line segment, whose length is finite. Along the curve,
λ = log(1/g) + O(1), which proves the upper bound.

Corollary 26.2 (Central-path excess and independent √ products). For the weighted tree, the central
route from its analytic center top gap ϵ has length W log(1/ϵ) + O(1). Its ratio to the shortest
distance to that target tends to W/Kc . It is asymptotically optimal in leading coefficient exactly
when every internal subtree has nonzero objective support.
    For independent trees and the objective a λa cTa wa , with fixed λa > 0 and unit ca , the squared
                                            P
                       P
distance coefficient is a Kca . Independent factors with zero objective weight contribute zero.

Proof. The weighted central-path formulas of the preceding section give the first length. The ratio
follows from Theorem 26.1; Kc = W holds exactly when V0 is empty. For products, sum the lower
potentials. Each individual gap is at most ϵ/λa , which changes only the fixed remainder, and the
squared dual norms add. For the upper bound synchronize the parameter λ in (26.2) across all
positively weighted trees. The total objective gap is a fixed positive constant times e−λ (1 + o(1)),
and squared speeds add. Keep the remaining independent factors at their reference points.

    For example, take a tree with two internal nodes, with the objective supported entirely on a
root leaf. Forpunit weights, the root is active and its internal
                                                          √       child is inactive. The shortest-distance
coefficient is 3/2, whereas the central coefficient
                                             √         is   2. If the objective also has nonzero support
in the child subtree, both coefficients are 2. Thus the coefficient need not vary continuously as a
component of the objective tends to zero: the asymptotic here holds with the objective fixed before
accuracy tends to zero.
    The classical study of barrier metrics and central-path distances [69, 72], and the uniform
norm-tree estimates in Central-path companion [21], provide the context for this calculation. The
specific result here is the explicit weighted coefficient Kc , including objective-free subtrees, for this
fixed family of linked Lorentz barriers. It does not identify the best barrier on the tree domain, nor
assert an iteration lower bound without a restriction on the metric distance traversed per accepted
step.




                                                      124
Part VI
Resource ledgers and computational contracts
27     Exact resource ledgers and their optimization
Cone dimension, ambient barrier parameter, restricted barrier parameter, and free affine coordinates
measure different aspects of a formulation. This section records exact integer tradeoffs. Its
optimization statements concern the indicated resource models; none by itself bounds running time.
The lifts and their slack factorizations are those of Sections 5–9. Their interpretation uses the
factorization framework of Gouveia et al. [43]; the balancing and integer optimization arguments
below are elementary consequences of the curvature and nullity bounds proved here.

27.1    Real PSD order caps
Let N ≥ 4 and R ≥ 2 be integers, and consider exact lifts of BN  2 through real PSD blocks of orders
2 ≤ ri ≤ R, with globally bi-C 1 full-slack contact selections. Put
                 cr = ⌊r2 /4⌋,      dr = r(r + 1)/2,             M=                 ρ=
                                                                      X                  X

                                                                       i                 i

The ambient product cone has optimal barrier parameter ρ, including among coupled barriers. This
does not identify the parameter after its affine equations are imposed. By Corollary 7.6,
                                                                                                    (27.1)

                                                  i

                                                       125
Proof. The count formula is immediate. Extend cr to nonnegative integer r. Its increments
cr − cr−1 = ⌊r/2⌋ are nondecreasing. Moving one unit from a smaller positive order to a larger
order below R therefore increases or preserves capacity. Repeating, a list of total order S = ℓR + j,
0 ≤ j < R, has capacity at most ℓcR + cj . This relaxation permits zero and one as orders, which
only
   √ strengthens its use as an upper bound on capacity. The smallest order with cr ≥ t > 0 is
⌈2 t⌉. Maximal blocks and this last block attain (27.3); the capacity bound just proved excludes
every smaller total order. Selecting the first block of an optimal multiset proves both inequalities
in (27.4).
    For r = 2j or 2j + 1 one has dr /cr = 2 + 1/j, whereas cr /r increases with r. These give the
cap-dependent bounds in (27.5). Also N ≤ i ri2 /4 ≤ ρ2 /4 and dr = 2cr + ⌈r/2⌉, proving the other
                                             P

two bounds. For R = 4, orders two and three cost three per unit of capacity; order four costs ten for
four units. Using the maximal possible number of complete four-unit blocks and paying three per
remaining unit is optimal; overshooting by another four-unit block costs ten rather than at most
nine. For R = 5, the ratio lower bound is 5/2. Every even integer at least four is a nonnegative
combination of four and six, realized by orders four and five at that ratio. For odd N ≥ 5, add one
order-two block to a realization of N − 1. This attains the ceiling.

For the fixed complex order-two dictionary, the corresponding triple is

                                (⌈N/2⌉, 4⌈N/2⌉, 2⌈N/2⌉)      (N ≥ 4);                          (27.9)

for the fixed quaternionic order-two dictionary it is

                                (⌈N/4⌉, 6⌈N/4⌉, 2⌈N/4⌉)      (N ≥ 6).

The direct exceptions are (1, 4, 2) for complex N = 3 and (1, 6, 2) for quaternionic N = 5. Indeed
these cones are Q4 , Q6 , with capacities two and four; the strict capacity increment and fixed-tail
construction of Section 7 give the claims. Dimensions here count full dictionary cones even for the
final partially filled coordinate group.

Let C = (Bs2 )b , where b ≥ 2 and s ≥ 3 are integers. Choose an integer 1 ≤ p ≤ s and split each

Every column belongs to one source group and appears once.

Proposition 27.2 (Exact finite packing ledger). Write H = qℓ + t, 0 ≤ t < ℓ. For fixed p, ℓ,
balanced column counts minimize maximum order, ambient cone dimension M , and free coordinate
count V . The resulting ledger is

       L = ℓ,                            rmax = p + ⌈H/ℓ⌉,                      ρ = pℓ + H,
       M = (ℓ − t)dp+q + tdp+q+1 ,         V = bs + (ℓ − t)dq + tdq+1 ,      νslice = H.      (27.11)

The last equality is the exact parameter of the restricted standard barrier; in fact the fixed affine
domain has intrinsic parameter H. The count V precedes the b independent allocation equations.
Under an order cap R, all undominated resource vectors of this fixed-width family are obtained by
enumerating
                          1 ≤ p ≤ min(s, R − 1),     ⌈H/(R − p)⌉ ≤ ℓ ≤ H                      (27.12)
and removing dominated vectors.

Proof. Schur complementation gives Dj = Sj − WjT Wj ⪰ 0. The allocation identities imply each
source has squared norm at most one. Conversely any source points in the balls admit diagonal
residuals with the required nonnegative sums. This proves exactness. Moving a column from a block
with at least two more columns to a smaller block decreases both dp+c and dc sums, and cannot
increase maximum order. This proves balancing and all counts except the barrier equality.
    The barrier − j log det Dj has parameter at most j cj = H by the matrix-epigraph calculation
                  P                                   P

in Theorem 9.6. Project the closed slice onto all Wj and all diagonal entries of Sj . Its image
is exactly the grouped domain of Theorem 9.4, with H nonempty groups. Fibers are bounded:
|Duv |2 ≤ Duu Dvv and each diagonal residual is at most one. There is a strictly feasible point at
W = 0 and positive allocation to every column. Lemma 9.5 therefore gives an intrinsic lower bound
H, proving equality. Finally, balanced maximum order is at most R exactly in (27.12); widths p > s
only add public padding and resources.

bhS , bhS , bs + bhS ) and balanced dimension from (27.7), summed over sources. They weakly improve

(2bs/D, 2bs/ D, 2 2bs/ D, 2bs). Cross-source sharing, rather than scalar-coordinate economy,
is the packing construction’s distinctive advantage in this comparison.

                                                                   √         (g + 1)(g + 2)

The first two resources times ν decrease with g, whereas the last has its unique integer minimum
at g = 4, if available, with value 15A3/2 /8. To verify this, consecutive values decrease exactly when
(g + 1)5 − g 3 (g + 3)2 = −g 4 + g 3 + 10g 2 + 5g + 1 ≥ 0; this holds for g = 1, 2, 3 and fails thereafter.
The polynomial is negative at four and has negative derivative thereafter. Heterogeneous groups
cannot improve the value when 4 | s: at fixed mean width u, convexity balances widths between
two neighboring integers. The interpolated cost is au + b with a > 0 and b ≤ 0, so the derivative
of (au + b)u−3/2 changes sign, if at all, from positive to negative. There is no interior minimum.
Its minimum is therefore at an integer width, where the preceding comparison applies. If four is
unavailable, the largest allowed width below four is optimal in the divisible model; all remaining
cases are handled by the exact integer ledger.


28     When geometric movement composes with computational work
The resource inequalities in Section 27 become work lower bounds only under an explicit cost
premise. The point requiring proof is that the rank paying for curvature is also the rank paying for
movement. A larger ambient or restricted barrier parameter cannot be substituted for that rank in
a movement lower bound.

28.3    Attainment in specified output models
For homogeneous source dimensions s suppose the integers allow p = 3R/5, c = 2R/5, h = s/p and

                                       h2 + τ 2 + h                                          h2 + τ 2 + h

                                       √      25 5 (bs)3/2

movement order with the resource ratio (s/(s − 1))3/2 to (28.5). The instance-dependent reference
scale in that theorem is retained; no uniform scale over all lifts is asserted.
    For a concrete dense-output upper cost, preprocess the fixed objective columns Vj and their
Gram matrices Gj = VjT Vj once. At each checkpoint the entire lifted point is

                                   Wj = rVj ,             Sj = r2 Gj + qIc .

Writing all ambient cone entries therefore takes O(M ) exact real arithmetic operations and writes
per checkpoint, including the fixed identity blocks if the output contract requires them. This proves
a matching postprocessing and serialization bound for that path, with preprocessing and scalar
evaluation charged separately. It does not require a fresh Gram computation at every round. An
explicit bounded-distance
                      √      schedule also avoids a hidden optimization subproblem:
                                                                           √              from τ = 0 to h,
use increments O(h/ H); subsequently use constant √       increments  O(1/    H)  in  log τ until τ = H/ε.

          p log-parameter speed is H(1 − h/ h + τ ) ≤ H, and before h the ordinary speed is
                                                      2    2

at most H/2/h. Also b(1 − r(τ )) ≤ H/τ . This verifies both its step budget and its endpoint
accuracy. Square-root and exponential evaluations, or their chosen precision implementations, are
separate scalar costs; there is no hidden dense linear solve here.
    A charge of one record per factor (J0 ), or ri records per factor (J1 ), is a different output model.
If those records consist of the public scalars and references to preprocessed fixed directions and Gram
matrices, they can be refreshed in O(J0 ) or O(J1 ) word operations. Equation (28.7) then describes
matching charged record work for this representation. It does not claim that all bs projected

                                                          132
coordinates, or all dense factors, can be produced in that cost. Conversely, the full-coordinate model
really does require fresh writes of every output entry in every counted round. Counting only free
coordinates instead leads to (27.13), whose optimizer is grouped Schur rather than 3 : 2 packing.
    Finally, none of these fresh charges follows from one-shot query complexity. Fixed input data
can be preprocessed and reused, and public constant cone blocks can be added without changing
                                             √
the problem. For example, let vaj = σaj / s with hidden signs σaj ∈ {−1, 1}. One phase-oracle
query prepares
                                             1 X
                                           √        σaj |a, j⟩.
                                             bs a,j
Every nonzero projected center and projected predictor on the displayed path is a public scalar
multiple of this same vector. Fresh preparation costs one hidden query per checkpoint, independently
of the number of PSD factors; it does not cost Ω(L) queries. This says nothing about auxiliary
Gram-state preparation, final classical readout, or other solver subroutines. It demonstrates why a
one-shot lower bound cannot be multiplied by a movement count without a further temporal or
output premise.


29     Newton systems: elimination, packing, and access
Cone resources do not determine the cost of a Newton equation. The projected variables, the
auxiliary iterate, the chosen elimination, and the requested output all matter. We establish this point
for the grouped and column-packed ball formulations. In particular, feasible auxiliary allocations
improve the off-center comparison from a quadratic to a sharp linear dependence on residual
eccentricity.

29.1    The common marginal barrier and its Newton equation
Let C = ba=1 B2sa , let n = a sa , and partition each source vector xa into ha ≥ 1 nonempty
          Q                    P

coordinate groups. Pad a group by public zero coordinates when necessary, and arrange the resulting
columns in real matrices Wℓ ∈ Rpℓ ×cℓ . Every projected coordinate occurs once. Let Eaℓ be the
diagonal projector onto the columns of block ℓ assigned to source a. These projectors are orthogonal
and sum to the identity in each block; source a has ha columns in total. The packed formulation is
                                !
                      Sℓ WℓT
               Zℓ =                 ≻ 0,           tr(Eaℓ Sℓ ) = 1,         F =−         log det Zℓ .   (29.1)
                                               X                                     X
                      Wℓ Ip ℓ
                                               ℓ                                     ℓ

All additional constraints and the objective in this subsection depend only on x. Write Dℓ =
Sℓ − WℓT Wℓ and ρa = 1 − ∥xa ∥2 > 0.
Proposition 29.1 (Packing-independent marginal and centered system). For fixed x ∈ int C, the
unique minimum of F on its auxiliary fiber has diagonal residuals

                             (Dℓ0 )γγ = da := ρa /ha          (γ assigned to a).                        (29.2)

                                           a       ρa            ρ2a

                                                        P
Its self-concordant barrier parameter is exactly a ha . The grouped paraboloid formulation with the
same groups has the same marginal. At their fiber centers, both formulations give the same projected
Newton matrix and right-hand side for every common affine constraint Ax = d, linear objective,
multiplier, and projected iterate.

Proof. The Schur determinant identity gives det Zℓ = det Dℓ . For fixed diagonal, Hadamard’s
inequality uniquely maximizes the determinant at a diagonal positive matrix. The allocation
equations become γ∈Γa dγ = ρa ; AM–GM uniquely maximizes their product at dγ = ρa /ha . The
                      P

same AM–GM calculation applies to the grouped slacks tγ − ∥xγ ∥2 . Differentiation gives (29.4).
Each summand is ha times the standard unit-ball barrier, and its squared dual gradient norm is
2ha ∥xa ∥2 /(1 + ∥xa ∥2 ). This tends to ha at the boundary. Additivity and simultaneous boundary
approach prove exactness of the parameter.
     For completeness, the equality extends to the uncondensed residual equations. For a direction
(Aℓ , Uℓ ) = (δSℓ , δWℓ ) put Bℓ = Aℓ − WℓT Uℓ − UℓT Wℓ . Direct differentiation along this affine direction
gives                                     Xh                                          i
                        D2 F [(A, U )]2 =    tr(Dℓ−1 Bℓ Dℓ−1 Bℓ ) + 2 tr(Dℓ−1 UℓT Uℓ ) .              (29.5)
                                           ℓ

At diagonal D0 , off-diagonal entries of B are independent positive quadratic modes. They have zero
gradient, objective, and constraint coefficients. The diagonal modes rγ = Bγγ obey

                           X rγ2       X ∥uγ ∥2
                                    +2                               (rγ + 2xTγ uγ ) = 0.            (29.6)
                                                              X
                                                    ,
                            γ   d2γ    γ       dγ             γ∈Γa

These are precisely the grouped residual equations, including their linear terms − γ rγ /dγ and the
                                                                                            P

common objective. Additional rows Au = 0 coincide. Eliminating r and the allocation multipliers
therefore gives the same matrix and right-hand side.

    With no additional affine coupling, the graph of (29.6), with one allocation multiplier per source,
is a disjoint union of stars. Solving these stars, or applying the diagonal-plus-rank-one inverse in
(29.4), costs O(n + a ha ) = O(n) arithmetic. The graph of a fully materialized generic Hessian is
                    P

instead a union of sa -cliques. Both descriptions are correct; they measure different representations
of the same operator. Adding constraint hubs for A gives a graph G on projected variables, source
hubs, and constraint hubs. Attaching the rγ leaves gives width max{1, tw(G)}; this graph, rather
than the PSD packing layout, controls the corresponding sparse solve.

Corollary 29.2 (Matched projected-oracle equivalence). Supply both formulations with the same
complete canonical oracles for their common projected data and reduced Newton equation, at the
same precision. Require the same projected direction, or its normalized quantum state when the
direction is nonzero. Then their minimum classical and quantum query complexities agree exactly.
This equivalence also holds along any adaptive procedure that uses only these projected records and
recenters the auxiliary fibers before each Newton call.

Proof. Proposition 29.1 identifies both the operator and the right-hand side, in the same coordinates.
Forward each query to the identical oracle; retain all other operations, measurements, and stopping
decisions. The simulation works in both directions. Induction on calls gives the adaptive statement.
A common charged compiler from common raw data likewise gives identical preparation work.

   Here “same oracle” includes its action on unused input and ancilla subspaces. Equality of an
encoded matrix block alone would permit a unitary completion to reveal extra information. Thus


                                                        134
the corollary is an exact interface statement, not an oracle construction at no cost. It preserves
normalization, spectrum, right-hand-side norm, solution overlap, and output accuracy. In residual
coordinates the centered PSD KKT system is the grouped system direct-summed with a positive
diagonal off-diagonal block and a zero right-hand side; public zero padding also identifies those
solution states. The original δS output is different: reconstructing it requires W T U + U T W . Its cost
is not covered by the projected equivalence. If all ha = h, changing h multiplies the unconstrained
marginal Hessian by h. It changes its metric scale but not its condition number or normalized inverse
state for a fixed right-hand side. This illustrates why fewer cone factors alone do not establish a
state-generation advantage.

29.2    A sharp comparison away from the fiber center

29.3    What exact recentering requires
The center has an inexpensive implicit representation. Store (W, d) with the semantics Sℓ =
WℓT Wℓ + Diag(dℓ ). Computing all squared source norms and all labels dγ = ρa /ha uses O(n)
arithmetic and storage, independently of packing, because a ha ≤ n. The source equations hold
                                                               P

exactly by ∥xa ∥2 + ha da = 1. For B-bit common-denominator dyadic coordinates the squared norms
and labels are exact rationals of O(B + log n) bits. Fast integer arithmetic gives the conservative
bound O(nM(B
        e        + log n)), where M(t) is the cost of multiplying t-bit integers. This represents the
exact center of the stored interior rational iterate. It does not require subtracting rounded Gram
matrices. Unrelated rational denominators, inverse evaluation, and requested numerical accuracy
have their own bit costs.
    A reversible value oracle for ρa similarly supplies dγ with one scale query and reversible division
by the public ha . Together with blockwise direction-state access, it permits the common rank-one
compiler for (29.4); radii and certified normalization bounds must be supplied or computed. In
particular normalized state access to xa alone does not provide this scale. The same complete
state-preparation unitary can prepare the direction of r0 v and r1 v, while their 1 − rj2 differ by
arbitrarily large factors. No algorithm with only that unitary can return both scales to a fixed
relative accuracy.

Proposition 29.6 (Cost of missing radial metadata). For coordinate-bit access to a source of
                                                                                                 √
dimension s ≥ 2, producing a classical center scale to relative error δ < 1/3 can require Ω( s)
quantum or Ω(s) randomized queries. For b independent sources, returning all scales with joint
                                  √
success at least 2/3 requires Ω(b s) and Ω(bs), respectively. These orders are attained on the
promise used in the proof. For k | s, 1 ≤ k ≤ s/2, the one-source promise can instead have smaller
slack ρ = k/s, with tight costs Θ(ρ−1/2 ) quantum and Θ(ρ−1 ) randomized.
                      √
Proof. Take xi = zi / s with z1 = 0 public and all other bits one, except for at most one additional
hidden zero. The slack is 1/s or 2/s. Their relative-δ intervals are disjoint precisely when δ < 1/3,
so recovering the scale solves promised unstructured search. The standard search bounds apply
[14, 18].
    For clarity, the quantum direct sum has a short adversary proof. Index one source’s promise
                                                                                              √ by
the no-mark input and its s − 1 single-mark inputs. The star adjacency matrix Γ has norm s − 1,
and filtering √by any queried bit leaves norm one. For b sources use ba=1 I ⊗(a−1) ⊗ Γ ⊗ I ⊗(b−a) .
                                                                         P

Its norm is b s − 1; filtering a query in one source retains only that summand. Its entries vanish
between inputs having the same output vector. The adversary lower bound for function evaluation
[59] gives the claim. For randomized algorithms, give each source an independent fair no-mark/single-
uniform-mark promise. Joint success implies constant average success for each output, and the usual
search indistinguishability argument requires a constant fraction of the s − 1 expected queries per
such source; summing proves Ω(bs). On this promise, exact amplitude amplification for the known


                                                 137
                                                                                       √
single-mark case followed by verification distinguishes zero from one mark with O( s) queries and
no error. Applied sourcewise it attains joint success without an amplification logarithm. Scanning
attains the randomized order.
    Finally partition coordinates into blocks of size k. One public block is zero; all candidate blocks
are one except possibly one hidden zero block. The two slacks are k/s and 2k/s. A representative
coordinate is an oracle for its block mark, reducing to search on s/k − 1 labels. This number is
within a constant factor of s/k in the stated range.

    The joint-output bound also applies to a standalone compiled value interface whose output can
be read on all labels with the same joint correctness guarantee and with no further coordinate-input
queries. It does not describe one online coherent evaluation: the promised search can be controlled
                          √
by the source label at O( s) input queries per evaluation. Those queries remain charged on every
invocation. The following fresh update family supplies a temporal bound under an explicit output
contract.
    A compile-and-commit maintainer returns, before the next epoch, either a classical record of all
source scales or a complete reusable unitary for their encoded numerical values. The latter must
allow every basis-label value to be copied out, using the unitary and its inverse, with no further
raw-update queries and with the entire backing workspace restored exactly, including correlations
with other retained registers. More precisely, each committed object has fixed value strings va ;
compute–copy–uncompute on label a acts as

                                       |a, z⟩|ψ⟩ 7−→ |a, z ⊕ va ⟩|ψ⟩

on its backing state and any retained reference. The strings encode numbers dba . Correctness means
that, with joint probability at least 2/3, all committed values at all epochs obey |dba − da | ≤ δda . A
state consumed or disturbed by reading its values does not meet this contract. Rational encodings
allow exact values even for δ = 0; fixed-point encodings require enough bits for the requested positive
error.

Theorem 29.7 (Fresh-update scale maintenance). Let b, T ≥ 1, s ≥ 2, and 0 ≤ δ < 1/3. For the
packing (29.1) with b sources of dimension s and public column counts ha , there is a family of T
fresh coordinate-bit update batches, each zero or one-sparse per source, on which compile-and-commit
maintenance has tight worst-case raw-query costs
                                              √
                                   Q = Θ(T b s),        R = Θ(T bs)                           (29.14)

for quantum and randomized algorithms, respectively. Each epoch starts from the same public
interior base and is reset after commitment. For k | s, 1 ≤ k ≤ s/2, a replicated-block version has
k-sparse updates and smaller slack ρ∗ = k/s, with costs
                                             −1/2
                                 Q = Θ(T bρ∗        ),     R = Θ(T bρ−1
                                                                     ∗ ).                         (29.15)

The lower bounds allow all epoch oracles from the start, superposed queries over epoch, source, and
coordinate, and arbitrary persistent workspace. The upper bounds have zero error on these promises.
                                  √
Proof. Use x̄ = (0, 1, . . . , 1)/ s. Independently for each pair (t, a), the update is either ut,a = 0 or
            √
ut,a = −ej / s at one hidden j ∈ {2, . . . , s}. The raw oracle returns the corresponding mark bit at
a queried coordinate. After the update,

                        ρt,a = 1 − ∥x̄ + ut,a ∥2 ∈ {1/s, 2/s},     dt,a = ρt,a /ha .


                                                     138
Both cases are strictly interior, and Proposition 29.1 gives these exact PSD fiber-center labels. Their
relative-error intervals are disjoint because 1 + δ < 2(1 − δ). Undo each batch after commitment;
inverse-update access uses the same bits and reveals no new information. A public reset only
strengthens the model.
    Read every committed label before continuing the maintainer, restoring its workspace in the
quantum case. Thresholding recovers m = T b independent presence bits with joint success 2/3
and no extra raw queries. Apply the star adversary √      from Proposition 29.6, with tensor factors
now indexed by (t, a). Its Kronecker sum has norm m s − 1 and every coordinate filter has norm
one. It is entrywise nonnegative and vanishes between inputs with the same output vector, so
the finite-output spectral adversary bound applies [2, Theorems 3–4]. The proof already uses
simultaneous oracle access and allows cross-epoch query scheduling.
    For the randomized bound, independently choose no mark with probability 1/2 and a uniform
single mark otherwise. Fix the randomness and all other pairs, and let L count distinct candidates
queried when this pair has no mark. The no-mark and random-mark transcripts coincide unless
the mark lies in these L locations. Averaging over the fixed data, the total variation distance is
at most EL/(s − 1). Marginal success at least 2/3 forces EL ≥ (s − 1)/3. Since the no-mark case
has probability 1/2, this pair contributes at least (s − 1)/6 expected queries under the product
distribution. Summing over the m pairs proves the worst-case randomized bound.
    Exact amplitude amplification designed for one marked coordinate, followed by verification,
                                    √
decides zero versus one mark in O( s) queries [18, Theorems 4 and 16]. Verification always rejects in
the zero-mark case. Search each pair and store its exact rational scale; no joint-success amplification
logarithm is needed. Scanning gives the classical upper bound.
    For the margin version, partition coordinates into s/k blocks of size k, with one public zero block
in the base. An update zeros either no candidate block or one whole candidate block. The slacks
are k/s and 2k/s. A coordinate query reveals only its block’s mark bit, and one representative
coordinate per block suffices. Replace s − 1 throughout by s/k − 1 = Θ(s/k), which is at least one
in the stated range.

integer budgets rt , the tight worst-case costs are

Proof. Choose the permitted client that reads min{rt , b} distinct basis labels at each epoch. Fix
all other update pairs to zero. Its transcript recovers t min{rt , b} independent search bits, so the
                                                       P

same adversary and product-distribution proofs give the lower bounds. For the upper bound, if

                                                        139
    This universal-service contract is stronger than a particular downstream algorithm’s structured
calls. One online coherent evaluation has no b factor; a service supporting at least b arbitrary
calls reaches the compilation cost. Neither result concerns arbitrary one-shot state output or
finite-precision gate complexity.
    For an explicit sparse list, the exact identity

                               ρa (x + αu) = ρa (x) − 2αxTa ua − α2 ∥ua ∥2                             (29.17)

instead maintains scales in O( a | supp ua |) arithmetic, given access to the current coordinates and
                                P

a list of affected sources. In particular one-sparse lists cost O(b) per epoch. The lower bound charges
discovery of hidden support, not sparsity itself. Nor does a snapshot lower bound imply a factor T
for one fixed oracle: if every round requests the same promised OR bit, compute and cache it once
       √
in Θ( s) quantum or Θ(s) randomized queries. All T subsequent requests then use no raw queries.

Proof. Stationarity is 2ha xa /ρa + ηca = 0. Substitution in ρa = 1 − ∥xa ∥2 gives ρa + τa2 ρ2a /4 = 1;
its positive solution is (29.18), also when qa = 0 or η = 0. It is interior, and strict convexity of Φ

    Thus supplied reversible access to the fixed norms qa evaluates a requested scale with O(1) norm
queries and charged scalar arithmetic; uncomputing norm scratch may require an inverse query.
Explicit objective data permits all norms to be acquired once in O(n) real arithmetic, including
square roots, and reused for every η. Requested precision and reversible arithmetic still have their
own costs. Additional affine constraints or inexact neighborhood iterates need not satisfy this
stationarity equation. The fresh batches in Theorem 29.7 have not been embedded in the Newton
trajectory of one fixed conic program: such an embedding and a contract recovering its independent
outputs would be needed to multiply a query lower bound by an iteration count. The closed-form
uncoupled path witnesses reuse of fixed data, and supplies no such embedding.
    Explicit dense auxiliaries have a different output cost: Ω( ℓ c2ℓ ) scalar writes and ordinary Gram
                                                               P

formation cost O( ℓ pℓ c2ℓ ). An implicit ambient encoding can avoid these writes: put Kℓ = (Wℓ Ipℓ )
                   P

and write Zℓ = KℓT Kℓ + Diag(dℓ , 0). Products of a supplied αK -normalized encoding of Kℓ and its
adjoint, followed by a positive linear combination with an αd -normalized diagonal encoding, give
normalization αK 2 + α . This costs a constant number of supplied encoding calls. Constructing
                        d
these encodings, their norms, and an inverse remains charged; no statement about a flattened
Gram-matrix output state follows.

29.4    Sparse augmented Lorentz systems
Low-rank augmentation of cone Hessians is classical, including in ECOS and nonsymmetric-cone
implementations [23, 27]. We use it to make the classical comparator explicit for the formulations
above. A graph below means the fixed structural graph of the augmented scalar matrix; special
numerical zeros may delete edges. Its treewidth is the minimum, over tree decompositions, of the
largest bag size minus one.

                                                  140
   For a Lorentz block x = (t, z), let q = t2 − ∥z∥2 > 0 and J = diag(1, −I). Its standard Hessian is

                                       2J   4(Jx)(Jx)T

                                        q       q2

the augmented right-hand side is (gx , 0, gy ). There is no assumption of a positive diagonal in D.

Proposition 29.10 (Exact field-operation comparison). Suppose A ∈ Rp×M has full row rank,
there are k Lorentz blocks, and a compact width-τ tree decomposition of the structural graph of K
                                                                                                f0
is supplied. Then a Newton equation can be solved in O((M + p + k)(τ + 1) ) exact field operations,
                                                                          2

after O(M + nnz A) coefficient formation. Compactness here means O(M + p + k) bags; processing
a larger supplied decomposition is an additional input cost.

Proof. Positive definiteness of H and full row rank of A imply nonsingularity of both matrices in
(29.20). Replace each vertex of each bag by its row and column copies. The resulting decomposition
of the matrix’s row–column bipartite graph has width at most 2τ + 1: every off-diagonal entry
has both endpoints in some original bag, each diagonal entry is covered, and connectedness of
each copy’s bags is preserved. Apply the exact low-treewidth linear-system algorithm of Fürer
et al. [39, Corollary 3]. Its bound is O((m + n)(w + 1)2 ) for a supplied compact width-w bipartite
decomposition. It allows nonzero pivots chosen during elimination and algebraic cancellations, so the
zero or negative diagonal entries here cause no difficulty. Formula (29.19) uses only field operations
and gives the formation cost.

   A positive-diagonal version is useful when a reusable symmetric factorization is wanted. If
r = ∥z∥ > 0 and e = z/r, put

Grouping (x, V ) as positive variables and (U, y) as negative variables shows symmetric quasidefinite-

Every symmetric permutation therefore admits an exact pivot-free LDLT factorization [86]. A
supplied width-τ elimination order gives O((M +p+k)(τ +1)2 ) real arithmetic, O((M +p+k)(τ +1))

the field-operation result.

                                                            141
   This is not an unconditional numerical stability statement. Writing Kϖ = K0 − ϖ diag(0, I),
the resolvent identity gives, for d0 = K0−1 g ̸= 0 and ϖ∥K0−1 ∥2 < 1,
                                     −1 g − d ∥
                                   ∥Kϖ       0      ϖ∥K0−1 ∥2
                                                ≤               .                               (29.23)
                                      ∥d0 ∥       1 − ϖ∥K0−1 ∥2
Working precision, small pivots, growth, and residual certification still need bounds. Quasidefiniteness
rules out exact zero-pivot breakdown, not arbitrarily small pivots. Also, two hubs cannot generically
be reduced to one while retaining a positive diagonal base: when m ≥ 3 and every coordinate of Jx
is nonzero, off-diagonal consistency in H = D+ + ϵaaT forces ϵ = 1 and a2i = 4(Jx)2i /q 2 . The axial
entry of D+ is then −2/q. This only proves minimality within that diagonal-plus-one-signed-rank-one
class.
    The two-hub graph can be bounded from coarse incidence. Make one vertex for each cone and
each equality row, joining them when the row uses a coordinate of that cone. If this graph has
width w and each scalar column of A has at most one nonzero, the expanded graph has width at
most max{2w + 1, 3}. Replace every cone vertex in a coarse bag by its two hubs. For each used
coordinate attach a bag consisting of that coordinate, its two hubs, and its single row, to a bag
covering the corresponding coarse edge. For unused coordinates omit the row. These bags cover all
edges and preserve the connected-bag condition. Thus a coarse forest gives width at most three
regardless of cone dimension.

29.5    Exact graph counts for the ball constructions
For N projected coordinates grouped into k nonempty groups, set qj = sj − ∥wj ∥2 and impose
  j sj = 1. The reduced barrier has Hessian blocks
P


                              diag(0, 2I/qj ) + qj−2 (1, −2wj )(1, −2wj )T .                    (29.24)

Their one-hub graph has the paths λ − sj − hj and leaves hj − wji . It is a tree with N + 2k + 1
vertices and N + 2k edges. The zero sj diagonal prevents an unqualified scalar leaf-pivot claim;
Proposition 29.10 instead supplies the exact solve. Restricting (29.21) by (s, w) 7→ ((1 + s)/2, (1 −
s)/2, w) gives positive base diag(1/q, 2I/q) and a positive downdated base by congruence. The
two-hub reduced graph has N + 3k + 1 vertices, 2N + 3k edges, and width two. Bags containing the
two hubs and one coordinate, together with bags {λ, sj }, prove the upper bound; each nonempty
group supplies a cycle for the lower bound.
    For comparison, retain all raw Lorentz coordinates (uj , vj , wj ) and equations uj + vj = 1,
  j j − vj ) = 1. The exact structural counts are

                  reduced grouped, one hub        N + 2k + 1      N + 2k          1
                  reduced grouped, two hubs       N + 3k + 1     2N + 3k          2

To verify the raw widths, the one-hub graph has K2,3 on axial coordinates uj , vj and the hub, local
row, and global row. Bags {uj , vj , a} for these three vertices a, plus hub–coordinate leaf bags,
give width two. The two-hub graph has a subdivision of K4 : its branch vertices are the two hubs
and uj , vj ; a row connects the two axial vertices and one w coordinate connects the two hubs. A
width-three decomposition uses the four branch vertices as a core, the two axial vertices with the

                                                   142
local and global rows as another bag, and bags of the two hubs with each w coordinate. Different
groups join through the global row. Counting incidences gives the edge totals.
    The direct ball formulation (t, x) ∈ QN +1 , t = 1, has a one-hub tree with N + 3 vertices;
eliminating t gives a star with N + 1 vertices. A binary norm tree with L = N − 1 internal nodes
has 3L raw cone coordinates, L hubs, L − 1 link rows, and one root row. It is connected with 5L
vertices and 3L + 2(L − 1) + 1 = 5L − 1 edges, hence a tree. Its rows are independent because each
link or root equality uses its own cone-coordinate slots. These counts also explain why the smooth
coordinatewise construction (k = N ) has raw width two but reduced width one. Smooth contact
selections do not impose an intrinsic width-two cost.
    All these isolated-ball Newton equations have linear exact arithmetic cost. The ambient and
restricted barrier parameters and the movement bounds proved earlier remain different. Thus
replacing a large cone by many small cones can increase the standard short-step certificate and the
bounded-Dikin movement cost without improving this linear solve. For products of cone epigraphs,
fixing their axial coordinates to one reduces to products of balls; the earlier additive curvature
bound forces at least k(s − 1) copies of Q3 to represent Qks+1 , even under joint reformulation. Their
ambient product barrier has parameter at least 2k(s − 1) by its orthant section. Separate binary
trees attain the count. This compares the generic ambient-barrier certificate with the 2k certificate
for the direct product, not all algorithms’ iteration counts.

29.6    The full-output comparison and its boundary
The preceding exact solves give a precise replacement principle. Suppose an outer algorithm requests
a classical direction or updated iterate in every round, its theorem accepts any direction satisfying
a stated Newton residual contract, and the current coefficients and right-hand side are classically
evaluable at the cost charged to its quantum access model. If fixed compact decompositions of widths
τt are supplied for its expanded systems, replacing the quantum Newton module by Proposition 29.10
costs
                                        O((Mt + pt + kt )(τt + 1)2 )                          (29.25)
                                     X

                                    t

exact field operations, plus coefficient formation and any decomposition input cost. The outer
guarantee is preserved because the exact solution meets its residual contract. This need not
reproduce the quantum algorithm’s random trajectory; the comparison requires the outer guarantee
and the structural width bound for every admitted trajectory. Dense materialization already requires
                                                                          o(1)
Ω(Mt + pt ) scalar writes per round. When pt + kt = O(Mt ) and τt = Mt , the Newton module
therefore gives no polynomial dimensional saving under this matched full-output, exact-arithmetic
contract. The centered packed family and the grouped or norm-tree ball family have O(n) or O(N )
replacements. Dense external affine coupling can invalidate their width bounds. A finite-precision
replacement additionally needs a stable implementation and certification at the prescribed tolerance.
    There is a complementary classical route for sparse well-conditioned systems. For a consistent
full-column-rank system Kd = r and zero initial guess, conjugate gradients on its normal equations,
implemented by matrix–vector products as CGLS or LSQR [74], has the exact-arithmetic residual
estimate
                                                  κ2 (K) − 1 j
                                                            
                               ∥Kdj − r∥2 ≤ 2                  ∥r∥2 .
                                                  κ2 (K) + 1
This
q    follows from the usual CG energy-norm bound, because that norm is ∥K(dj − d∗ )∥2 and
  κ2 (K T K) = κ2 (K). If one pair of products by K, K T costs M, including current entry evaluation,
a relative residual 0 < ϵlin < 1 costs O(Mκ2 (K) log(2/ϵlin )). The case κ2 (K) = 1 terminates


                                                 143
after one step. No normal matrix need be materialized. Sparse bounded-size conic blocks fit this
comparison only when their actual augmented entries are cheaply available. Quantum-only encodings
and tall implicit Hessian formation are different access models; in particular this argument does not
replace the row-sampling work in Apers and Gribling [5].
    The classical algorithms, signed-rank augmentations, and search bounds used here are established
tools. The formulation-specific conclusions are the packing-independent centered equation, its sharp
feasible off-center transfer, and the exact access and output conditions under which the same classical
Newton work applies. Published quantum SDP and portfolio IPM analyses explicitly account for
conditioning, data access, and classical readout [7, 26]; the present comparison is confined to the
displayed structured families. In a block-diagonal SDP embedding the matrix order is the sum of
block orders, not their maximum. Substituting a block cap for that sum in a generic solver theorem
is therefore unjustified. State, sample, scalar, or coherent-iterate output can have different costs, as
the following query results demonstrate.


30     Query access and classical output
A formulation can have few cone factors, a simple Newton system, and an inexpensive normalized
quantum state, while its requested classical output still requires reading most of the input. We make
this distinction on the same instances used for the geometric results. The query ingredients are
classical: the polynomial method, search, approximate counting, and quantum information bounds
[11, 14, 18, 66, 67]. The contribution here is their combination with specified conic lifts, barriers,
and output contracts. We do not claim new Boolean query bounds.

30.1    Access conventions and an information bound
The input is a string b ∈ {0, 1}n , with σj = (−1)bj , accessed through the clean indexed oracle

                                         Ob |j, z⟩ = |j, z ⊕ bj ⟩.

Quantum and randomized query complexities below have worst-case success probability at least
2/3. Public arithmetic, magnitude preparation, index routing, numerical precision, and writing
the output are separate costs. An explicit classical vector means a list of real coordinates at the
accuracy specified, rather than a circuit, a sampling data structure, or an amplitude state. For a
nonzero real vector v, its amplitude state is |v⟩ = ∥v∥−1 j vj |j⟩ in an ordinary complex Hilbert
                                                          P

space.
    Whenever sample-and-query access is mentioned, it supplies coordinate queries, squared-
coordinate samples, and the specified global norm. Coherent preparation access has a fixed
completion Ub = Oσ Upub , with Oσ the diagonal sign action on specified slots. Both Ub and its
inverse then have constant raw-query cost. The action on all other slots is public. A reference slot
on which the oracle acts trivially may be added, so controlled phase queries and clean bit queries
are equivalent up to a constant. No free norms of hidden-dependent projections, free postselection
probabilities, or arbitrary hidden-dependent unitary completions are included.

Lemma 30.1 (Explicit approximate sign recovery). Suppose a T -query algorithm, on each σ ∈
{−1, 1}n , returns a classical string with at most αn errors with probability at least 2/3, where
0 ≤ α < 1/4 is fixed. Then T = Ωα (n).

verify the quantum lower bound, on one block use the adversary matrix joiningpall indices in H to
all in its complement. Its norm is s/2; masking by a single query gives norm s/2. For k blocks,

all terms except its own block, giving the same denominator. The spectral adversary bound [56]

number remaining, exact amplitude amplification uses O( ks/j) queries at the j-remaining stage,
                    √
whose sum is O(k s) [18]. This avoids a joint success penalty. For randomized algorithms under
independent uniform marks, a randomly chosen block, with all other blocks simulated for free, has
expected query cost T /k. Its half-location bit requires Ω(s) expected queries, hence T = Ω(ks).

advantage. These different output contracts explain why the linear arithmetic Newton solves do not
settle the query comparison.

    At Γ = N and fixed sufficiently small error, these explicit formulations thus combine √one cone
factor, Θ(N ) scalar or explicit output queries, constant-query projected states, and Ω( N log N )
bounded-movement rounds under the stated metric contracts. A model charging both resources
obtains their maximum as a lower bound. Multiplying them would require a separate theorem
forcing fresh query work at every round; these fixed-input families provide no such theorem. This is
consistent with quantum optimization analyses that separately charge data access, linear solving,
and classical output [5].


31     Acquiring an exact smaller formulation
The preceding formulation bounds concern a representation once it is known. Removing redundant
constraints can require information about the input. We give families in which this acquisition
cost, the resulting barrier, and its metric can all be calculated. The search bounds are classical
quantum-query results; the point is their precise connection with an exact conic reformulation. In
particular, an existential small barrier need not provide a cheap way to evaluate that barrier from
the supplied data.

31.1    Output and access conventions
A source query returns one indexed input symbol, either classically or coherently in the usual
reversible oracle. Public arithmetic and the preparation of index registers do not count as source
queries. Their gate and bit costs are separate. All bounded-error statements require success

                                                     151
probability at least 2/3 on the complete output, on every promised input. An exact reusable
compiler produces a finite classical description of the exact projected body, usable without further
source queries. It can, for example, return its retained inequalities and a barrier for their intersection.
A wrapper that continues calling the source oracle does not meet this output convention. Lower
bounds also cover a classical description with exact membership or boundary evaluation, provided
that this evaluation requires no further source queries. Computation using the description may be
arbitrarily expensive in a query lower bound.
Lemma 31.1 (Acquisition of independent marked positions). Let b ≥ 1 and m ≥ 2. In each of b
public blocks of m bits, exactly one bit is one. Returning all marked positions has quantum query
                √
complexity Θ(b m) and randomized query complexity Θ(bm).   √     If exactly k of N bits are marked,
with k known, all marks can be found with certainty in O( N k) quantum queries.

   p Exact amplitude amplification with a known number of marked positions finds a mark in
Proof.
O( n/t) queries when t > 0 of n positions are marked [18, Theorem 4]. Apply this separately        to
the blocks. For known total cardinality, suppress previously found indices and sum O( N/t) for
                                                                                            p

t = k, k − 1, . . . , 1. There is no accumulation of failure probabilities in these exact-query upper
bounds.
   For completeness, index block inputs by r ∈ [m]b and use the nonnegative adversary matrix
                                      b
                                 Γ=         I ⊗(j−1) ⊗ (Jm − Im ) ⊗ I ⊗(b−j) .

Its norm is b(m − 1). A query to position ℓ of block j retains only the jth summand,     √ replacing
Jm −Im by the adjacency matrix of the star joining
                                              √    ℓ to the other positions. Its norm is  m − 1. The
                                                               √
spectral adversary bound therefore gives Ω(b m − 1) = Ω(b m); this is the standard direct-sum
argument [2, Theorems 3–4]. Classically, under independent uniform marked positions, success
probability 2/3 for all outputs implies that probability for each individual position. Revealing the
other blocks in advance cannot make a uniform marked position in an unread block less uncertain.
A successful search in that block requires Ω(m) expected queries, even allowing a final unqueried
guess. Summing over blocks gives Ω(bm); scanning gives the matching upper bound.

   The exact quantum upper bounds use the standard query model, which permits the public
rotations needed for exact amplitude amplification. An approximate gate implementation must
separately account for its precision.

31.4    A growing-dimensional fixed-objective decoder
The distinction between body compilation and a fixed objective can be removed by allowing the
projected dimension to grow. Let N = km, m ≥ 2, and uℓ = ℓ/[2(m + 1)]. In block j exactly one
position ℓj is marked. In coordinates (y, pj , qj ) retain the public sentinel and the marked inequality

                   4(y 2 + p2j + qj2 ) ≤ 1,      y 2 + (1 + uℓj )2 p2j + (3 − uℓj )2 qj2 ≤ 1.      (31.7)

Before compilation there are m hidden factors in each block, each either the sentinel or the marked
factor, and one additional public sentinel. The coefficient matrices are rational with O(log m) bits,
and all factors are direct Q4 slices. The common y is the sole coupling between blocks.



                                                     155
Proposition 31.4 (Fixed-objective acquisition and metric separation). The body (31.7) has dimen-
sion 2k + 1 and 2k essential retained inequalities. It has a lift with Θ(N ) constant-size cone factors,
bounded row and column incidence, and bounded scalar KKT treewidth, all with bounds independent
of k, m. Both exact reusable compilation and a coordinate-readable solution of max j qj with
                                                                                            P
                                                                      √
coordinate error below 1/[40(m + 1)] require and suffice with Θ( N k) quantum queries or Θ(N )
randomized queries. The same lower bounds hold for an exactly feasible coordinate-readable solution
with total objective gap below 1/[40(m + 1)].
    For the separate objective max y, the natural and compiled explicit barriers have parameters of
orders N and k and central lengths, from their common center to y = 1/2 − ϵ,
                         √                    √            
                       Θ N log(1/ϵ) ,         Θ k log(1/ϵ)         (0 < ϵ ≤ 1/4).

Proof. The origin is strict. To make the marked factor of block j alone tight, set y = pj = 0,
qj = (3 − uℓj )−1 , and all other coordinates zero. Its sentinel is strict since 3 − uℓj > 5/2. To make
its sentinel alone tight, take y = qj = 0, pj = 1/2; the marked factor is strict since 1 + uℓj < 3/2.
These points also prove essentiality after small outward perturbations. For bounded incidence,
give every slot a triple copy and join triples along paths within blocks. Connect representative y
copies between blocks along a path. A constant-size bag for consecutive copies, their multipliers,
and each local cone block gives a tree decomposition; at block junctions only a constant number
of representative coordinates need be retained. This also proves the assertion for the scalar KKT
graph, including dense local cone Hessians.
    For the decoding objective, every feasible point has qj ≤ (3 − uℓj )−1 . All these bounds are
attained simultaneously only at

                                 y = 0,   pj = 0,      qj∗ = (3 − uℓj )−1 .

The optimum is unique. Neighboring candidate qj∗ values differ by more than 1/[18(m + 1)]. A
coordinate error below 1/[40(m + 1)] is less than half that spacing, so rounding recovers every
mark. For a feasible point the deficits qj∗ − qj are nonnegative; a total objective gap below the
same threshold bounds each deficit. Lemma 31.1 proves the lower bounds and the acquisition upper
bounds. Once the marks are known, the exact optimizer takes O(k) arithmetic operations. The
conclusion is for readable coordinates, not the objective value alone or a normalized quantum state.
    For max y, reflection symmetry gives pj = qj = 0 on both central paths. Write fA (y) =
− log(1 − Ay 2 ). Their restrictions are exactly

                      Fo (y) = N f4 (y) + kf1 (y),         Fc (y) = kf4 (y) + kf1 (y).
                                                             q
The term f1′′ is uniformly bounded on [0, 1/2), whereas f4′′ has order (1/2 − y)−1 near the endpoint.
Integration proves the length assertions. The squared gradient dual norm on this axis tends to N
or k, respectively, while additivity gives upper parameters N + k and 2k. The diagonal symmetry
removes mixed Hessian entries on the axis, so these are also lower bounds for the full barriers.

    The post-compilation problem is only coupled through one scalar. Its explicit decoding objective
is easy once the hidden constraints are known. This is an acquisition advantage; it does not require
a quantum linear-system solver.

31.5    Blockwise relative-entropy compilation
The same acquisition phenomenon occurs for nonsymmetric cones. Use D(U ∥V ) = U log(U/V ),
with its closed perspective extension. Let N = Bm, B ≥ 1, m ≥ 2, and choose public values

                                                     156
γ1 < · · · < γm in [1, 2] with minimum spacing h ≍ m−1 . Each block has exactly one marked position
rj . Set dji = 1 except djrj = e−γrj . An oracle query returns the mark bit; the coefficient at a
marked position is a public function of that position. Coefficients may be retained symbolically, so
no exact transcendental-number encoding is hidden in this query convention. All source terms in
block j share Uj , Vj .
     The elementary identity (21.7) becomes
