One can equivalently retain the rational/public coefficient γrj in the middle expression. Projection

Proposition 31.5 (Entropy acquisition and matched-gap lengths). An exact reusable compiler for
these B aggregate epigraphs has query complexities
                               √         √
                      Q = Θ(B m) = Θ( N B),        R = Θ(Bm) = Θ(N ).

the natural and compiled projected central points coincide. Between gaps ∆0 > ϵ > 0 their exact

for their respective displayed product barriers.

Proof. The projected epigraph in block j, evaluated at Uj = Vj = 1, has lower boundary tj = γrj .
Hence an exact reusable description determines all rj ; exact membership at the public midpoints
between candidate boundary values suffices. Lemma 31.1 gives both bounds and the upper algorithm.
For the solution task, feasibility gives ej = tj − γrj ≥ 0. Total excess below h/3 implies ej < h/3 for
every j, and nearest-value rounding recovers all marks.
    With Uj = Vj = 1, the usual relative-entropy cone barrier restricts to − log(tji − D(1∥dji )), up
to a constant. At multiplier η > 0, all N source slacks equal 1/η, so their total gap is N/η and
each projected block slack is m/η. The compiled barrier has B slacks, each 1/ηb, total gap B/ηb. At
η = N/∆ and ηb = B/∆, both project to tj = γrj + ∆/B. A positive orthant logarithmic barrier has
line element i (dsi /si )2 . Along these curves it is respectively N (d∆/∆)2 and B(d∆/∆)2 , proving
             P

(31.9).

    For the natural formulation the first length is measured in its full source-slack coordinates. It
is also the length of the common projected path for the marginal barrier obtained by minimizing
over the source slack allocations: that marginal is −m j log(tj − γrj ), up to a constant. Equal
                                                         P

multipliers do not give equal projected points; matched gaps are essential. The statement does not
extend here to moving Uj , Vj . Allowing feasibility violations also changes the decoder: negative
deficits must be charged in addition to the objective excess.
    The two full ambient exponential-cone products have optimal barrier parameters 3N and 3B,
even among coupled barriers, by Theorem 19.2. On the fixed slice in Proposition 31.5, the displayed
parameters are N and B. Neither ambient number is an intrinsic lower bound for every barrier on

                                                   157
the projected epigraph. The small relative-entropy barriers of Fawzi and Saunderson [35] and the
positive-map constructions of He et al. [50] are the relevant prior barrier results. Our identity is
their elementary scalar setting, not a new relative-entropy barrier theorem. The conclusions here
concern acquiring the effective coefficients, decoding readable outputs, and comparing the specified
metrics at equal gap.
    For scalar variables, bounded incidence and bounded KKT treewidth can be retained before
compilation. Arrange each block as a binary tree; carry copies of Uj , Vj down its edges and sum
the epigraph variables up the same tree. Each node has only a constant number of variables and
equations, and each parent–child separator has constant size. These separators give an explicit
bounded-width tree decomposition. For matrix entropy variables the separator size grows with the
matrix dimension; no dimension-free scalarized treewidth claim follows.

31.6    What the oracle advantage includes
Consensus reconstruction is public: a projected vector supplies every copied coordinate. For a
nonzero amplitude-encoded vector, equal copying uses the public map |z⟩ 7→ |uN ⟩|z⟩. An explicit
array of all N d copied entries still costs Ω(N d) output operations. In the entropy example an
                                                                            √
implicit online compiler could instead search one requested block in O( m) queries and retain
access to the input; that output does not incur all-block acquisition until all blocks are requested.
    There is also no free explicit-input loading theorem. With bounded fan-in g > 1, a readable OR
output sensitive to m source bits has a backward dependency cone containing those bits, and therefore
requires Ω(m) input incidences and depth Ω(logg m). Applying this to the independent promised
OR chains gives total Ω(N ) incidences and depth Ω(logg (N/k)). Parallel classical reductions match
these orders. The query improvements above consequently assume a supplied coherent oracle or an
already constructed access structure.
    Redundancy removal before an interior-point solve is classical presolve [42]; quantum search on
partially ordered sets is also established [63]. Our chain model gives unordered access to inclusion
keys, whereas the poset-search models in the latter reference have their own comparison or ordering
promises. Minimum finding, amplitude amplification, and adversary direct sums are established
methods. The explicit families above combine them with essential-constraint witnesses, sparse
conic lifts, readable-output reductions, and exact metric comparisons. The results distinguish
that conjunction from a general quantum optimization speedup, an all-barriers lower bound, or a
universal claim that fewer retained inequalities always give the best lift.


32     Closing synthesis
Conic reformulation has several distinct costs. Local curvature constrains cone resources; the
full certificate fiber determines the least exposing rank; global regularity can impose additional
topological costs. Affine restriction can lower a standard barrier parameter, while changing the
barrier on the same domain is another optimization problem. The exact formulas proved here
identify these costs in their stated models. The bounded interval (8.6) and the nonsymmetric
parameter intervals delimit their reach. The general integer ledgers give necessary resource bounds;
their integer minima need not be attainable lift optima.
    The weighted tree coefficient makes the metric distinction concrete. Active support ranks explain
part of the distance, but objective-free linked subtrees contribute half their barrier weight to its
squared leading coefficient. A central route pays their full weight. The PSD quotient theorem gives
a different concrete lesson: retaining exact source allocations improves a spectral transfer from


                                                158
squared to first powers, while changing the auxiliary point can still change the Newton right-hand
side and the required data.
    These geometric conclusions become computational conclusions only through a specified interface.
A short description, an inexpensive normalized state, a readable solution, and a reusable exact
compiler are different outputs. Fresh scale updates have a temporal query cost under the stated
commitment contract; fixed data can instead be cached. Thus the paper’s work bounds use explicit
access and charging assumptions, and its metric bounds retain their objective, endpoint, and barrier.
Keeping these conditions visible is what makes the formulation comparisons reusable.




                                                159
Appendices
A     Further topological restrictions
These refinements retain information about abstract tangent splittings and individual saturated
channels. Joint saturation is governed by the stronger covering theorem in Section 10.

A.1    What the abstract tangent splitting alone implies
For C 2 factors with P = D = M and ζ the identity, suppose the full mixed form H is positive definite.
Each individual form Hi = −DA∗i DBi is positive semidefinite: the Hessian of the nonnegative
function ⟨Ai (x), Bi (z)⟩ at (x, x) is positive semidefinite and annihilates the tangent diagonal. Its
diagonal blocks are therefore Hi , and its off-diagonal blocks are −Hi . If i rank Hi = n everywhere,
                                                                           P

lower semicontinuity of each rank and constancy of their sum make the individual ranks locally
constant. Whitening by H gives positive semidefinite matrices of total rank n summing to the
identity. Factoring them as Zi Zi∗ and concatenating the n columns shows that each whitened matrix
is an orthogonal projection. Thus

                                TM =                  Ei = im(H −1 Hi ).                         (A.1)
                                       M
                                             Ei ,
                                         i

For exact globally smooth cone factors at capacity equality, Theorem 10.1 is stronger than the
following necessary conditions. They remain useful when only the bundle splitting is retained.

Proposition A.1 (Ranks in a sphere tangent splitting). Suppose T Sn = i Ei , with positive ranks
                                                                            L

ri , and put s = ρ(n + 1) − 1, where ρ((2a + 1)24b+c ) = 8b + 2c for 0 ≤ c ≤ 3. Every subset sum
rI = i∈I ri satisfies
      P

                                  0 < rI ≤ n/2 =⇒ rI ≤ s.
If s < n/3, there is a unique rank greater than n/2, and it is at least n − s. If all ranks are at most
c ≤ n/2, the splitting is impossible when s = 0; otherwise it needs at least ⌈n/ min{c, s}⌉ summands.
For n ≥ 3, if a summands have rank one and b have rank two, then a + 2b ≤ s.

Proof. For completeness, the plane-field implication can be seen from clutching. At the endpoint
2r = n, a splitting would make e(T Sn ) = e(E)e(F ) = 0, contradicting its Euler number two. For
0 < 2r < n, both summands in a decomposition T Sn = E r ⊕ F n−r are orientable. Their clutching
maps take values in SO(r) and SO(n − r). If r ≤ n − r, the two inclusions in SO(n) can be
conjugated into the same copy of SO(n − r); the conjugations are homotopic to the identity in
SO(n). The sum of the resulting homotopy classes lies in the image of πn−1 SO(n − r). Hence T Sn
admits a reduction to SO(n − r), which supplies r independent tangent vector fields. This is the
classical plane-field argument of Steenrod [83]. Adams’s theorem bounds their number by s [1].
Apply it to i∈I Ei . For n = 1 the subset assertion is empty.
             L

    If every rank were at most n/2, take the first partial sum exceeding n/2. The preceding sum
and the last added rank are each at most s. The complementary sum, when nonzero, is also at
most s. This gives n ≤ 3s (and n ≤ 2s for empty complement), a contradiction. The complement of
the unique large rank then has rank at most s. The capped-count assertion follows by applying
the subset bound to each summand. For n ≥ 3, rank-one bundles are trivial and oriented rank-two
bundles are classified by their Euler class in H 2 (Sn ; Z) = 0. These summands jointly supply a + 2b
fields.


                                                    160
    The largest-rank bound is sharp for abstract splittings: a maximal s-frame and its orthogonal
complement have ranks s and n−s. The inequality s < n/3 holds for n+1 ≥ 3 except n+1 = 4, 8, 16,
as follows directly from the displayed formula for ρ. For even n, a proper splitting is impossible
already from e(E ⊕ F ) = e(E)e(F ): both intermediate-degree Euler classes vanish, whereas
⟨e(T Sn ), [Sn ]⟩ = 2. For higher ranks the precise classical test is the unstable clutching condition

                 blockdiag∗ (α1 , . . . , αk ) = τn     in πn−1 SO(n),      αi ∈ πn−1 SO(ri ),

where τn classifies T Sn . Stable triviality of the tangent bundle alone does not imply this condition.

A.2    Restrictions on one everywhere saturated factor
The normalization argument in the proof of Theorem 10.1 needs only that the chosen channel have
positive capacity ci and rank ci everywhere. It then gives an individual submersion P → Sci , even
when the total capacity is larger than dim P . The next classical sphere consequence and its product
extension concern this weaker hypothesis.
Proposition A.2 (Sphere and equal-sphere-product submersions). A C 1 submersion Sn → Sr with
0 < r < n is possible only for (n, r) = (3, 2), (7, 4), (15, 8), and each pair occurs. More generally, for
q ≥ 2 and 1 ≤ r < kq, a C 1 submersion (Sq )k → Sr requires

                                       r=q   or       q = 2r − 1 with r even.                          (A.2)

Projection realizes the first possibility when k ≥ 2. Projection followed by a Hopf map realizes the
three corresponding possibilities in the second case. No sufficiency assertion is made for the other
dimension pairs in (A.2).
Proof. A C 1 submersion from a compact source has a uniformly positive surjectivity modulus.
Smooth approximation preserves submersivity, so Ehresmann’s theorem gives a smooth fiber bundle
for purposes of an existence obstruction [29]. A target circle is excluded by lifting to R and taking a
maximum. For a sphere total space and r ≥ 2, the fiber is connected. Browder’s theorem gives its
homotopy type as Sj , j ∈ {1, 3, 7} [19, Theorem 1]. The two-row mod-two Serre spectral sequence
forces r = j + 1: its only possible nonzero differential killing the fiber class has length j + 1, and
must land in the sole positive base degree r. The top surviving class gives n = 2j + 1. The Hopf
fibrations attain these pairs.
    Now write E = (Sq )k . If r ≥ 3, the closed fiber F is simply connected by the homotopy exact
sequence. It is orientable because the base and total space are orientable. Both its rational homotopy
and its cohomology are finite-dimensional, so it is elliptic. The classical formal-dimension identity
[38, Theorem 32.6] is

                                                                 j odd,
                                                 (
                                                     j,
         dim F =         wj dj (F ),     wj =                              dj (X) = dimQ πj (X) ⊗ Q.
                   X

                   j≥2                               −(j − 1),   j even,

Let aj denote the rational rank of πj (E) → πj (Sr ). Exactness gives dj (F ) = dj (E) + dj+1 (Sr ) −
aj − aj+1 . Since wj + wj−1 is two for odd j and zero for even j, substitution gives

                                                 kq − (r − 2) − 2ar ,  r odd,
                                             (

                                                 kq + 2 − r − 2a2r−1 , r even.

Comparison with dim F = kq − r forces the displayed rank to equal one. An odd sphere has rational
homotopy only in its own degree; an even q-sphere has it in degrees q and 2q − 1. Thus odd r forces

                                                         161
r = q if q is odd. The other candidate, r = 2q −1 for even q, contradicts 2k = χ(E) = χ(Sr )χ(F ) = 0.
Even r gives r = q for even q, and q = 2r − 1 for odd q.
   It remains to treat r = 2, where the fiber need not be simply connected. If q ≥ 4, a minimal
Sullivan model of E has no elements in degrees two or three. Hence a model of f sends both
generators of (Λ(x2 , y3 ), dy3 = x22 ) to zero. The homotopy-fiber model is then

                               ME ⊗ Λ(u1 ) ⊗ Q[v2 ],         Du1 = Dv2 = 0,

by the standard fiber-model construction [38, Section 15]. One can obtain it explicitly by adjoining
u1 , v2 to the sphere model with Du1 = x2 and Dv2 = y3 − u1 x2 , then pulling back along the zero
model map. Its cohomology is infinite-dimensional, contradicting the compact fiber. Consequently
q = 2 or 3, exactly the alternatives in (A.2) for r = 2.

    In particular, one cannot apply the sphere-total-space theorem to a product source to discard,
for example, (q, r) = (11, 6). The rational restriction above does not decide that case. At joint
capacity equality, however, Theorem 10.1 already rules out every smaller target, including genuine
Hopf targets.

A.3    Balanced real projector orbits
The preceding sphere-fiber argument also restricts individual support maps into real Grassmannians.
It is useful when a single symmetric-cone block is saturated but the whole collection is not.

Proposition A.3 (No sphere submersion onto a large balanced real orbit). Let Br = Gr⌊r/2⌋ (Rr ).
For r ≥ 4 no sphere admits a C 1 submersion onto Br or any of its connected finite covers. For
r = 3, the possible source dimensions are exactly two and three. For r = 2, they are exactly one; the
maps are circle coverings. The r = 1 target is a point.

Proof. For r ≥ 3, a submersion from Sn must have n ≥ 2 and lifts to the simply connected oriented

   The topology used here—plane fields, sphere fiberings, rational formal dimension, and Grassman-
nian cohomology—is classical. The contribution of this section is the passage from saturated cone
contact channels to normalized-base submersions and their joint covering, together with the resulting
formulation consequences. These statements concern one specified globally C 1 slack factorization.
They have different quantifiers from the factorization of all positive linear maps through sums of
Lorentz cones studied by Aubrun, La Piana, and Müller-Hermes [6].


B     Restricted kernels: embeddings, locality, and flat residuals
The full labelled rows in an affine lift carry more information than a single weighted contact kernel.
This section quantifies that distinction. It also gives a nullity obstruction that remains useful when
the mixed contact curvature is zero. All factorization classes used below are stated explicitly; a
factorization of a restricted kernel need not extend to the full slack operator of a convex body.

B.1    Projective contact forces an embedding
Write Px = xxT for x ∈ Sr−1 and let RPr−1 denote the space of such rank-one projectors. Its
strict-diagonal contact kernel is
                               κ([x], [y]) = 1 − tr(Px Py ) = 1 − (xT y)2 .
A nowhere-zero factorization means that every selected primal and dual factor is nonzero at every
point; zero factors cannot be discarded only on an open dense subset for this definition.
Theorem B.1 (Projective phase embedding). Let r ≥ 3, k ≥ 1, and λa > 0. Suppose that on

         Ai (u) = ai (u)(1, pi (u)),       Bi (u) = bi (u)(1, −pi (u)),                      ai , bi > 0,   pi (u) ∈ S1 .

     The bundle obstruction itself is classical. The factorization-specific step is the passage from
positive complementary Lorentz phases to an injective Euclidean phase map. This statement
concerns only the displayed rank-one kernel and its nowhere-zero selections. In particular, its finite
upper bound is consistent with Fawzi’s theorem that the full cone S3+ has no finite second-order-cone
lift [32].

   In the paired construction each stereographic pair image is noncospherical. Indeed, the inverse

Global stereographic projection of X(M ) from p therefore gives D − 1 smooth nowhere-zero channels.
To verify dependence on all sources, choose a unit v ∈ p⊥ such that each va ≠ 0 and va ⊥ pa . This
is possible because each block has dimension at least three. One coordinate is f = v T X/c, where
c = 1 − pT X. The time component of its primal factor is T = c(1 + f 2 )/2. Fix a source a. On
an open subset of its sphere there are tangent variations with d(v T X) = 0 and dc ̸= 0, because va
and pa are independent. At points with f ̸= ±1 these give dT = (1 − f 2 )dc/2 ̸= 0. Such points
exist: the equation v T X = ±c cannot hold on an open subset of the product, since its two sides
would give a nontrivial affine relation among the constant and coordinate functions. This argument
applies to every a. Thus one channel can depend nontrivially on all k sources. Positivity does not
justify assuming source locality or pair locality in an unrestricted proof.

B.3    Column ranks detect curvature-flat residuals
The next statement uses finite-dimensional linear algebra rather than contact differentiation. For a
kernel Fa : Xa × Xa → R, its column rank is the dimension of the span of functions Fa ( · , v) on Xa .
No topology is needed in the first theorem.

has dimension at least its cardinality plus one; select a nonzero vector in the last subspace and
quotient by its span, preserving the inductive inequalities for the others. Finally, multilinearity of
the exterior product expands independent choices from the Va into at least one independent choice
from the generating sets Sa . This proves the claimed criterion without a finiteness assumption on
those sets.
    Put Vi = spana∈J,v Ran Bia (v) and mi = dim Vi . The range of a block-diagonal matrix contains
each block-supported range vector. Consequently the deficient span is exactly i Vi , not merely
                                                                                   L

a subspace of it, and i mi ≤ j − 1. All the dual matrices for a ∈ J lie in i Sym(Vi ), whose
                        P                                                        L

                           X mi (mi + 1)       R+1X           R+1

Pairing these matrices with A(x) bounds by the same number the span of all cylindrical column
functions Fa (xa , v), a ∈ J. On the other hand, a relation a∈J fa (xa ) = 0 makes each fa constant,
                                                              P

by varying one coordinate at a time. Its relation space has dimension at most j − 1, so the cylindrical

                               P

the difference of twice the two sides is R + 3 + j(2ρ − R − 3), an affine function of 1 ≤ j ≤ b. It is
positive at j = 1, and at j = b positivity is exactly (B.10). For b = 1 this condition is automatic.

Corollary B.7 (Antipodal separation and squared slack). Let s ≥ 3, Xa = Ss−1 , and assume the
kernels in (B.8) are continuous. If each Fa (u, u) = 0 and Fa (u, τa (u)) > 0, where τa is topologically
conjugate to the antipodal map, then the nullity conclusion holds whenever

                                        (R + 3)(b − 1) < 2bs.                                    (B.11)

For the squared kernels Fa (u, v) = (1 − uT v)2 /2, their exact column rank is ρ = s(s + 3)/2. Thus
the stronger sufficient condition is

                                     (R + 3)(b − 1) < bs(s + 3).                                 (B.12)

Irrespective of either cap condition, every factorization of these squared kernels satisfies
                                X ri (ri + 1)        s(s + 3)
                                                                    
                                                ≥1+b          −1 .                               (B.13)
                                 i
                                        2                2

Proof. Choose a basis f1 , . . . , fρa of the column space and write Fa (u, v) = ℓ fℓ (u)cℓ (v). The
                                                                                   P

coefficient map c : Ss−1 → Rρa is continuous: select ρa evaluation points for which the basis
evaluation matrix is invertible and recover c from those evaluations. If c(v) = c(τa (v)), evaluation
at u = v contradicts the assumed separation. After conjugating τa to the antipodal map, the
Borsuk–Ulam theorem [48, Section 2.2] implies ρa ≥ s. Theorem B.6 proves (B.11).

                                                  168
    For the squared kernel the odd parts of the columns span all s linear coordinate functions.
The even parts span all quadratic forms restricted to the sphere: their coefficient matrices are
proportional to I + vv T . A matrix C orthogonal to every I + vv T obeys tr C + v T Cv = 0 for every
unit v, which first makes C scalar and then zero. The restriction of homogeneous quadratic forms to
the sphere is injective, and the odd and even spaces are independent. Thus ρ = s + s(s + 1)/2. This
gives (B.12). The constant function belongs to the column span, for example by averaging Fa ( · , v)
over the sphere. Coordinate column spaces are independent modulo this common one-dimensional
space, so the labelled cylindrical column rank is exactly 1 + b(ρ − 1). Pairing with the full matrix
space i Sri proves (B.13).
       L


    These squared kernels have zero mixed contact curvature, so the preceding lower bound cannot
be recovered by applying the contact Hessian estimate to them. If the selected primal tuples also
belong to the closure of a relative-Slater affine slice, Lemma 8.1 converts the nullity conclusion
into νstd,slice ≥ b for its standard barrier. This extra affine-slice hypothesis is needed for the barrier
conclusion.
    For example, the identity

                                              1 − (uT v)2 (1 − uT v)2
                                 1 − uT v =              +
                                                   2           2
splits ball slack into a projective term and a flat residual. The first term has the order-s factorization
tr((I − uuT )vv T /2). A construction using one such separate block for each source spends b primal
nullities at every contact; if the residual uses order at most s, Corollary B.7 forces at least another
b at some simultaneous contact. Moreover, with ds = s(s + 1)/2, (B.13) gives

                                                        b(s − 1) + 1
                                                                  
                                  Lresidual ≥ b +                    > b.
                                                             ds
Thus this prescribed positive splitting needs at least 2b + 1 factors, whereas the grouped Schur
construction for the unsplit full slacks uses 2b. The unrestricted full-slack rank theorem does not
assume such a splitting; the example explains why a curvature-saturating projective summand need
not be an economical component of a complete formulation.
    Ordinary-rank lower bounds for PSD factorizations are standard [36]. The additional step
in Theorem B.6 is to combine the independent-transversal criterion with simultaneous contact
and the blockwise matrix-space bound. Stating the criterion in terms of the actual column ranks
preserves information that the weaker antipodal estimate ρa ≥ s discards. The conclusions concern
a simultaneous primal nullity and a prescribed labelled kernel; they do not assert a lower bound on
every contact or on an arbitrary barrier without further hypotheses.


C     Global obstructions for conditioned approximate factors
The local rank estimates of Section 20 have global consequences when the factors have fixed labels
and vary smoothly over the entire contact sphere. These hypotheses are not supplied by an arbitrary
approximate lift. We first retain tangent-bundle information without requiring the selected factors
to lie on cone boundaries. We then obtain stronger channelwise conclusions for nonzero boundary
selections.

All ranks attain their bounds, and their images are in direct sum. Continuity and constant rank

already trivial. A complete rank-one splitting forces the familiar parallelizable dimensions 1, 3, 7.
For even n, a splitting into two positive intermediate ranks would give e(T Sn ) = e(E)e(F ) = 0,
since intermediate integral cohomology vanishes. This contradicts Euler number two.

    The stronger subset-rank restrictions in Proposition A.1 also apply. The canonical maps in this
proof are essential: unrelated pointwise low-rank approximants do not automatically define vector
bundles. The hypotheses exclude rays with nonzero selected factors, since for a ray δi = ∥ai ∥∥bi ∥ ≥ µ2 .
Allowing a factor to vanish or losing uniform control is a permitted failure of the hypotheses.

If a base factor vanishes anywhere, the displayed value for that gauge is defined as +∞.

Corollary C.2 (Gauge-invariant degeneration). If T ∗ Sn admits no splitting with ranks (mi − 2)+ ,
then D ≥ 1.

Proof. If D < 1, some fixed gauge has both suprema below one. Its γi < 1 excludes rays and
makes the primal and dual factors nonparallel everywhere. The canonical maps of the preceding
proof then have rank at most mi − 2 and an invertible sum, supplying the forbidden splitting. If
the original coordinates are changed by Ti , all subsequent gauges Si range through the same net
transformations Si Ti as before. Thus the infimum is invariant under every fixed blockwise invertible
reparameterization. No covariance of the individual Euclidean projectors is needed.

   This index does not assert a lower bound on a barrier Hessian or a Newton-system condition
number. The infimum ranges over fixed linear block gauges, not parameter-dependent gauges or
changes of the contact sphere itself.

C.3    Rank-one phase dropout
The next statement is stronger than the frame consequence in source sphere dimensions three and
seven, but requires nonzero boundary maps. Let M be a closed, connected, simply connected
smooth Riemannian manifold. Let A, B : M → ∂Q3 \ {0} be C 1 and write

                   A = a(1, p),            B = b(1, −q),             a, b > 0,        p, q : M → S1 .

Set σ = ⟨A, B⟩, Cx (u, v) = −⟨dAx u, dBx v⟩ and

               KA = sup∥d log a∥,              KB = sup∥d log b∥,                K = min{KA , KB }.
                       M                                  M

Bilinear forms need not be symmetric; all norms are operator norms.




                                                          171
Theorem C.3 (Quantitative phase dropout). If 0 ≤ σ ≤ ϵ and ∥dσ∥ ≤ η, then there is x∗ ∈ M
with
                                  ∥Cx∗ ∥ ≤ Kη + K 2 ϵ.
If every Cx is within δ of a positive rank-one form whose nonzero eigenvalue is at least µ > 0, then
                                          δ + Kη + K 2 ϵ ≥ µ.                                    (C.3)
Proof. Simple connectivity lifts p to a real C 1 phase. At a maximum of that phase, dp = 0, so
dA = α ⊗ A, where α = d log a. At this point
                           ⟨A, dB⟩ = dσ − σα,         C = −α ⊗ (dσ − σα).
Its norm is at most KA η + KA 2 ϵ. A critical point of the dual phase gives C = −(dσ − σβ) ⊗ β, with

β = d log b, and the bound using KB . Taking the smaller bound proves the first assertion. At the
corresponding point, any rank-one approximant of norm at least µ has distance at least µ − ∥C∥
from C, proving (C.3).
                                                                                √
    The hypotheses can be checked using norms of the original factors: ∥A∥ = 2a and ∥dA(u)∥2 =
2|da(u)|2 + a2 ∥dp(u)∥2 , whence KA ≤ LA /mA if ∥A∥ ≥ mA and ∥dA∥ ≤ LA . The dual bound is
identical. Reciprocal constant scalar gauges leave KA , KB , σ, and C unchanged.

                        a = ℓ(A),   p = A/a : Sn → J,            KA = sup∥d log a∥,
                       gB (v) = ⟨A, dB(v)⟩,        ηB = sup∥gB ∥,      C = −dA∗ dB.

Write sr (C) for the rth largest singular value.

Theorem C.5 (Robust submersion obstruction). If (n, r) ∈
                                                       / {(3, 2), (7, 4), (15, 8)}, there is a point
with
                                    sr (C) ≤ KA ηB .
Consequently, if every C is within δ of a rank-r form whose rth singular value is at least µ > 0, then

                                           δ + KA ηB ≥ µ.                                        (C.5)

In a Hopf dimension pair, the same conclusion holds if the particular map p has a critical point.
If a dual normalized base also has C 1 boundary, the term KA ηB in (C.5) may be replaced by
min{KA ηB , KB ηA }, with dual definitions.

Proof. Radial projection from a relative interior point of the convex base identifies J C 1 diffeomor-
phically with Sr . The inverse is C 1 because the supporting normal at each boundary point has
strictly positive inner product with its radial vector. Outside the three Hopf pairs, Proposition A.2
rules out an everywhere submersive map p. Hence rank dp ≤ r − 1 somewhere. Writing α = d log a,
differentiation of A = ap gives

                             C(u, v) = −a⟨dp(u), dB(v)⟩ − α(u)gB (v).

At that point the first term has rank at most r − 1 and the second has norm at most KA ηB .
The singular-value min–max inequality gives sr (C) ≤ KA ηB . Singular values change by at most
the operator perturbation norm, proving (C.5). Applying the same argument to the normalized
dual map supplies the second bound, possibly at a different point; both inequalities hold, so their
minimum is valid.

    Here gB (x) is the one-sided derivative dy ⟨A(x), B(y)⟩|y=x , with x held fixed. It can be bounded
from small contact defect and a modulus in that variable. Specifically, if F (x, y) = ⟨A(x), B(y)⟩ ≥ 0,
F (x, x) ≤ ϵ, and the second-variable derivative has uniform geodesic modulus ωB , then

HA , HB give the corresponding ψ bounds, and, when their optimizing radii fit,

                               δ + min{KA 2HB ϵ, KB 2HA ϵ} ≥ µ.

    A total diagonal derivative bound is insufficient for this higher-rank argument. With σ = ⟨A, B⟩,

    Finally, consider a saturated profile i ri = n with at least two positive ranks, where every block
                                            P

satisfies these boundary and base-regularity hypotheses and its channel is uniformly δi close to rank
ri with singular-value floor µi . At least one pair (n, ri ) is forbidden: outside source dimensions
3, 7, 15 all proper pairs are forbidden; in those three dimensions the only permitted proper targets
are 2, 4, 8, respectively, and copies of that target dimension cannot sum to n. Thus, under common
bounds δi ≤ δ, KA,i ≤ K, ηB,i ≤ η∂ , and µi ≥ µ, every such profile satisfies

                                                δ + Kη∂ ≥ µ.

A single full-capacity block is outside this obstruction.
    These conclusions combine elementary perturbation estimates with the classical sphere topol-
ogy already cited in Appendix A. The added statements concern quantitative degeneration of
specified global cone-contact selections. They do not follow from the finite slack-matrix approxima-
tion framework of [44], and do not obstruct arbitrary nonsmooth, unlabelled, or vanishing-factor
approximations.


D     Contact sensitivity and Newton conditioning
The approximation obstructions concern derivatives of selected contact maps across labels. A Newton
condition number concerns a linear system at one interior point. The following two separations
identify why an additional sensitivity theorem is needed to connect them.

D.1    A sparse change of cone coordinates
Suppose F (Gz) = F (z) + cG for a linear cone automorphism G. In an equality formulation Ez = b,
put z ′ = Gz, E ′ = EG−1 and H = ∇2 F (z). Differentiation gives

                         H ′ = G−T HG−1 ,             E ′ (H ′ )−1 (E ′ )T = EH −1 E T .          (D.1)

The raw augmented system instead changes by congruence:
                                        !                   !          !             !
                          H ′ (E ′ )T           G−T     0       H ET        G−1 0
                                            =                                     .
                          E′    0                0      I       E 0          0  I

Thus the Schur matrix is literally unchanged, whereas the Euclidean spectrum of the augmented
matrix need not be.
   For Qm , m ≥ 3, let a = e0 + e1 , b = e0 − e1 , and let Gλ be the identity on transverse coordinates,
with first block
                                1 λ + λ−1 λ − λ−1
                                                         !
                                                           ,    λ > 0.
                                2 λ − λ−1 λ + λ−1



                                                       174
It preserves the Lorentz quadratic form and its future sheet. Moreover, Gλ a = λa and G−T
                                                                                       λ b = λb.
Consequently both primal and dual Euclidean norms can approach zero while all their pairings
remain unchanged. This is a genuine contact example:
                                q                                 q
                    A(u) = ( 1 + ∥u∥2 , 1, u),           B(v) = ( 1 + ∥v∥2 , −1, −v)

satisfy ⟨A(u), B(v)⟩ ≥ 0, with equality at u = v. At zero their transverse derivatives are isometries
and are unchanged by the boost; the base norms scale by λ. The standard Lorentz barrier is
invariant, so (D.1) applies. At e0 , H = 2I, but at Gλ e0 it is 2G−2
                                                                  λ , with condition number λ
                                                                                               −4 for

0 < λ < 1. This calculation prevents a lower bound involving unnormalized Euclidean factor norms
from being read as a lower bound on the intrinsic Schur condition number. The boost only mixes
two columns of E but can change their magnitudes, data-access normalization, and the cost of a
solver using the raw augmented system.

D.2    Bounded pointwise geometry and unbounded contact derivatives
Transporting the normalization covector avoids the preceding coordinate issue, but leaves a second
obstruction. For maps A, B and a strictly positive covector ℓ, the transformations

                                     (A, B, ℓ) 7→ (GA, G−T B, G−T ℓ)

preserve ⟨A, B⟩, −dA∗ dB, A∗ dB, and d log ℓ(A). Their cross-label derivatives can nevertheless grow
while all pointwise interior geometry stays bounded.
    Use the Lorentz Jordan product (t, z) ◦ (s, w) = (ts + z T w, tw + sz) and fix c± = (1, ±p0 )/2 for
p0 ∈ S 1 . On S n , n ≥ 1, put ak (x) = exp(sin(kx1 )) and

            Ak = ak c+ ,      Bk = a−1
                                    k c− ,        Xk = ak (c+ + τ c− ),    Sk = a−1
                                                                                 k (c− + τ c+ ),   (D.2)

where 0 < τ < 1 is fixed. The boundary maps are complementary, e−1 ≤ ak ≤ e, and
∥d log ℓ(Ak )∥∞ = k for any positive ℓ. Yet Xk ◦ Sk = τ e exactly. Since ∇2 F (az) = a−2 ∇2 F (z), the
primal and dual Hessian norms and inverse norms are uniformly bounded in k. In the common
                                                                   √          √
Jordan frame the Nesterov–Todd scaling point has eigenvalues ak / τ and ak τ ; its spectral ratio is
1/τ . Here centrality uses the displayed Jordan-product convention; a constant trace-inner-product
normalization has no effect on the conclusion.
    For a different example fix 0 < θ < π/2 and put pk (x) = (cos(kx1 ), sin(kx1 )), qk = Rθ pk , and

             Ak = (1, pk ),     Bk = (1, −qk ),       Xk = (1 + τ, pk ),    Sk = (1 + τ, −qk ).    (D.3)

The diagonal defect is the constant 1 − cos θ, but

                d⟨Ak , Bk ⟩ = 0,      A∗k dBk = sin θ d(kx1 ),        ∥A∗k dBk ∥∞ = k sin θ.

All interior pairs are common spatial rotations of one fixed pair, so Hessian spectra and scaling-point
spectra are independent of k. Their relative Jordan central residual is
                                              2(1 + τ ) sin(θ/2)
                                                                  .
                                             2τ + τ 2 + 1 − cos θ
For any prescribed positive central-neighborhood radius, sufficiently small fixed θ > 0 puts the entire
family inside that neighborhood. Alternatively, θ =√k −1/2 gives defect Θ(k −1 ) and central residual
O(k −1/2 ), while the one-sided derivative grows as Θ( k). The mixed channel is cos θ d(kx1 )⊗d(kx1 );
it vanishes at the critical points of x1 , consistently with the phase obstruction.

                                                      175
   These explicit computations prove that pointwise centrality, Hessian conditioning, scaling-point
conditioning, and bounded factor scales do not control either radial or one-sided contact sensitivity.
The pointwise interpretation of self-scaled geometry is classical [71]; the examples distinguish it
from the different, cross-objective quantities used here.
                                                       One   may even impose the identity equality
z = Xk at each label. The assembled saddle matrix I 0 and its inverse are uniformly bounded
                                                        µH I
                                                                                   
whenever µ stays in a fixed positive compact interval, since its inverse is I0 −µH
                                                                                I
                                                                                   . These artificial
instances have changing right-hand sides. They are not a single approximate lift of a fixed body.

D.3    What an additional sensitivity hypothesis would give
In the robust submersion inequality (C.5), suppose the finite-radius estimate reduces to
                                                          q
                              δ + KA 2HB ϵ ≥ µ,               2ϵ/HB ≤ r0 ,
                                      p


where δ < µ, ϵ > 0 and HB > 0. Then HB ≥ (µ − δ)2 /(2KA          2 ϵ). The finite-radius expression

inf 0<s≤r0 (ϵ/s + HB s/2) remains the valid form when HB = 0 or the optimizing radius does not
fit; one cannot divide by HB in those cases. If a separately established, representation-specific
sensitivity theorem provides KA ≤ cK χa and HB ≤ cH χb , with positive constants and exponents,
substitution yields
                                                    1/(a+b/2)
                                             µ−δ
                                         
                                    χ≥        √                .
                                           cK 2cH ϵ
Likewise, if C > 0, K0 > 0, and q > 0, then HB ≤ C2qβ and KA ≤ K0 imply

                                               1      (µ − δ)2
                                          β≥     log2          .
                                               q       2CK02 ϵ

These are conditional algebraic consequences. Neither an ordinary Newton condition number nor a
coefficient bit count is proved to satisfy these extra hypotheses. In particular, differentiating a KKT
solution map requires control of both its inverse Jacobian and the variation of the input data with
the contact label.
